Covariate Shift in High-Dimensional Random Feature Regression
Nilesh Tripuraneni nilesh_tripuraneni@berkeley.edu, Ben Adlam adlam@google.com, Jeffrey Pennington jpennin@google.com

Abstract
A significant obstacle in the development of robust machine learning models is covariate shift , a form of distribution shift that occurs when the input distributions of the training and test sets differ while the conditional label distributions remain the same. Despite the prevalence of covariate shift in real-world applications, a theoretical understanding in the context of modern machine learning has remained lacking. In this work, we examine the exact high-dimensional asymptotics of random feature regression under covariate shift and present a precise characterization of the limiting test error, bias, and variance in this setting. Our results motivate a natural partial order over covariate shifts that provides a sufficient condition for determining when the shift will harm (or even help) test performance. We find that overparameterized models exhibit enhanced robustness to covariate shift, providing one of the first theoretical explanations for this intriguing phenomenon. Additionally, our analysis reveals an exact linear relationship between in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization performance, offering an explanation for this surprising recent empirical observation.

1
Introduction

Theoretical justification for almost all machine learning methods relies upon the equality of the distributions from which the training and test data are drawn.
Nevertheless, in many real-world applications, this equality is violated. Naturally-occurring distribution shift between the training data and the data encountered during deployment is the rule, not the exception
(
Koh et al. 2021
)
. Even
non-adversarial
changes in distributions can reveal the surprising fragility of modern machine learning models
(
Recht et al. 2018
;
Recht et al. 2019
;
Miller et al. 2020
;
Hendrycks et al. 2021
;
D’Amour et al. 2020
;
Ovadia et al. 2019
)
.
Such shifts are distinct from adversarial examples, which require explicit poisoning attacks
(
Goodfellow et al. 2015
)
; rather, they can result from mild corruptions, ranging from changes of camera angle or blur
(
Hendrycks et al. 2021
)
, to subtle, unintended changes in data acquisition procedures
(
Recht et al. 2019
)
.
Moreover, this fragility limits the application of deep learning in certain safety-critical areas
(
Koh et al. 2021
)
. Empirical studies of distribution shift have observed several intriguing phenomena, including linear trends between performance on shifted and unshifted test distributions
(
Recht et al. 2019
;
Hendrycks et al. 2021
;
Koh et al. 2021
)
, dramatic degradation in calibration
(
Ovadia et al. 2019
)
, and surprising spurious inductive biases
(
D’Amour et al. 2020
)
. Theoretical understanding of why such patterns occur across a variety of real-world domains is scant.
Even basic questions such as what makes a certain distribution shift likely to hurt (or help) a model’s performance, and by how much, are not understood. One reason that these phenomena have eluded theoretical understanding is that even the qualitative behavior of different shifts can vary widely. In many cases, there is a strong coupling between the model and distribution shift; for example, in the context of character recognition, spatial translations typically hurt the performance of fully-connected networks but have minimal effect on convolutional models. On the other hand, there do exist certain types of shifts that affect most models in roughly the same way—a shift that reduces the frequency of ambiguous or contradictory examples would be of this type. Simultaneously capturing these model-dependent and model-independent characteristics of distribution shift is a significant obstacle in developing a realistic theoretical model.

Another reason that many phenomena related to distribution shift continue to lack satisfactory theoretical explanations is that the go-to formalism for studying generalization in classical models, namely uniform convergence theory
(
Wainwright 2019
, see e.g.)
, may be insufficient to explain the behavior of modern, deep learning methods even in the absence of distribution shift
(
Nagarajan and Kolter 2019
;
Yang et al. 2021
)
. Indeed, classical measures of model complexity, such as various norms of the parameters, have been found to lead to ambiguous conclusions
(
Neyshabur et al. 2017
)
. To overcome these limitations, it can be fruitful to shift the focus from worst-case bounds for generic distributions to the average-case behavior for narrowly specified distributions. While this change in perspective sacrifices generality, it can produce more precise predictions, which we believe are necessary to fully capture the relevant phenomenology of distribution shift.

In this paper, we study a specific type of distribution shift called covariate shift, in which the distributions of the training and test covariates differ, while the conditional distribution of the labels given the covariates remains fixed. In particular, we focus on the setting in which the covariates are multivariate Gaussian and the targets are generated by a simple linear signal-plus-noise model. This narrow specification of the data distribution enables an asymptotically exact computation of the generalization error of random feature regression under covariate shift, which we perform using tools from random matrix theory. The results naturally lead to a model-independent notion of shift strength and facilitate a nuanced analysis of the model-dependent behavior of covariate shift within the context of random feature regression.

1.1
Summary of contributions

Here we provide a brief overview of the paper and highlight our main results. After discussing related work in
Section

1.2
, we discuss the general random feature regression setup in
Section

2
and build some intuition for our approach in the simpler context of linear regression in
Section

3
. In the remainder of the paper, we:

1.

Compute the test error, bias, and variance of random feature regression for general multivariate Gaussian covariates under covariate shift in the high-dimensional limit (see
Section

5.1
). The results generalize prior work in two important ways: first, they provide one of the first asymptotically exact results for random feature regression with anisotropic covariates, even in the absence of covariate shift; second, they provide one of the first asymptotically exact results for covariate shift, even in the simple special case of linear regression;

2.

Provide a
model-independent
partial order over covariate shifts that can indicate how a given shift will affect the performance of random feature regression (see
Definition

4.1
). When the shift does not alter the covariates’ overall scale, the partial order is sufficient to determine whether the bias and test error will increase or decrease in response to the shift; otherwise, we characterize the
model-dependent
conditions that are necessary to understand the effect of the shift (see
Section

5.2
);

3.

Prove that overparameterization enhances robustness to covariate shift, and that the error, bias, and variance are nonincreasing functions of the number of excess parameters (see
Section

5.3
). Such benefits of overparameterization have been previously observed empirically for practical models and real-world datasets, but our results show that the behavior persists in simpler settings and may have a prosaic explanation stemming from the high-dimensionality of the problem;

4.

Deduce a linear relationship between in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization performance, offering an explanation for this surprising recent empirical observation (see
Section

5.4
). The relationship holds exactly in the ridgeless limit and is given parameterically in the degree of overparameterization, i.e. between models with varying numbers of excess parameters. The linearity approximately persists under non-zero regularization, but it is clearly violated in the underparameterized regime (see
Section

7.4
);

5.

Prove that, in the limit of infinite overparameterization, the optimal regularization constant is independent of the covariate shift, and compute the associated optimal test error (see
Section

6
). Owing to the implicit regularization effect of the nonlinear feature map, the optimal regularization constant can be negative. When the constant is optimized on the shifted test distribution, we observe the total error to be a nonincreasing function of the number of parameters, i.e. the characteristic double descent peak is eliminated; however, when it is tuned on the unshifted training distribution, a peak persists and the resulting error curve remains nonmonotonic;

6.

Examine how shifts in covariate scale can induce unexpected effects, such as nonmonotonicity of the bias with respect to the number of parameters and the amount of ridge regularization (see
Section

7.3
). By investigating this behavior in a special coordinate system, we develop an interpretation in terms of a coordinate-wise rescaling of the model’s expected estimator for the target coefficients. Notably, this nonmonotonicity in the bias is only uncovered when the activation function is nonlinear and when the shift alters the scale of the covariates.

1.2
Related work

There is extensive literature focusing on the empirical analysis of distribution shift in all of its myriad forms, ranging from domain adaptation
(
Sugiyama et al. 2007
;
Glorot et al. 2011
;
Becker et al. 2013
;
Zhao et al. 2018
;
Zhao et al. 2019
;
Long et al. 2017
;
Long et al. 2016
)
to defenses against adversarial attacks
(
Madry et al. 2018
;
Schmidt et al. 2018
)
to distributionally robust optimization
(
Sagawa et al. 2020
;
Duchi et al. 2020
;
Duchi and Namkoong 2021
)
, among many others. Interestingly, for naturally occurring distribution shifts
(
Koh et al. 2021
;
Hendrycks et al. 2021
)
, standard robustness interventions provide little to no protection
(
Recht et al. 2019
;
Taori et al. 2020
)
. Indeed, empirical risk minimization on clean, unshifted training data often performs better on out-of-distribution benchmarks than more sophisticated methods such as invariant risk minimization
(
Koh et al. 2021
)
. One of the most striking observations in the context of natural distribution shifts is that models become more robust as their in-distribution accuracy increases
(
Recht et al. 2019
;
Taori et al. 2020
;
Hendrycks et al. 2021
;
Miller et al. 2020
)
. For example,
Recht et al. 2019
found that if a classifier’s accuracy increases by 1.0% on the unshifted CIFAR-10 test set, this tends to increase its accuracy by 1.7% on the CIFAR-10.1 dataset (a dataset subject to natural distribution shift). Moreover, such linear trends between the unshifted and shifted measures of error have now been observed in a variety of contexts
(
Recht et al. 2019
;
Taori et al. 2020
;
Miller et al. 2020
;
Miller et al. 2021
;
Mania and Sra 2021
)
.

The number of theoretical works studying the impact of distribution shift on generalization is far smaller. The pioneering work of
Ben-David et al. 2007
provides VC-dimension-based error bounds for classification that are augmented by a discrepancy measure between shifted source and target domains, while
Cortes and Mohri 2014
demonstrate a similar class of uniform convergence-based results in the setting of kernel regression.

Zhao et al. 2019
show that learning domain-invariant features is insufficient to guarantee generalization when the class-conditional distributions of features may shift; while
Kumar et al. 2020
provide non-vacuous margin-based bounds for self-training when the source domain gradually shifts toward the target domain.
Lei et al. 2021
compute the minimax risk for linear models under distribution shift given access to labeled data from a source domain and unlabeled data from the target domain.
Mania and Sra 2021
introduce assumptions based on model similarity to help explain why classifiers exhibit linear trends between their accuracies on shifted and unshifted test sets.

Our technical tools build on a series of works that have studied the high-dimensional limit of the test error for a growing class of model families and data distributions. In the context of linear models,
Richards et al. 2021
and
Wu and Xu 2020
analyze ridge regression for general covariances and a general non-isotropic source condition on the parameters that generate the targets, extending earlier work studying minimum-norm interpolated least squares and ridge regression in the random design setting
(
Belkin et al. 2019
;
Dobriban and Wager 2018
;
Hastie et al. 2019
)
. The non-isotropy of source parameter effectively induces a shift on the bias term of this model, but the phenomenon is distinct from the covariate shifts we study here.

Beyond linear regression, random feature models provide a rich but tractable class of models to gain further insight into generalization phenomena
(
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
;
Mei and Montanari 2021
;
Liao et al. 2020
)
. These methods are of particular interest because of their connection to neural networks, with the number of random features corresponding to the network’s width or model complexity
(
Neal 1996
;
Lee et al. 2018
;
Jacot et al. 2018
)
, and because they serve as a practical method for data analysis in their own right
(
Rahimi and Recht 2008
;
Shankar et al. 2020
)
. In this context,
Yang et al. 2021
provide a precise characterization of the gaps between uniform convergence and the exact (asymptotic) test error as a function of the sample size and number of random features.

From the technical perspective, our results rely on the concept of Gaussian equivalents, specifically a linear-signal-plus-noise representation of the random feature matrix, an approach whose origin stems from
Karoui 2010
in the context of kernel random matrices and from
Pennington and Worah 2017
;
Adlam et al. 2019
; and
Péché et al. 2019
for the Gram matrices relevant here. This linearization technique was further developed with applications to high-dimensional learning problems by a number of authors, including
Adlam et al. 2019
;
Mei and Montanari 2021
;
Goldt et al. 2020
;
Chang et al. 2021
, among many others. Our analytic techniques build upon these works and a series of recent results stemming from the literature on random matrix theory and free probability
(
Pennington and Worah 2018
;
Pennington and Worah 2017
;
Adlam et al. 2019
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
;
Louart et al. 2018
;
Péché et al. 2019
;
Far et al. 2006
;
Mingo and Speicher 2017
)
.

An abbreviated version of this article focusing on the restricted setting in which the covariate shift preserves the overall scale may be found in
Tripuraneni et al. 2021
.

2
Preliminaries

Here we introduce our problem setup, some important assumptions we make throughout the paper, and a simple example to illustrate our setup and assumptions.

2.1
Problem setup and notation

As in prior work studying random feature regression
(
Hastie et al. 2019
;
Mei and Montanari 2021
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
)
, we compute the test error in the high-dimensional, proportional asymptotics where the dataset size
m
, input feature dimension
n_{0}
, and hidden layer size
n_{1}
all tend to infinity at the same rate, with the constants
\phi
and
\psi
defined as the limits of the ratios
n_{0}/m
and
n_{0}/n_{1}
respectively. We may think of
n_{1}
and
m
as functions of
n_{0}
, to write

\phi\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lim_{n_{0}\to\infty}\frac{n_{0}}{m(n_{0})}\quad\text{and}\quad\psi\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lim_{n_{0}\to\infty}\frac{n_{0}}{n_{1}(n_{0})}.

(1)

We further define the
overparameterization ratio
as
\phi/\psi
, which is the limit of
{n_{1}}/{m}
and characterizes the normalized complexity of the random feature model.

Interestingly, in this high-dimensional limit only linear functions of the data are learnable. A wide class of nonlinear teacher functions give rise to the same generalization behavior as linear labeling functions asymptotically
(
Mei and Montanari 2021
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
, for more details, see)
. With this in mind, we consider the task of learning an unknown function from
m
i.i.d. samples
(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}}\times\mathbb{R}
for
i\in\{{1,\ldots,m}\}
, where the covariates are Gaussian,
\mathbf{x}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
with positive definite covariance matrix
\Sigma
, and the labels are generated by a linear function parameterized by
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}}
, drawn from
\mathcal{N}\left({0,I_{n_{0}}}\right)
. In particular,

y(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon_{i},

(2)

where
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})
is additive label noise on the training points. We note that assuming the target vector is random and isotropic is common in the high-dimensional regression literature (see e.g.
Dobriban and Wager 2018
) as it decouples the geometry of
\Sigma
and
\beta
, which would otherwise introduce additional complications (though see
Hastie et al. 2019
;
Wu and Xu 2020
; and
Mel and Ganguli 2021
for an analysis of these complications in the context of linear regression without distribution shift).

We study the class of prediction models defined by kernel ridge regression using unstructured random feature maps
(
Rahimi and Recht 2008
)
. The random features are given by a single-layer neural network with independent random weights. Given training data
X=[\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m}]
and a prospective test point
\mathbf{x}
, the random features embeddings of the training and test data are given by

F\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma(WX/\sqrt{n_{0}})\,\quad\text{and}\quad f\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})\,,

(3)

for a random weight matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{1}\times n_{0}}
with i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries and an activation function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
applied elementwise. The induced kernel is

K(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sigma(W\mathbf{x}_{1}/\sqrt{n_{0}})^{\top}\sigma(W\mathbf{x}_{2}/\sqrt{n_{0}})\,,

(4)

and the model’s predictions are given by
\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})=YK^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}}
, where
Y\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=[y(\mathbf{x}_{1}),\ldots,y(\mathbf{x}_{m})]
,
K\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=K(X,X)+\gamma I_{m}
,
K_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=K(X,\mathbf{x})
, and
\gamma
is a ridge regularization constant.
1
1

1

We overload the definition of
K
to include the additive regularization whenever no arguments are present. Also, if
\gamma=0
and
K
is not full-rank,
K^{-1}
should be understood as the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse.
Owing to the implicit regularization effect of the nonlinear feature maps
(
Bartlett et al. 2021
)
, in low noise settings the optimal value of
\gamma
can sometimes be negative
(
Kobak et al. 2020
)
, as we discuss in
Section

6
. Otherwise, for simplicity, throughout the rest of the paper we make the standard assumption that
\gamma\geq 0
.

Our central object of study is the expected test error for a datapoint
\mathbf{x}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma^{*})
where
\Sigma^{*}
may be different from the training covariance
\Sigma
. The expected test error (without label noise on the test point) is

\displaystyle E_{\Sigma^{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}}-YK^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}})^{2}]

(5)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}[(\mathbb{E}[\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})]-y(\mathbf{x}))^{2}]}_{B_{\Sigma^{*}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}[\mathbb{V}[\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})]]}_{V_{\Sigma^{*}}}\,,

(6)

where the inner expectations defining the bias and variance are computed over
W
,
X
, and all
\varepsilon_{i}
.
2
2

2

In fact, the outer expectation over
\beta
can be removed, since the expectation concentrates around its mean.

Note that this definition of the bias and variance is somewhat nonstandard in the statistics literature, where typically only the randomness from the label noise is considered. While this is appropriate for analyses of linear regression that condition on the design matrix, in our setting there is additional randomness in the model coming from
W
, and so it would be inappropriate to compute the bias conditional on
W
as well. Instead, we treat all random variables (
X
,
W
, and
\varepsilon
) equivalently, which leads to unambiguous interpretations of the bias-variance decomposition
(
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
;
Lin and Dobriban 2020
)
. Indeed, failing to incorporate the additional random variables
X
and
W
into the inner expectation of
Eq.

6
has the effect of moving a component of the variance into the bias and can lead to counterintuitive conclusions (see
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
for additional discussion).

For finite values of
n_{0}
, the covariate shift is entirely specified by the training and test covariance matrices
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
, but to characterize the shift asymptotically we have to do so with their limiting spectral properties. Denote the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
in nondecreasing order by
{0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda_{n_{0}}}
and denote a choice for the corresponding eigenvectors by
{\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\mathbf{v}_{n_{0}}}
. Similarly, denote a choice for
\Sigma^{*}
by
{0<\lambda^{*}_{1}\leq\lambda^{*}_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\lambda^{*}_{n_{0}}}
and
{\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}^{*}\leq\ldots\leq\mathbf{v}_{n_{0}}^{*}}
.
3
3

3

Though when
\Sigma
or
\Sigma^{*}
have repeated eigenvalues their eigendecompositions are not unique, the choice of eigendecomposition will not influence later conclusions (see
Appendix

C
).
We define the
overlap coefficients
of
\Sigma^{*}
with respect to
\Sigma
by

\displaystyle r_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{*}\mathbf{v}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}(\mathbf{v}^{*}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{i})^{2}\lambda^{*}_{j}

(7)

to measure the alignment of
\Sigma^{*}
with the
i
th eigendirection of
\Sigma
. In particular,
r_{i}
is the induced norm of
\mathbf{v}_{i}
with respect to
\Sigma^{*}
.

We use
\bar{\mathrm{tr}}
to denote the dimension-normalized trace: for a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
,
\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(A)=\mathrm{tr}(A)/n
. We also use
\lVert A\rVert_{\infty}
and
\lVert A\rVert_{F}
to denote the operator norm and Frobenius norm of a matrix
A
respectively. Finally, we use
\delta_{\mathbf{x}}
to denote the Dirac delta function centered at
\mathbf{x}
.

2.2
Assumptions

Regularity assumptions on the spectra of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
are necessary to state the limiting behavior of this system. As in
Wu and Xu 2020
, it is not sufficient to consider the spectra of these matrices individually; they must be considered jointly. A natural choice is to do this in an eigenbasis of
\Sigma
.

Assumption 1
.

We define the empirical joint spectral distribution (EJSD) as

\mu_{n_{0}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\delta_{(\lambda_{i},r_{i})}

(8)

and assume it converges in distribution to some
\mu
, a distribution on
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
as
n_{0}\to\infty
. We refer to
\mu
as the limiting joint spectral distribution (LJSD), and emphasize that this defines the relevant limiting properties of the training and test distributions.
4
4

4

The EJSD depends not only on
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
but also on a choice of eigendecomposition for
\Sigma
when it has repeated eigenvalues. However, all possible choices for the EJSD form an equivalence class, and the ambiguity does not affect later definitions and conclusions. Again see
Appendix

C
.
Additionally, we require that
\limsup_{n_{0}}\max(\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty},\lVert\Sigma^{*}\rVert_{\infty})\leq C
for a constant
C
.

Often we use
(\lambda,r)
for random variables sampled jointly from
\mu
and denote the marginal of
\lambda
under
\mu
with
\mu_{\text{train}}
: for measurable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}
, define

\mu_{\text{train}}(\mathcal{E})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mu({\mathcal{E}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}).

(9)

Since the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]
is an important object in our study, we assume the following for simplicity.

Assumption 2
.

\mu
is either absolutely continuous or a finite sum of delta masses. Moreover, the expectations of
\lambda
and
r
are finite.

When the eigenspaces of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
are aligned and
r_{i}=\lambda_{i}^{*}=\Phi(\lambda_{i})
for some smooth function
\Phi
, the support of the LJSD degenerates. Here,
1
is essentially equivalent to assuming the empirical spectral distribution of
\Sigma
converges in distribution to some
\mu_{\text{train}}
, which is a standard assumption in the high-dimensional regression literature
(
Dobriban and Wager 2018
;
Mei and Montanari 2021
, e.g.)
; moreover,
Wu and Xu 2020
use an identical specification in the context of linear regression. One special case of note is when there is no shift, i.e.
\Phi
is the identity, in which case the LJSD degenerates to
\mu_{\emptyset}
defined by

\mu_{\emptyset}(\mathcal{E})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mu_{\text{train}}(\{{x:(x,x)\in\mathcal{E}}\})\,,\quad\text{i.e.}\quad(\lambda,\lambda)\sim\mu_{\emptyset}\;\;\text{for}\;\;\lambda\sim\mu_{\text{train}}\,

(10)

for measurable
\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
and any choice of
\mu_{\text{train}}
a distribution over
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

As our analysis takes place in the high-dimensional limit, we further define the limiting scales of the training and test covariances as

s\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lim_{n_{0}\to\infty}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lambda]\quad\text{and}\quad s_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lim_{n_{0}\to\infty}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]

(11)

under the limiting behavior specified in
1
.

Throughout this paper, we also enforce the following standard regularity assumptions on the activation functions to ensure the existence of the moments and derivatives we compute.

Assumption 3
.

The activation function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is assumed to be differentiable almost everywhere. We assume that,
|\sigma(x)|,|\sigma^{\prime}(x)|\leq c_{0}\exp(c_{1}x)

for constants
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
.

2.3
A simple family of diatomic distributions

Because the above assumptions allow for such a general class of covariance structures, it will prove useful for examples and illustrations to consider our results in the context of a simple family of distributions that readily admits straightforward interpretation.

Definition 2.0
.

For
\alpha\geq 1
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define the family of
(\alpha,\theta)
-diatomic LJSDs as

\mu^{\text{diatomic}}_{\alpha,\theta}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\alpha+1}\delta_{(\alpha,C\alpha^{\theta})}+\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}\delta_{(\alpha^{-1},C\alpha^{-\theta})},

(12)

where
C
is a normalization constant chosen so that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu^{\text{diatomic}}_{\alpha,\theta}}[r]=1
.
Note that
\mu^{\text{diatomic}}_{\alpha,\theta}
is the limit of

\Sigma_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{cases}\alpha&\text{if }i=j\text{ and }i\leq\lfloor\frac{n_{0}}{1+\alpha}\rfloor\\
\alpha^{-1}&\text{if }i=j\text{ and }i>\lfloor\frac{n_{0}}{1+\alpha}\rfloor\\
0&\text{if }i\neq j\end{cases}\qquad\text{and}\quad\Sigma^{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{\theta})}\Sigma^{\theta}\,.

(13)

This simple two-parameter family of distributions captures the fast eigenvalue decay observed in many datasets in machine learning, for which the covariance spectra are often dominated by several large eigenvalues and exhibit a long tail of many small eigenvalues
(
Lee et al. 2020
)
. Note that the trivial case of
\alpha=1
yields an identity covariance with no shift. For the nontrivial setting
\alpha>1
, the exponent
\theta
parameterizes the strength of the shift in an intuitive way. When
\theta=1
, there is no shift. When
\theta<1
,
\alpha^{\theta}<\alpha
, so the large eigendirections of the training distribution are suppressed in the test distribution, suggesting that the shift makes learning harder. When
\theta>1
,
\alpha^{\theta}>\alpha
, so the large eigendirections of the training distribution are further emphasized in the test distribution, suggesting that the shift makes learning easier. We will return to the notion of shift strength in
Sections

3
and
4
.

3
Motivating example: linear regression

We first consider the relatively simple case of ridgeless linear regression (LR), which will help build some intuition for the more general analysis of random feature regression in
Section

5.1
. Assuming the labels are generated by the linear model defined above, i.e.
y_{i}=\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon_{i}
, the estimator is given by
\hat{\beta}=(XX^{\top})^{-1}XY
, and the test risk (see
Eq.

5
) has the following simple form.

Proposition 3.0
.

For fixed dimension
n_{0}
and sample size
m\!>\!n_{0}\!+\!1
, the test error of LR is given by

\displaystyle E_{\Sigma^{*}}^{\text{LR}}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}\Sigma^{-1})=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\frac{r_{i}}{\lambda_{i}}.

(14)

Under
1
, as
n_{0},m\to\infty
such that
n_{0}/m\to\phi
,
E_{\Sigma^{*}}^{\text{LR}}\to E_{\mu}^{\text{LR}}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\phi/(1-\phi)\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r/\lambda]
.

One immediate question is whether a given shift will increase or decrease the test error relative to
E^{\text{LR}}_{\Sigma}
. While the precise answer is of course determined by the value of
\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}\Sigma^{-1})
, it useful for the subsequent analysis to develop an understanding of the individual contributions to this term. Similar decompositions of the test error into eigenspaces have proved useful in a variety of other contexts
(
Mel and Ganguli 2021
;
Advani et al. 2020
)
. We begin with a specific example in the setting of the finite-dimensional analog of
Definition

2.1
.

Example 3.0
.

For the finite form of the
(\alpha,\theta)
-diatomic LJSD defined in Eq. (
13
), the test error of LR is given by

\displaystyle E_{\Sigma^{*}}^{\text{LR}}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{\alpha+w(\alpha^{2\theta-1}-\alpha)}{1+w(\alpha^{2\theta}-1)}\,\quad\text{for}\quad w=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\left\lfloor\frac{n_{0}}{1+\alpha}\right\rfloor\,,

(15)

and so
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}E_{\Sigma^{*}}^{\text{LR}}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{2(1-w)w\alpha^{2\theta-1}(1-\alpha^{2})\log(\alpha)}{(1+w(\alpha^{2\theta}-1))^{2}}\leq 0
, which implies
E_{\Sigma^{*}_{1}}^{\text{LR}}\leq E_{\Sigma^{*}_{2}}^{\text{LR}}
whenever
\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2}
, in accordance with the discussion in
Section

2.3
. It follows from
Eq.

13
that the condition
\theta_{1}\geq\theta_{2}
not only implies
\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{1}\Sigma^{-1})\leq\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2}\Sigma^{-1})
, which is necessary for an ordering of the test error, but also that the ratios of overlap coefficients
r_{i,1}/r_{i,2}
form a nondecreasing sequence. It is this condition involving all the eigendirections that will generalize to the nonlinear random feature setting in
Section

4
.

The following proposition captures the essence of these considerations in the context of linear regression.

Proposition 3.0
.

Let
r_{i,1}
and
r_{i,2}
denote the overlap coefficients
5
5

5

Recall the definition of the overlap coefficients in Eq. (
7
).
of
\Sigma^{*}_{1}
and
\Sigma^{*}_{2}
relative to
\Sigma
. If
\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})\geq\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})
and the ratios
{r_{i,1}}/{r_{i,2}}
form a nondecreasing sequence, then in the setting of
Proposition

3.1
,
E_{\Sigma^{*}_{2}}^{\text{LR}}\geq E_{\Sigma^{*}_{1}}^{\text{LR}}
.

Proof.

Using the Harris inequality, we see

E_{\Sigma^{*}_{1}}^{\text{LR}}=\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\frac{r_{i,2}}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{r_{i,1}}{r_{i,2}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\frac{r_{i,2}}{\lambda_{i}}\leq E_{\Sigma^{*}_{2}}^{\text{LR}},

(16)

since
1/\lambda_{i}
and
{r_{i,1}}/{r_{i,2}}
are nonincreasing and nondecreasing in
i
respectively and
r_{i,2}/\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})
is a discrete distribution over
\left\{{1,\ldots,n_{0}}\right\}
.
6
6

6

For more discussion and a generalization of this result and proof technique, see
Lemma

A.3
.

∎

Whereas the
(\alpha,\theta)
-diatomic LJSDs of
Example

3.2
explicitly enforce the trace normalizations that
{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma)=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})=1}
,
Proposition

3.3
provides sufficient conditions for the ordering of test errors for non-unit traces. The fact that
E_{\Sigma^{*}}^{\text{LR}}
scales linearly with the overall scale of
\Sigma^{*}
is a unique feature of linear regression and does not generalize to the nonlinear random feature setting. We return to this issue in
Section

4
.

4
Definition of shift strength

Deriving conditions governing whether a shift will hurt or help a model’s performance is crucial to building an understanding of covariate shift. Motivated in part by the above results for linear regression, and in part by the results for random feature regression that we present in
Section

5.3
, we introduce the following definition of shift strength, which is a direct generalization of the conditions of
Proposition

3.3
.

Definition 4.0
.

Let
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
be LJSDs with the same marginal distribution over
\lambda
. If the asymptotic overlap coefficients are such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]
is nondecreasing as a function of
\lambda
on the support of
\mu_{\text{train}}
and
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]}
, we say
\mu_{1}
is
easier
than
\mu_{2}
(or
\mu_{2}
is
harder
than
\mu_{1}
), and write
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Comparing against the case of no shift,
\mu_{\emptyset}
, we say
\mu_{1}
is
easy
when
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{\emptyset}
and
hard
when
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{\emptyset}
.

A priori, there is little reason to hope that such a
model-independent
definition of shift strength would adequately characterize a shift’s impact on the total error, bias, or variance of a given model. Even for the relatively simple case of random feature regression, the nonlinear feature maps of
Eq.

3
would seem to inextricably couple the covariance distribution to the model. Indeed, in general, it is impossible to completely disentangle the distribution from the model (see
Section

7
).

Nevertheless, as we show in
Section

5.1
, the coupling between model and shift simplifies considerably in the high-dimensional limit. It is characterized by a handful of constants that depend solely on the overall covariance scale
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]
and a collection of functionals of
\mu
that can be controlled. Specifically, when comparing against the case of no shift, i.e.
\mu_{2}=\mu_{\emptyset}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\emptyset}}[r|\lambda]=\lambda
.
Definition

4.1
then places conditions on
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]/\lambda
, which, when combined with simple constraints on the model, bound how the total error and bias will respond to a shift of a given strength. We develop this analysis in
Section

5.3
.

Remarkably, it turns out that nearly all of the model dependence can be eliminated by merely normalizing the scales of the covariate distributions, i.e. enforcing
s=s_{*}
, in which case
Definition

4.1
provides an essentially
model-independent
definition of shift strength that determines how random feature models respond to shifts of different strength. This observation motivates the following assumption:

Assumption 4
.

The training and test covariance scales are equal,
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lambda]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]
, i.e.
s=s_{*}
.

We emphasize that
4
reflects common practice for many models and data modalities, as preprocessing techniques such as standardization are ubiquitous and many architectural components such as layer- or batch-normalization achieve a similar effect
(
Goodfellow et al. 2016
;
Ioffe and Szegedy 2015
;
Nado et al. 2020
)
. While we state all of our main results without this assumption, we will at times invoke
4
to illustrate how the results simplify when
s=s_{*}
.

In the expression for the test error for linear regression,
Eq.

14
, we saw how the effects of the scale in the test distribution decoupled from the more nuanced relative restructuring of the covariates. As discussed above, this decoupling does not happen for random feature regression. Nevertheless, it is useful to keep in mind these four possibilities for the covariate shift: (1)
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]=\lambda
, which occurs when there is no covariate shift; (2)
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]=(s^{*}/s)\lambda
with
s^{*}\neq s
, which occurs when there is a pure-scale shift; (3)
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]\not\propto\lambda
with
s^{*}=s
, which occurs when the covariates are restructured without changing the scale, i.e. in the setting of
4
; and (4)
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]\not\propto\lambda
with
s^{*}\neq s
, which occurs for a generic covariate shift. Throughout the paper, we consider these various categories of shift, with certain results specializing to certain cases.

5
Covariate shift in random feature kernel regression

In this section, we first present our main results characterizing the bias, variance, and total test error of random feature regression. We then use these results to prove that harder shifts (in the sense of
Definition

4.1
) lead to increased bias and total error, that overparameterization improves performance and robustness under covariate shift, and that linear trends exist between the generalization error on unshifted and shifted distributions.

5.1
Main results

Our main result characterizes the high-dimensional limits of the test error, bias, and variance of the nonlinear random feature model of
Section

2
. Before presenting these results, we must first introduce some additional constants that capture the effect of the nonlinearity
\sigma
. These constants depend only on the overall scale of the covariance. For the training covariance, let
z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)
and define

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{V}[\sigma(z)]\,,\;\;\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\tfrac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}[z\sigma(z)])^{2}\,,\;\;\zeta

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=s\rho\,,\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\omega\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=s(\eta/\zeta-1)\,.

(17)

Viewing the constants in
Eq.

17
as function of
s
, i.e.
\Omega_{\sigma}:s\mapsto(\eta,\rho,\zeta,\omega)
, we write

(\eta_{*},\rho_{*},\zeta_{*},\omega_{*})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Omega_{\sigma}(s_{*})

(18)

for the test covariance scale
s_{*}
. Similarly, sometimes it will be convenient to compare two different LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, so we define
(\eta_{i},\rho_{i},\zeta_{i},\omega_{i})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\Omega_{\sigma}(s_{i})
, where
s_{i}
is the test covariance scale for
\mu_{i}
for
i\in\{{1,2}\}
.

We also define
\xi\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{\rho_{*}/\rho}
, which measures the overall scale mismatch between the training and test covariance matrices.

Remark 5.0
.

Many of our results will simplify considerably under
4
, which implies
\eta_{*}=\eta
,
\zeta_{*}=\zeta
,
\rho_{*}=\rho
,
\omega_{*}=\omega
, and
\xi=1
.

Remark 5.0
.

Many of our results will also simplify if the activation function is affine. In this case, if
\sigma(z)=az+b
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\rho=\rho_{*}=a^{2}
,
\zeta=\eta=sa^{2}
, and
\zeta_{*}=\eta_{*}=s_{*}a^{2}
, which imply
\xi=1
and
\omega=\omega_{*}=0
.

Finally, the bias, variance, and total error depend on the covariance spectra through two sets of functionals of
\mu
, which we define as

\mathcal{I}_{a,b}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\phi\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{\lambda^{a}\left({\phi+x\lambda}\right)^{-b}}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\phi\ \mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{r\lambda^{a-1}\left({\phi+x\lambda}\right)^{-b}}\right].

(19)

Theorem 5.3
.

Under
1
,
2
and
3
, as
n_{0},n_{1},m\to\infty
the test error
E_{\Sigma^{*}}
converges to
{E_{\mu}=B_{\mu}+V_{\mu}}
, with the bias
B_{\mu}
and variance
V_{\mu}
given by

\displaystyle B_{\mu}

\displaystyle=(1-\xi)^{2}s_{*}+2(1-\xi)\xi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+\xi^{2}\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}\qquad\text{and}

(20)

\displaystyle V_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\bigg(\mathcal{I}_{1,1}(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\frac{\phi^{2}}{\psi}\gamma\bar{\tau}\mathcal{I}_{1,2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}(\omega_{*}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\Big((\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\frac{\phi}{\psi}\gamma\bar{\tau}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}\Big)\bigg),

(21)

where
x
is the unique nonnegative real root of
x=({1-\gamma\tau})/({\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}})
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{\gamma+\rho\gamma(\tau\psi/\phi+\bar{\tau})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})},

(22)

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{(\psi-\phi)^{2}+4x\psi\phi\gamma/\rho}+\psi-\phi}{2\psi\gamma},

(23)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\bar{\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi-\psi}{\phi}\frac{1}{\gamma}+\frac{\psi}{\phi}\tau\,.

(24)

We note that
\tau
is limiting Stieltjes transform of the random feature kernel matrix
\tfrac{1}{n_{1}}F^{\top}F
and
\bar{\tau}
is the companion transform, i.e. the limiting Stieltjes transform of
\tfrac{1}{n_{1}}FF^{\top}
. Numerical predictions from
Theorem

5.3
can be obtained by first solving the self-consistent equation for
x
by fixed-point iteration,
x\mapsto({1-\gamma\tau})/({\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}})
, and then plugging the result into the remaining terms.
Fig.

1
shows excellent agreement between these asymptotic predictions and finite-size simulations.

Figure 1
:
Predictions of
Theorem

5.3
as a function of the overparameterization ratio (
\phi/\psi=n_{1}/m
) and the shift power (
\theta
) for the
(2,\theta)
-diatomic LJSD (
Eq.

12
) with
{\phi=n_{0}/m=0.5}
,
\sigma=\text{ReLU}
,
\gamma=0.001
, and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0.1
. (
a
) The test error exhibits the characteristic double descent behavior for all shift powers. (
b
) The bias is a nonincreasing function of
\phi/\psi
for all shift powers, as in
Proposition

5.10
. (
c
) The variance is the source of the double-descent peak, and is a nonincreasing function of
\phi/\psi
for all shift powers in the overparameterized regime, as in
Proposition

5.11
. In (
a
,
b
), the total error and bias are nonincreasing functions of
\theta
, as in
Proposition

5.7
. (
d
) 1D horizontal slices of (
a
,
b
,
c
) demonstrate the monotonicity in
\phi/\psi
predicted by
Propositions

5.10
and
5.11
. (
e
) 1D vertical slices of (
a
,
b
,
c
) demonstrate the monotonicity in
\theta
predicted by
Proposition

5.7
(the variance also appears monotonic, but it need not be in general). (
f
) The generalization gap between the error on shifted and unshifted distributions is a nonincreasing function of
\phi/\psi
in the overparameterized regime, as in
Proposition

5.12
. Markers in (
d
,
e
,
f
) show simulations for
n_{0}=512
and agree well with predictions.

At times we will find it convenient to consider various limits of
Theorem

5.3
. By carefully expanding
x
and
\tau
for small
\gamma
, it is straightforward to obtain the following corollary, which characterizes the ridgeless limit of
Theorem

5.3
.

Corollary 5.0
.

In the setting of
Theorem

5.3
, as the ridge regularization constant
\gamma\to 0
,
E_{\mu}=B_{\mu}+V_{\mu}
with
B_{\mu}
given in
Eq.

20
and
V_{\mu}
given by

\displaystyle V_{\mu}=\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{|\phi-\psi|}x(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\begin{cases}\xi^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}&\phi\geq\psi\\
\xi^{2}\frac{x^{2}\psi\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}{\phi-x^{2}\psi\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\big(\omega_{*}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}\big)&\phi<\psi\\
\end{cases}\,,

(25)

where
x
is the unique positive real root of
x={\min(1,\phi/\psi)}/({\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}})
.

As a consistency check, taking
\sigma(x)=x
and
\psi\to 0
in
Corollary

5.4
yields an expression for the test risk of ridgeless linear regression that agrees with the results of
Hastie et al. 2019
and with the asymptotic form of
Proposition

3.1
(see
Section

D.2
).

Similarly, the limit of infinite overparameterization can be obtained by expanding
Theorem

5.3
for small
\psi
(or large
\phi/\psi
with fixed
\phi
):

Corollary 5.0
.

In the setting of
Theorem

5.3
, as the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi\to\infty
,
{E_{\mu}=B_{\mu}+V_{\mu}}
with
B_{\mu}
given in
Eq.

20
and
V_{\mu}
given by

\displaystyle V_{\mu}=\xi^{2}\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}x\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}\,,

(26)

where
x
is the unique positive real root of
x=1/(\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})
, and
\gamma_{\text{eff}}=\gamma/\rho+\omega
.

Remark 5.0
.

Under
4
, the expressions in
Corollary

5.5
are identical to those of linear ridge regression with ridge constant equal to
\gamma_{\text{eff}}
.

Corollary

5.5
highlights how the nonlinearity
\sigma
acts as an implicit regularizer through the constant
\omega
, an effect described previously for isotropic data without covariate shift in
Bartlett et al. 2021
. To the best of our knowledge, for nontrivial covariate shift, even the linear ridge regression limit (
\omega\to 0
) of
Corollary

5.5
has not appeared in previous work. In the absence of covariate shift,
Corollary

5.5
does yield expressions that agree with existing results for ridge regression, as we show in
Section

D.2.3
.

5.2
Harder shifts increase the bias and test error

In
Section

3
, we found that the test error of linear regression increases in response to harder shifts, as defined by
Definition

4.1
. The same is true for random feature regression, provided that the label noise
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
is not too large and that the model-dependent constants in
Eqs.

17
and
18
respect the ordering of scales. As a matter of notation, recall that we use the subscripts “
1
” to denote parameters (i.e.
\eta_{1},\zeta_{1},\xi_{1})
associated with
\mu_{1}
and the subscript “
2
” to denote parameters (i.e.
\eta_{2},\zeta_{2},\xi_{2})
associated with
\mu_{2}
.

Proposition 5.0
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Then, in the setting of
Theorem

5.3
,

\displaystyle B_{\mu_{1}}\leq B_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1

(27)

\displaystyle E_{\mu_{1}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega\,.

(28)

In
Section

7
, we thoroughly investigate the consequences of the above conditions on the model-dependent constants. Here we simply note that, when the training and test covariance scales are the same,
Remark

5.1
implies that the conditions on the constants in
Proposition

5.7
are automatically satisfied and we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 5.0
.

Consider two LJSDs satisfying
4
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Then, in the setting of
Theorem

5.3
,
B_{\mu_{1}}\leq B_{\mu_{2}}
and, if
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
,
E_{\mu_{1}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}
.

When the scale of the training and test distributions are equal,
Corollary

5.8
shows that
Definition

4.1
provides an essentially model-independent condition to determine the impact of covariate shift on the test error. Interestingly, both the bias (which arises from both regularization and model misspecification) and the total error (which has additional contributions from the randomness in
W
,
X
, and
\varepsilon
) respond in tandem to a covariate shift. This behavior is illustrated in
Fig.

1
for the
(\alpha,\theta)
-diatomic LJSD. Following the vertical lines upward in (a), (b), or the x-axis rightward in (e) yields easier shifts and a corresponding decrease in the bias and total error.

As we discuss in
Section

7
, even when
4
and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
hold, the variance need not be ordered. The underlying obstacle is a lack of ordering on the
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}
functionals in general; however, in the special case of a
pure-scale
shift, this ordering is restored, and we can derive conditions that guarantee that the variance is also ordered.

Proposition 5.0
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{i}}[r|\lambda]=s_{i}\lambda
, i.e. a pure-scale shift by
s_{i}
, with
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Then
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
and, in the setting of
Theorem

5.3
,

\displaystyle B_{\mu_{1}}\leq B_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1

(29)

\displaystyle V_{\mu_{1}}\leq V_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}

(30)

\displaystyle E_{\mu_{1}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}\,.

(31)

Proposition

5.9
provides a complement to many of our results, which otherwise emphasize nontrivial spectral differences that persist even when the training and test scales are equal. By focusing on pure-scale shifts,
Proposition

5.9
isolates the model-dependent conditions under which an increase in scale results in an increase in the bias, variance, and total error, generalizing the analogous results from
Section

3
on linear regression.

5.3
The benefit of overparameterization

While
Proposition

5.7
shows that harder shifts increase the error, it is natural to wonder whether this increase can be mitigated by judicious model selection. In practice, empirical investigations have shown that the performance of large, overparameterized models tends to deteriorate less under distribution shift than their smaller counterparts
(
Hendrycks et al. 2021
)
. We obtain a number of theoretical results that formally prove the benefit of overparameterization in our random feature setting.

First, we show that the bias decreases (or stays constant) when additional random features are added, which increases the model capacity and accords with the intuition of the bias as a measure of the model’s ability to fit the data.

Proposition 5.0
.

In the setting of
Theorem

5.3
, if
\xi\leq 1
, the bias
B_{\mu}
is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
.

The condition
\xi\leq 1
is necessary to
Proposition

5.10
, as without it the bias can behave nonmonotonically, demonstrating one counterintuitive consequence of covariate shift (see
Section

7
for more discussion). We emphasize that if we additionally invoke
4
, then this condition is automatically satisfied. The following proposition shows that, in contrast to the bias, the variance is strictly monotonic in the overparameterized regime, irrespective of the value of
\xi
. Note that our proof requires the setting of ridgeless regression (
\gamma=0
), but numerical investigation suggests this condition may not be necessary (see
Fig.

1
).

Proposition 5.0
.

In the setting of
Corollary

5.4
and in the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\psi<\phi
), the variance
V_{\mu}
is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
.

The explosion of variance at the interpolation threshold and its subsequent decay have been demonstrated in previous studies of the high-dimensional limit of random feature regression in the absence of covariate shift
(
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
;
Mei and Montanari 2021
)
, in stark contrast to what classical theory would suggest.
Proposition

5.11
confirms the existence of analogous behavior under covariate shift.

Taken together,
Propositions

5.10
and
5.11
imply that some of the benefits of overparameterzation extend to models evaluated out-of-distribution. An additional benefit is that overparameterized models are more robust: the difference in error between unshifted and shifted test distributions is smaller for larger models. A formal statement of this enhanced robustness is given in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.0
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
{1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}}
. Then, in the setting of
Corollary

5.4
and in the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\psi<\phi
), the generalization gap
E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}}
is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
.

Recalling
Remark

5.1
, we again note that if we additionally invoke
4
, then the condition on the model-dependent parameters
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
is automatically satisfied.

In
Fig.

1
,
Propositions

5.10
,
5.11
and
5.12
are illustrated. Following the horizontal lines rightward in (a), (b), and (c) or the x-axis rightward in (d) and (f) leads to models with more parameters. The monotonicity of the bias across the whole range of parameterization is evident, as is the necessity of considering the monotonicity of the variance and generalization gap only when
\phi>\psi
.

5.4
Linear trends between in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization

We have discussed how overparameterization yields improved generalization performance for both unshifted and shifted distributions, which hints that these two quantities are positively correlated. Indeed, recently
Hendrycks et al. 2021
have suggested increasing model size as a path to increased robustness. Additionally, the empirical studies of
Recht et al. 2019
;
Taori et al. 2020
;
Miller et al. 2021
have further refined this observation by discovering a linear relationship between the performance of models of varying complexity on unshifted and shifted test data. In the context of ridgeless random feature regression, we provide a formal proof of this linear relationship, which holds in the overparameterized regime. In the setting of random feature regression, the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
provides a surrogate for the complexity of the model class.

Figure 2
:
Linear relationship between in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization error.
(a)
Theoretical predictions for shifted versus unshifted error for models with varying degrees of overparameterization
\phi/\psi>1
, obtained via
Corollary

5.4
for the
(3,\theta)
-diatomic LJSD (
Eq.

12
) with
\phi=n_{0}/m=0.5
,
\sigma=\text{ReLU}
,
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0.01
and two different values of the shift-power
\theta
. Markers represent simulations for
n_{0}=512
. The negated errors are plotted so that performance improves left to right and bottom to top, in order to match the behavior of the accuracy metric.
(b)
Reproduction of the empirical results of
Recht et al. 2019
;
Taori et al. 2020
, showing the relationship between the classification accuracy of various models on the original ImageNet test set and two shifted ImageNet datasets: a “hard" dataset with greyscale corruptions, Grey, and an “easy" dataset with high inter-annotator agreement, TopImgs. In both (
a
) and (
b
), the slope is greater than one for the hard shift and less than one for the easy shift, in accordance with
Proposition

5.13
.

Proposition 5.0
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. In the setting of
Corollary

5.4
and in the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\psi<\phi
),
E_{\mu_{2}}
is linear in
E_{\mu_{1}}
, parametrically in
\phi/\psi
:

E_{\mu_{2}}=E_{0}+\underbrace{\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}}\left({\frac{\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}}\right)}_{\textsc{slope}}E_{\mu_{1}}\,,

(32)

where
E_{0}
and
\textsc{slope}\geq 0
are constants independent of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
. Moreover, if
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
, then
\textsc{slope}\geq 1
.

As above,
Remark

5.1
implies that the condition
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
is automatically satisfied if we additionally invoke
4
. Specializing
Proposition

5.13
to the case
\mu_{1}=\mu_{\emptyset}
yields the claimed linear relationship between the generalization performance with shifted and unshifted test data as the overparameterization ratio varies. Finally, we note that
\textsc{slope}\geq 0
implies that improved performance on the unshifted distribution translates into improved performance on the shifted distribution as well.

Remark 5.0
.

In the setting of
Proposition

5.13
, if
4
holds and
E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}
denotes the error on the unshifted distribution,
E_{\mu}=E_{0}+\textsc{slope}\cdot E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}
, parametrically in
\phi/\psi
, where
E_{0}
and
\textsc{slope}\geq 0
are constants independent of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
. Moreover,
\textsc{slope}\geq 1
when
\mu
is hard and
\textsc{slope}\leq 1
when
\mu
is easy.

Remark

5.14
makes the additional nontrivial prediction that an improvement on the unshifted distribution leads to a relatively greater improvement on the shifted distribution when the shift is hard, and to a relatively smaller improvement when the shift is easy. These predictions are corroborated qualitatively in the data from
Recht et al. 2018
;
Recht et al. 2019
;
Miller et al. 2020
;
Miller et al. 2021
. We plot this linear behavior in
Fig.

2
, where (a) shows the random feature model and (b) shows an example of data from
Recht et al. 2019
;
Taori et al. 2020
. The striking similarity in these plots is evident.

6
Optimal regularization

Several prior works studying double descent in the random feature model with identity covariance have found a divergence of the total error and the variance at the interpolation threshold (
\phi=\psi
) in the ridgeless limit
(
Mei and Montanari 2021
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
, e.g.)
. For non-zero regularization, the divergence is tamed, and when the regularizer is tuned optimally for each overparameterization scale,
Mei and Montanari 2021
observed that this effect is removed and the test error appears to be strictly decreasing in the number of random features.

The story is more nuanced in the presence of covariate shift because the optimal regularizer depends on the LJSD
\mu
, i.e.
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\argmin E_{\mu}(\gamma)
. Moreover, there are two natural ways to tune the regularizer: it can be tuned to an optimal value
\gamma^{\text{opt}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu_{\emptyset}}
that minimizes the test error on the unshifted training distribution, or it can be tuned to an optimal value
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}
that minimizes the test error on the shifted test distribution. These settings can be approximately realized when one has access to a large validation set drawn from either the training or test distributions, respectively.

We investigate the behavior of the test error under these two choices of optimal regularization in
Fig.

3
. The curves in this figure were obtained by minimizing the shifted and unshifted test error with a zeroth-order optimizer over a nonnegative support. Note that, counterintuitively, there is no guarantee in this setting that the optimal value of
\gamma
will be nonnegative
(
Kobak et al. 2020
;
Wu and Xu 2020
;
Bartlett et al. 2021
, see e.g.)
. While negative regularization is certainly an interesting scenario worthy of further investigation, it does not arise for the low signal-to-noise ratios (or equivalently large values of
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
) chosen for these simulations.

Figure 3
:
The limiting test error and relative regularization strength under two methods of optimal regularization as a function of the overparameterization ratio (
\phi/\psi=n_{1}/m
) and the shift power (
\theta
) for the
(4,\theta)
-diatomic LJSD (
Eq.

12
) with
{\phi=n_{0}/m=0.5}
,
\sigma=\text{ReLU}
, and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=1
. (
a
) With regularization tuned on the unshifted training distribution,
\gamma=\gamma^{\text{opt}}
, the test error exhibits nonmonotonicity in the overparameterization ratio when the shift is sufficiently hard. (
b
) With regularization tuned on the shifted test distribution,
\gamma=\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
, the test error is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio. (
c
) The ratio of optimal regularization values,
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}/\gamma^{\text{opt}}
is large for hard shifts in the underparameterized regime, but approaches one as
\phi/\psi
becomes large, as predicted by
Proposition

6.1
. Dashed lines denote
5\%
deviations from unity, and highlight the existence of a finite
\phi/\psi\approx 2
for which the optimal regularization value is independent of shift strength, which is also evident in (
f
). (
d
) 1D horizontal slices of (
a
,
b
) demonstrate the monotonicity in
\phi/\psi
for
\gamma=\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
(dashed lines) and nonmonotonicity for
\gamma=\gamma^{\text{opt}}
(solid lines) for hard shifts. Consistent with
Proposition

6.1
, for large overparameterization ratios, the solid and dashed lines overlap. (
e
) 1D vertical slices of (
a
,
b
) demonstrate monotonicity in
\theta
and that the distinction between
\gamma^{\text{opt}}
and
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
diminishes for large overparameterization ratios. (
f
) 1D horizontal slices of (
c
) show that
\gamma^{\text{opt}}\approx\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
for large overparameterization ratios, consistent with
Proposition

6.1
.

We observe that the qualitative features of optimal regularization with and without covariate shift are similar if the regularizer is tuned on the shifted distribution. Specifically, the peak at the interpolation threshold vanishes and the test error becomes nonincreasing as a function of the overparameterization ratio. Indeed, horizontal slices of
Fig.

3
(b) (shown as dashed lines in (d)) exhibit the monotonicity of the total error in this setting.

On the other hand, if
\gamma
is tuned to minimize the test error on the unshifted training distribution, horizontal slices of
Fig.

3
(a) (shown as solid lines in (d)) show residual nonmonotonicity in the test error. Interestingly, when a peak in the test error does persist under optimal regularization, it no longer appears at the interpolation threshold, suggesting that the residual peak may be of a different origin.

The behavior of the test error under decreasing shift strength can be seen by following vertical slices upward in
Fig.

3
(a,b), or by following the x-axis rightward in (e), and, as might be expected, the distinction between
\gamma^{\text{opt}}
and
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
is less apparent for easier shifts. Indeed, the nonmonotonicity of the test error in (d) appears only for hard shifts, and the curves for
\gamma^{\text{opt}}
and
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
in (e) overlap for hard shifts.

The relative strength of the regularizer when optimized on the shifted versus unshifted distributions is shown in
Fig.

3
(c), with horizontal slices for several values of the shift strength shown in (f). In the underparameterized regime,
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}/\gamma^{\text{opt}}
depends strongly on the shift strength, with
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}\gg\gamma^{\text{opt}}
for hard shifts. In the overparameterized regime, however, the optimal
\gamma
values are not significantly different. For all values of the the shift strength
\theta
, it appears that
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}=\gamma^{\text{opt}}
for some intermediate overparameterization ratio (around
\phi/\psi\approx 2
in (c) and (f)). While we have no immediate explanation for this curious observation, the equality
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}=\gamma^{\text{opt}}
does occur when
\phi/\psi\to\infty
, as following proposition shows.

Proposition 6.0
.

In the setting of
Corollary

5.5
and under
4
, the optimal regularization
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}
is independent of
\mu
and satisfies,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\argmin_{\gamma\geq-\rho\omega}E_{\mu}(\gamma)=\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}-\omega)\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{\mu}^{\text{opt}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{\mu}(\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu})=\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}\,.

(33)

Moreover, the optimal error is ordered with respect to shift strength, i.e. for two LJSDs
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
,
E_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{opt}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{opt}}
.

As also observed by
Kobak et al. 2020
;
Wu and Xu 2020
;
Bartlett et al. 2021
,
Proposition

6.1
confirms that the optimal regularization coefficient
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}
can actually be negative. Nevertheless, owing to the implicit regularization of the nonlinear feature map described in
Corollary

5.5
, the effective regularizer
\gamma_{\text{eff}}
remains nonnegative, which can be seen by rewriting the expression for
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}
in terms of
\gamma_{\text{eff}}=\gamma/\rho+\omega
to find
\gamma_{\text{eff}}^{\text{opt}}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
. The latter relationship is the same as the well-known result for optimal regularization in linear ridge regression with orthonormal design
(
van Wieringen 2015
, see e.g.)
, and might have been anticipated given the connection to ridge regression described in
Remark

5.6
. Even without strict orthonormality,
Dobriban and Wager 2018
established that the optimal regularizer in high-dimensional ridge regression also equals
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
in the absence of covariate shift and under an isotropic source condition on the coefficient of the linear data-generating process.
7
7

7

Their optimal regularizer is given as
\phi\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}/\mathbb{E}[\beta^{\top}\beta]
but is equivalent to our
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
owing to normalization differences.
Perhaps surprisingly,
Proposition

6.1
shows that their result extends to the setting of random feature regression in the overparameterized limit, and, moreover, that the optimal regularizer is actually independent of the covariate shift, highlighting an additional benefit of overparameterization. This behavior can be seen in
Fig.

3
(f) where the optimal regularizers under all shifts approach each other for large
\phi/\psi
.

7
Analysis of shift hardness, covariate scale, and assumptions

In the previous sections, many concepts and assumptions have emerged as prerequisites for the stated results. The goal of this section is to interpret those assumptions and explain why they are a natural consequence of the model and our analysis. By giving concrete counterexamples, we exhibit the various ways our results can break down when certain assumptions are violated. In some cases, assumptions were used in the proof of a result but the conclusion seems robust to relaxing the assumption—this kind of robustness speaks to the generality of our qualitative observations. This section also allows for us to introduce some refinements of our previous results. Interestingly, we find that optimal regularization can preserve the monotonicity of the total error with respect to the shift strength, even when the assumptions of
Proposition

5.7
are violated.

In
Section

7.1
, we look in detail at the monotonicity condition in the partial order over covariate shifts given in
Definition

4.1
. By constructing a simple example of several LJSDs, we demonstrate that when the monotonicity condition is violated between a pair of LJSDs, i.e. the LJSDs are incomparable, the ordering of their total errors becomes model-dependent and can be changed by varying the overparameterization ratio or the activation function. Nevertheless, we introduce a quantitative measure of a shift’s deviation from monotonicity that can be leveraged to deduce model-independent upper and lower bounds on its total error.

In
Section

7.2
, we investigate the impact of label noise, i.e. the signal-to-noise ratio for
Eq.

2
. More specifically, we show what happens when the condition
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
is violated. We find this condition is necessary to guarantee the error ordering in
Proposition

5.7
and
Corollary

5.8
, since making the label noise sufficiently large can cause the variance to greatly increase and violate the asserted inequalities. We find that optimal regularization can prevent this behavior and maintain the error ordering, even in the presence of large label noise.

Section

7.3
contains a detailed discussion of the effect of the training and test covariance scales, which are denoted by
s
and
s_{*}
. In
Section

7.3.1
, we begin by developing an intuition for how a mismatch between
s
and
s_{*}
affects the random feature model in high dimensions and can lead to nonmonotonicity in the bias as a function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
and the regularization constant
\gamma
. Following that, in
Section

7.3.2
, we study how
s
and
s_{*}
are related to the model-dependent constants of
Eq.

17
and the model-dependent conditions of several propositions, such as the condition
\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
in
Eq.

28
of
Proposition

5.7
, and show that the monotonicity of the bias and total error can fail when these conditions are violated. Finally, in
Section

7.3.3
, we focus on the case of pure-scale shifts, refine
Proposition

5.9
, and show how the conditions for the monotonicity of the bias and the variance can be mutually exclusive.

To conclude this section,
Section

7.4
probes the prerequisite conditions in
Proposition

5.13
. We find that the overparameterization condition
\phi>\psi
is critical, with highly nonlinear trends when it is violated. In contrast, we observe the ridgeless assumption can be relaxed without significant deviations from linearity.

7.1
Monotonicity condition in partial order of shifts

The partial order we introduced on covariate shifts in
Definition

4.1
has been central to much of our discussion. The core idea arises from asking what assumption is necessary to make conclusions about the ordering of the functionals
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
using the Harris inequality, but a natural first-principles interpretation can be developed based on how well the target function can be learned in different eigendirections from the training data, and then how much the learned function will be tested in each eigendirection based on its representation in the test data.

Unfortunately, the partial order condition is quite strict, as it requires all eigendirections of the shift to act in concert, rendering many shifts incomparable under the partial order. Such incomparable shifts do not unanimously hurt or help in all eigendirections, and thus their combined effect is a complicated average of the effects in each eigendirection. While
Theorem

5.3
accounts for this averaging in a precise way, it nevertheless remains challenging to draw simple conclusions about the behavior of incomparable shifts.

7.1.1
Monotonicity is necessary to guarantee ordering of the total error

The monotonicity of overlap coefficients in
Definition

4.1
is necessary in order to guarantee the ordering of errors in
Proposition

5.7
. For a simple counterexample, consider the following four LJSDs,

\displaystyle\mu_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\delta_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{2})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{4},\lambda_{1})})

(34)

\displaystyle\mu_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\delta_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{4})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{1})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{4},\lambda_{2})})

(35)

\displaystyle\mu_{3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\delta_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{4})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{4},\lambda_{1})})

(36)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\mu_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}(\delta_{(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{2},\lambda_{2})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{3},\lambda_{3})}+\delta_{(\lambda_{4},\lambda_{4})})\,,

(37)

where
\lambda_{1}=0.6
,
\lambda_{2}=0.24
,
\lambda_{3}=0.12
and
\lambda_{4}=0.04
. Note that
\mu_{4}=\mu_{\emptyset}
and these LJSDs have equal training and test covariance scales, i.e.
s=s_{1}=s_{2}=s_{3}=s_{4}=1
. Moreover, the partial order in
Definition

4.1
gives
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{4}
and
\mu_{2}\geq\mu_{4}
, with all other pairs of LJSDs incomparable. In particular, focusing on
\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{3}
, the ratios of overlap coefficients are

\frac{r_{2,1}}{r_{3,1}}=\frac{1}{6}\,,\quad\frac{r_{2,2}}{r_{3,2}}=3\,,\quad\frac{r_{2,3}}{r_{3,3}}=5\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{r_{2,4}}{r_{3,4}}=\frac{2}{5}\,,

(38)

so the sequence
r_{2,i}/r_{3,i}
is nonmonotonic in
i
. The violation of monotonicity is minimal, in the sense that only a single ratio (
r_{2,4}/r_{3,4}
) is out of order; nevertheless, the strict model-independent ordering of test errors is broken because of this nonmonotonicity, as can be seen in
Fig.

4
. The model-dependence in the ordering is evident for models of varying sizes in
Fig.

4
(a) and models with different nonlinearities in
Fig.

4
(b). On the other hand, for the pairs of LJSDs for which the monotonicity of overlap coefficients is satisfied,
Fig.

4
shows that the ordering of test errors is satisfied for all models, as predicted by
Proposition

5.7
.

Figure 4
:
Predictions of
Theorem

5.3
for the total test error with
\phi=0.5
,
\gamma=0.1
,
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0.01
,
\phi=0.5
, and
\sigma(x)=\alpha(e^{x}-1)1_{x\leq 0}+\text{ReLU}(x)

(
Clevert et al. 2016
, i.e. the ELU function of)
as a function of (
a
) the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
for
\alpha=0
and (
b
) the ELU scale parameter
\alpha
for
\phi/\psi=0.25
, for four different LJSDs
\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{4}
. The
\mu_{i}
are described in Eqs. (
34
)-(
37
). The only pairs of LJSDs that are comparable via the partial order in
Definition

4.1
are
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{4}
and
\mu_{2}\geq\mu_{4}
, and the strict ordering of the error for those pairs is seen for all values of
\phi/\psi
and
\alpha
. The orange (
\mu_{2}
) and green (
\mu_{3}
) curves cross one another in both (
a
) and (
b
), illustrating two ways the ordering of the error exhibits model dependence induced by nonmontonicity of overlap coefficients in
Definition

4.1
, thereby showing that the monotonicity condition is necessary for
Proposition

5.7
.

7.1.2
Predictable behavior for incomparable shifts

One natural question is whether
Proposition

5.7
has anything at all to say about shifts for which the overlap ratios are not strictly monotonic. First, note that small violations of monotonicity for finite-size systems caused by
o(1)
overlap coefficients do not survive in the high-dimensional limit. To make this point concrete, consider the training and test covariance matrices given by
\Sigma=\diag(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n_{0}})=\diag(1/n_{0},2/n_{0},\ldots,1)
and

\Sigma^{*}_{ij}=\begin{cases}1/n_{0}&\text{if }i=j=2\\
\Sigma_{ij}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

(39)

resulting in overlap coefficients
r_{2}=1/n_{0}
and
r_{i}=\lambda_{i}
otherwise. Evidently, the monotonicity condition on
r_{i}/\lambda_{i}
akin to
Proposition

3.3
does not hold; however, the limit of this covariate shift has LJSD
\mu=\mu_{\emptyset}=\text{Unif}(\{{(x,x):x\in[0,1]}\})
, meaning that the finite-size nonmonotonicity vanishes in the high-dimensional limit. This observation suggests that the limiting predictions might still be useful in practice despite small deviations from monotonicity at finite-size.

In fact, even if nonmonotonicity in the overlap ratios does persist in the high-dimensional limit,
Definition

4.1
and
Proposition

5.7
can still provide useful information. To illustrate this point, suppose the LJSD
\mu
is incomparable with
\mu_{\emptyset}
, implying that the function
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]/\lambda
is nonmonotonic, and introduce the functions

L(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf_{0\leq\lambda\leq x}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}\quad\text{and}\quad U(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq x}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}\,,

(40)

which satisfy
L(\lambda)\leq{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}/{\lambda}\leq U(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
, with
L(\lambda)
nonincreasing and
U(\lambda)
nondecreasing in
\lambda
. The functions
L
and
U
are a quantitative way to characterize the extent to which
\mu
violates the monotonicity condition. Indeed, if
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}/{\lambda}
is nonincreasing or nondecreasing, then
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}/{\lambda}=L(\lambda)
or
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}/{\lambda}=U(\lambda)
respectively. In general,
L
and
U
create the smallest envelope around the function
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]/\lambda
possible with nonincreasing and nondecreasing functions (see
Fig.

5
(a,b) for an example).

In
Proposition

7.1
, we translate this quantitative characterization of nonmonotonicity into a bound on
B_{\mu}
and
E_{\mu}
relative to
B_{\mu_{\emptyset}}
and
E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}
.

Figure 5
:
We define the parametric family of LJSD
\mu
whose distribution is given by the random variable
\left({\lambda,\lambda-c\sin(\lambda)^{3}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
for
\lambda\sim\text{Unif}[0,2\pi]
.

(a)
The support of
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
is plotted for different values of
c\in\{{0,0.3,0.6,0.9}\}
. As expected
\mu_{\emptyset}
is recovered by
c=0
and
\mu
becomes increasingly nonmonotonic as
c
increases.

(b)
The ratio of overlaps
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]/\lambda
, as in
Definition

4.1
, is plotted as a function of
\lambda
(solid line) for the same values of
c
. Clearly this function is neither nonincreasing nor nondecreasing in
\lambda
when
c>0
, and moreover, the size of its oscillations are controlled by
c
. This is reflected in
U
(dashed line) and
L
(dotted lined), which progressively differ from
1
as
c
increases, thus increasing the shaded area between these curves.
(c)
The total error (blue) and bias (orange) are plotted for
\mu
as a function of
c
, along with their upper (dashed) and lower (dotted) bounds from
Proposition

7.1
. For large,
c
the gap between the upper and lower bounds are large, but as
c\to 0
they tightly bound the total error and bias.
(d)

B_{\mu}
and
E_{\mu}
are tracked and enveloped by their bounds for all parameterization ratios.

Proposition 7.0
.

Let
\mu
be a LJSD such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lambda]=s=s_{*}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]
. Then, in the setting of
Theorem

5.3
,

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda L(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}B_{\mu_{\emptyset}}\leq B_{\mu}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda U(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}B_{\mu_{\emptyset}},

(41)

and, if
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
,

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda L(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}\leq E_{\mu}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda U(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}.

(42)

In the special case where
\mu=\mu_{\emptyset}
,
Proposition

7.1
yields a tight bound since
L(\lambda)=U(\lambda)=1
. We exemplify
Proposition

7.1
for a specific family of LJSDs
\mu
given by the random variable

\left({\lambda,\lambda-c\sin(\lambda)^{3}}\right)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}\text{ for }\ \lambda\ \sim\text{Unif}[0,2\pi]

(43)

parameterized by some constant
c\in[0,1]
(see
Fig.

5
(a) for the LJSD’s support). When
c=0
, we recover the case of no shift
\mu_{\emptyset}
. By increasing
c
, the magnitude of the nonmonotonicity increases, since
c
