is the coefficient of the oscillatory term
\sin(\lambda)^{3}/\lambda
. Note that for all
c
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\frac{x-c\sin(x)^{3}}{2\pi}dx=\pi,

(44)

implying
\mu
is always an equal-scale shift. Calculating further, we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]/\lambda

\displaystyle=1-c\sin(\lambda)^{3}/\lambda,

(45)

\displaystyle L(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1-c\sin(\lambda)/\lambda&\text{if }\lambda\leq x_{L}\\
1-c\sin(x_{L})/x_{L}&\text{if }\lambda>x_{L}\end{cases},\quad\text{and}

(46)

\displaystyle U(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\lambda\leq\pi\\
1-c\sin(\lambda)/\lambda&\text{if }\pi<\lambda\leq x_{U}\\
1-c\sin(x_{U})/x_{U}&\text{if }x_{U}<\lambda\end{cases},

(47)

where
x_{L}\approx 1.324
and
x_{U}\approx 4.641
are solutions to
3x=\tan(x)
. In
Fig.

5
(b), we can see the effect of
c
on the functions
L
and
U
: as
c
increases, the envelopes around
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]/\lambda
must also increase. Indeed, many possible LJSDs could lie within each envelope, which should make intuitive the requirement for a bound, as well as for this bound to loosen as the envelope increases in size.

Finally, the coefficients
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lambda L(\lambda)]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lambda U(\lambda)]
can be calculated by integrating
Eqs.

46
and
47
with respect to the uniform density over
[0,2\pi]
. Clearly, the difference in these coefficients (and hence the bounds on the bias and test error) depend on how close
L
and
U
are, i.e. the size of the envelope. In
Fig.

5
(c), we can observe this behavior. As
c
increases, so does the envelope around
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]/\lambda
and thus also the looseness of the bound on the bias and total error of the shift. Note that for the simple LJSDs considered here, we found that
L
,
U
,
B_{\mu}
, and
E_{\mu}
are linear in
c
, which occurs because the nonmonotonic component of
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]
, i.e.
\sin(\lambda)^{3}/\lambda
, is scaled by
c
. Of course, this linear behavior is specific to this example and need not occur in general. Finally, in
Fig.

5
(d), we observe how the respective bounds follow the bias and total error as a function of the overparameterization ratio,
\phi/\psi
.

7.2
Label noise

Proposition

5.7
and
Corollary

5.8
provide conditions for the bias and total error to decrease in response to easier shifts, but they place no explicit constraints on the variance. Indeed, the variance is not guaranteed to decrease, and while it happened to do so in the setting of
Fig.

1
, it may instead increase, as can be seen in
Fig.

6
. Still, for the total error to decrease in response to easier shifts, any increase in the variance must be offset by a larger decrease in the bias. The small label-noise condition
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
ensures that this is the case. Note that since the label noise is simply an additive effect on the labels, its magnitude does not affect the bias, but recall from
Eq.

6
that this is not the only source of variance in the random feature model
(
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
)
.

Fig.

6
(a) shows the bias, variance, and total error as a function of the label noise for an easy shift (
\theta=2
) and a hard shift (
\theta=-2
). As expected, the bias is independent of the label noise, and the variance and total error increase with increasing
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
. For small label noise (
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}<\omega
), the total error for the hard shift is larger than that of the easy shift, consistent with
Proposition

5.7
. For
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}>\omega
, however, as the label noise increases, the variance for the easy shift increases more rapidly than that of the hard shift, leading to a crossing of the error curves so that the total error for the easy shift becomes larger than that of the hard shift.

Figure 6
:
Predictions from
Theorem

5.3
for the total test error, bias, and variance with
\alpha=4
,
\phi=4
,
\psi=1/4
,
\sigma=\text{ReLU}
,
\gamma=10^{-4}
, and
\omega=1-\tfrac{2}{\pi}\approx 0.36
as a function of
(a)
increasing label noise and
(b,c)
increasing shift power
\theta
(corresponding to progressively easier shifts in the sense of
Definition

4.1
).
(a)
For sufficiently large label noise, the variance for easy shift increases more rapidly than that of the hard shift, leading to a crossing of the error curves so that the total error for the easy shift becomes larger than that of the hard shift.
(b)
The predictions of
Proposition

5.7
are corroborated when
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}<\omega
, but when
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}>\omega
the total error and variance can increase with increasing
\theta
.
(c)
Under the same configuration in (
b
), but with optimal regularization, the total error is seen to decrease for easier shifts, irrespective of the label noise.

Fig.

6
(b) shows a complementary perspective on the same phenomenon. In this panel, the bias, variance, and total error are plotted as a function of the shift power for large (
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=4>\omega
) and small (
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=1/4<\omega
) label noise. As predicted by
Proposition

5.7
, the bias is the same for both values of label noise, and it decreases for easier shifts. On the other hand, the variance is seen to increase for easier shifts, and for large label noise, it increases sufficiently rapidly so as to induce an increase in the total error, whereas for small label noise it does not.

Together,
Fig.

6
(a) and
Fig.

6
(b) show that the small label-noise condition in
Proposition

5.7
and
Corollary

5.8
is necessary in order to prevent a large increase in the variance from breaking the ordering of the total error. Of course, in practice it might not be possible to avoid large label noise, and regularization is an effective remedy to prevent the variance from becoming too large in such cases. It is therefore natural to wonder whether tuning the regularizer optimally is sufficient to guarantee that the total error decreases in response to easier shifts, irrespective of the label noise.
Proposition

6.1
proves this is indeed the case for infinite overparameterization and
Fig.

6
(c) also shows it to be the case for finite overparameterization ratios in the setting of
Fig.

6
(b).

7.3
Discrepancies in scale between the training and test distributions

In the high-dimensional limit, the behavior of the random feature matrix can be modeled by a projection of the data into a linear component and noise component, whose scales are determined by the activation function
\sigma
. This notion is made mathematically precise by the linearization technique of
Adlam et al. 2019
, reviewed in
Section

I.2
. Recalling the definition of the constants in
Eq.

17
, the linear component has scale
\sqrt{\rho}
and the noise component has scale
\sqrt{\eta-\zeta}
. Note that the bias of the predictive function is only affected by its linear component, whereas the variance depends on the noise component as well. This observation helps explain why results concerning the bias, such as
Proposition

5.7
in
Eq.

27
, only make assumptions on
\rho
, while results concerning the variance or total error, such as
Proposition

5.7
in
Eq.

28
, also make assumptions on
\eta
.

In the presence of covariate shift, the training and test distributions may have different scales, leading to two separate sets of constants and an expanded set of possible relationships between them. As a result, for unequal scales, many of our results require specifying substantially more intricate conditions on the constants (e.g. compare
Proposition

5.7
to
Corollary

5.8
). In this section, we provide a detailed analysis of these conditions and the effect of unequal scales.

7.3.1
Unequal scales and nonmonotonicity of the bias

Among the various constants induced by the activation function
\sigma
, of particular interest is
\xi=\sqrt{\rho_{*}/\rho}
, which is the ratio of the scales of the linear component of
\sigma
on the training and test distributions.
Theorem

5.3
shows that the bias and variance have markedly different dependence on
\xi
—while the variance scales multiplicatively with
\xi^{2}
, the bias has nontrivial quadratic dependence.

In fact, the behavior of the bias can be radically different depending on whether
\xi
is greater or less than one. Indeed,
Propositions

5.7
and
5.10
both require the assumption that
\xi\leq 1
, since the bias can be highly nonmonotonic when
\xi>1
. To elucidate this dichotomy, it is fruitful to understand how the bias behaves as a function of
x
. Because
x
is monotonic with respect to various other parameters, such as
\gamma
(see
Eq.

S1
) and
\phi/\psi
(see
Eq.

S114
), any nonmonotonicity with respect to
x
implies nonmonotonicity with respect to those parameters as well.

Recall that the bias compares the average prediction at a test point
\mathbf{x}
against its label in squared error, and then averages over the test point and
\beta
(see
Eq.

6
). Using the linearization technique and splitting the random features on the test point into their linear and noise components, we find that the average predictor satisfies

\mathbb{E}[\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})]=\beta^{\top}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\mathbb{E}\left[{XK^{-1}F^{\top}f/n_{1}}\right]\approx\hat{\beta}^{\top}{\mathbf{x}}/{\sqrt{n_{0}}},

(48)

where
\hat{\beta}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{\rho_{*}}\,\mathbb{E}\left[{{W^{\top}FK^{-1}X^{\top}}/({{n_{1}\sqrt{n_{0}}}})}\right]\beta
. Writing
\beta
and
\hat{\beta}
in the eigenbasis of
\Sigma
, where their coefficents in eigendirection
\mathbf{v}_{\lambda}
are
\beta_{\lambda}=\mathbf{v}_{\lambda}^{\top}\beta
and
\hat{\beta}_{\lambda}=\mathbf{v}_{\lambda}^{\top}\hat{\beta}
, the bias can be expressed as,

\displaystyle B_{\Sigma^{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}[(\mathbb{E}[\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})]-y(\mathbf{x}))^{2}]

(49)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}[(\hat{\beta}-\beta)^{\top}\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})^{2}]

(50)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[(\hat{\beta}-\beta)^{\top}\Sigma^{*}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)]

(51)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[(\hat{\beta}_{\lambda}-\beta_{\lambda})^{\top}\mathbf{v}^{\top}_{\lambda}\Sigma^{*}\mathbf{v}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\hat{\beta}_{\lambda^{\prime}}-\beta_{\lambda^{\prime}})]

(52)

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{\lambda}r_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{\beta}(\hat{\beta}_{\lambda}-\beta_{\lambda})^{2}\,,

(53)

where final line follows from the (nontrivial) observation that
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[(\hat{\beta}-\beta)(\hat{\beta}-\beta)^{\top}]
is approximately diagonal in the eigenbasis of
\Sigma
.
Eq.

53
shows that the bias can be understood as a weighted measurement of how well the expected estimator
\hat{\beta}
approximates
\beta
in each of the eigendirections.

Figure 7
:
Predictions for the bias under LJSD
\mu=({\delta_{1/50,1}+\delta_{5,1}+\delta_{1000,1}})/3
with
\gamma=0
and
\phi=1
for two different values of
\xi
,
1/3
and
8/5
.
(a)
The components of the bias in each eigendirection,
Eq.

54
, normalized by the probability of the eigenvalue (i.e.
1/3
under
\mu
) are plotted as a function of
x
for each
\lambda
and both
\xi
. When
\xi=1/3<1
(dashed), all components start at
1/3
, then decrease monotonically to
(1-\xi)^{2}/3
; when
\xi=8/5>1
(solid), each component starts at
1/3
, decreases, has a root and minimum at
x=\phi/(\lambda(\xi-1))
, and then increases again to
(\xi-1)^{2}/3
. This difference between
\xi<1
and
\xi>1
translates into the total bias (orange curves), which is obtained by summing the components from each
\lambda
.
(b)

x
as a function of
\phi/\psi
for three values of
\omega
. While
x
is monotonic to
\phi/\psi
, not all values of
x
are achievable, with
x
plateauing to a constant for
\phi>\psi
. These limiting values of
x
and their corresponding biases are denoted with dotted lines in (a).
(c)
Combining (a) and (b), the bias is also nonmonotonic in
\phi/\psi
. Smaller values of
\omega
allow for larger values of
x
, which uncover greater portions of the bias curve in (a), leading to additional nonmonotonicity.

In fact, it is straightforward to extend this interpretation to the high-dimensional limit by rearranging the expression in
Eq.

20
to find
B_{\mu}=\int B_{\lambda}(x;\xi)d\mu_{\text{train}}(\lambda)
, where

B_{\lambda}(x;\xi)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]w_{\xi}(x)\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad w_{\xi}(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left({1-\xi+\xi\frac{\phi}{\phi+x\lambda}}\right)^{2}\,.

(54)

Comparing
Eq.

53
to
Eq.

54
, we can immediately see that
\mathbb{E}_{\beta}(\hat{\beta}_{\lambda}-\beta_{\lambda})^{2}\to w_{\xi}(x)
in the high-dimensional limit. The factor
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]
reweights the contribution of each eigendirection by its relative importance in the test distribution. Note that
w_{\xi}
depends on the scale ratio
\xi
but is otherwise independent of the shift. For the simple uniform setting in which
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]=1
,
Fig.

7
(a) illustrates
B_{\lambda}(x;\xi)=w_{\xi}(x)
for
\lambda\in\{{10^{-2},5,10^{3}}\}
and
\xi\in\{{1/3,8/5}\}
. It can be observed that the overall shape depends strongly on the value of
\xi
, with monotonicity for
\xi=1/3<1
and nonmonotonicity for
\xi=8/5>1
.

To build intuition for this behavior, we first note that
x\to 0
yields the zero predictor, i.e.
{\hat{\beta}=0}
, which follows from the fact that
w_{\xi}(0)
is independent of
\lambda
and satisfies
{w_{\xi}(0)=1=\mathbb{E}[\beta^{\top}\beta]/n_{0}}
.
Similarly,
\lim_{x\to\infty}w_{\xi}(x)=(1-\xi)^{2}
, which is also independent of
\lambda
, and is consistent with a mean predictor that scales each eigendirection uniformly, i.e.
\hat{\beta}=\xi\beta
. Piecing these two limits together paints a picture of how the bias depends on
x
, but the details depend on the value of
\xi
.

When
\xi\leq 1
,
w_{\xi}
is a decreasing function of
x
and asymptotes for large
x
to
(1-\xi)^{2}
, implying that
x\to\infty
is optimal from the perspective of bias reduction for all
\lambda
. Still, unless
\xi=1
, the predictor remains biased and
underestimates
all
\beta_{\lambda}
by a factor of
\xi
. This underestimation is due to the fact that
\xi<1
implies
\rho_{*}<\rho
, i.e. the linear component of the random feature projection of the data is
smaller
on the test distribution than it is on the training distribution, using the terminology of the linearization technique described above.

When
\xi>1
,
w_{\xi}
also asymptotes to
(1-\xi)^{2}
for large
x
, but this behavior is due to the opposite effect, namely an
overestimation
of all
\beta_{\lambda}
by a factor of
\xi
. This overestimation occurs because
\xi>1
implies
\rho_{*}>\rho
, i.e. the linear component of the random feature projection of the data is
larger
on the test distribution than it is on the training distribution. Because
x=0
corresponds to the zero predictor (which necessarily underestimates the coefficients in each eigendirection), there must be a transition between underestimation and overestimation, leading to a local minimum and nonmonotonicity in
x
. It follows from
Eq.

54
that the local minimum is in fact a zero and occurs at
x=\phi/(\lambda(\xi-1))
, implying that for every
\lambda
there is a unique
x
that renders the predictor unbiased in the corresponding eigendirection. However, because this optimal
x
depends on
\lambda
, it is not possible to optimize all eigendirections simultaneously, creating a tradeoff that can result in highly nonmonotonic behavior of the bias.

This behavior is depicted in
Fig.

7
(a), which shows that when
\xi=1/3<1
(dashed lines), all curves are strictly decreasing, but when
\xi=8/5>1
(solid lines), the minima of the curves for each
\lambda
occur at different values of
x
, resulting in several local minima. Note also that
x\to\infty
is not optimal.

In
Fig.

7
(b), we show how varying
\phi/\psi
and
\omega
adjusts the value of
x
. Observe that
x
is monotonic in
\phi/\psi
and
\omega
but not all values of
x>0
are achievable. Specifically, when
\phi>\psi
,
x
is constant in
\phi/\psi
and is bounded by
1/\omega
. Finally,
Fig.

7
(c) demonstrates that because
x
is monotonic in
\phi/\psi
, the nonmonotonic behavior of the bias as a function of
x
when
\xi>1
causes nonmonotonicity of the bias as a function of
\phi/\psi
as well.

7.3.2
Scale-induced model dependence

We now examine
Proposition

5.10
and the consequences of violating the condition
\xi\leq 1
. In
Fig.

8
(a), we show the total error, bias, and variance as a function of the overparameterization ratio for a model and LJSD for which
\xi>1
. While
Proposition

5.11
does still guarantee that the variance is nonincreasing in the overparameterized regime,
Proposition

5.10
no longer constrains the bias, and indeed we observe nonmonotonicity of the bias, with it actually increasing for large overparameterization ratios. Consistent with the discussion in the previous section, the underlying reason for this behavior can be traced to the
\xi
-dependence of
Eq.

20
, which allows for nontrivial interference between the various summands for
\xi>1
. This tradeoff between terms can be seen as a function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
in
Fig.

8
(a), which shows that the bias can be nonmonotonic and increase sufficiently rapidly so as to induce an increase in the total error, even in the overparameterized regime. While it may seem counterintuitive that the bias could increase when the overparameterization ratio is increased, the fact that
\partial x/\partial\psi\leq 0
(see
Eq.

S114
and
Fig.

7
(b)) means that the discussion in the previous section about
x
provides the explanation.

It is natural to wonder if the observed nonmonotonicity in
Fig.

8
(a) might be a consequence of sub-optimal regularization. To examine this question, in the same figure we also show the behavior of the model under optimal regularization, and indeed we observe that the nonmonotonicity disappears and the bias and total error become nonincreasing functions of the overparameterization ratio.

Figure 8
:
Predictions for the total error, bias, and variance for the random feature model with
\sigma(x)=x\exp(-x^{2})
,
\phi=0.25
,
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0.1
, and
\theta=2
in (
a
) and
\phi/\psi=2
in (
b
), for a scaled version of the
(4,\theta)
-diatomic LJSD with
s=1
and
s_{*}=0.25
, violating the model-dependent conditions in
Proposition

5.7
and
Proposition

5.10
. For fixed regularization,
\gamma=0.1
, the bias and total error are no longer nonincreasing functions of (
a
) the overparameterization ratio when
\phi/\psi>1
, nor (
b
) the shift power
\theta
. For optimal regularization,
\gamma=\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{*}
, the bias and total error are nonincreasing in
\phi/\psi
, but they are still nonmonontic as a function of
\theta
.

Next, we turn to
Proposition

5.7
and a comparison of the bias and total error of two different shifts. In this setting, there are two separate sets of model-dependent constants to consider, leading to the somewhat complicated set of conditions given in
Eq.

27
and
Eq.

28
. We first remark that these inequalities are sufficient to establish the results, but they are not necessarily optimal. In particular, the condition
\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1
in
Eq.

27
forces
\xi_{1}=1
whenever
\mu_{2}=\mu_{\emptyset}
, preventing any conclusions about
easy
shifts in the presence of unequal scales. While the bounds on
\xi_{1}
in
Eq.

27
can be loosened to allow for such conclusions, the improvements require introducing additional nontrivial spectral information, thereby coupling the model and the shift in an intricate way. We defer this more detailed analysis to future work.

Instead, we focus on the setting in which the two test scales are equal but differ from the training scale in such a manner as to violate the conditions of
Proposition

5.7
, i.e.
1<\xi=\xi_{1}=\xi_{2}
. In this case, the bias and total error can actually increase as the shifts become easier, as illustrated in
Fig.

8
(b). Again, the origin of this behavior can be traced to the nontrivial interference between the various summands in
Eq.

20
for
\xi>1
. Interestingly,
Fig.

8
(b) also shows that, unlike the behavior as a function of the overparameterization ratio, optimal regularization does not seem to guarantee monotonicity of the bias and error with respect to shift strength.

7.3.3
Pure-scale shifts

Much of the complexity in the statements and analysis of Props.
5.7
-
5.13
stems from the model’s dependence on the training and test covariance scales,
s
and
s_{*}
. To examine the effect of scale alone, we consider the simple pair of LJSDs
\mu_{1}=\delta_{(s,s_{1})}
and
\mu_{2}=\delta_{(s,s_{2})}
, which are the asymptotic limits of
\Sigma=sI_{n_{0}}
,
\Sigma^{*}_{1}=s_{1}I_{n_{0}}
, and
\Sigma^{*}_{2}=s_{2}I_{n_{0}}
. We assume
s_{1}\leq s_{2}
so that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
, as in the statement of
Proposition

5.7
. As an example, we focus on the activation function
\sigma(x)=x\exp(-x^{2})
, which was introduced in
Fig.

8
. By straightforward Gaussian integration, one can easily compute the scale-dependence of the quantities appearing in
Proposition

5.7
:

\displaystyle\eta_{1}

\displaystyle=\eta(s_{1})=\frac{s_{1}}{(1+4s_{1})^{3/2}}\,,\quad\zeta_{1}=\zeta(s_{1})=\frac{s_{1}}{(1+2s_{1})^{3}}\,,\quad\xi_{1}=\xi_{1}(s_{1})=\frac{(1+2s)^{3/2}}{(1+2s_{1})^{3/2}}\,,

(55)

\displaystyle\eta_{2}

\displaystyle=\eta(s_{2})=\frac{s_{2}}{(1+4s_{2})^{3/2}}\,,\quad\zeta_{2}=\zeta(s_{2})=\frac{s_{2}}{(1+2s_{2})^{3}}\,,\quad\xi_{2}=\xi_{2}(s_{2})=\frac{(1+2s)^{3/2}}{(1+2s_{2})^{3/2}}\,.

(56)

Figure 9
:
Predictions for the effect of pure-scale shifts on the total error, bias, and variance, where the shifted distribution is simply a scaled version of the identity, i.e.
\mu=\delta_{(s,s_{*})}
, with (
a
)
s=1
and (
b
)
s=1/250
, for
\gamma=10^{-3}
,
\phi=1/8
,
\phi/\psi=4
,
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=1
, and
\sigma(x)=x\exp(-x^{2})
. The model-dependent conditions in
Proposition

5.9
predict monotonicity of the bias, variance, and total error in the indicated shaded regions. Nonmonotonicity can be observed outside these regions, highlighting the importance of these conditions in
Proposition

5.9
. (
a
) For
s=1
, the regions of increasing bias and increasing variance do not overlap, and, as such, the region in between need not have increasing total error. (
b
) For
s=1/250
, the overlap of the regions of increasing bias and increasing variance extends from
s_{*}=1/250
to
s_{*}=1
, and the total error is observed to be increasing in this region.

We now examine how the scale-dependence of these quantities affects the bounds in
Proposition

5.9
. For the bias, the condition in
Eq.

29
,
\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1
, can be rewritten in this setting as

\displaystyle s\leq s_{1}\leq s_{2}\,.

(57)

All pairs of shift scales
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
satisfying
Eq.

57
will obey
B_{\mu_{1}}\leq B_{\mu_{2}}
, which implies that the bias corresponding to
\mu=\delta_{(s,s_{*})}
should be a nondecreasing function of the shift strength
s_{*}
, so long as
s\leq s_{*}
. The earlier discussion in
Section

7.3
explains this behavior. See
Fig.

9
for an illustration, which demonstrates how the bias may generally be nonmonotonic as a function of shift strength, but so long as
s_{*}\geq s
(to the right of the dashed orange lines), the bias is nondecreasing.

For the variance, the condition in
Eq.

30
,
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
, can be written in this setting as

\displaystyle s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\left(\sqrt{1+\frac{2}{s_{1}}}(1+2s_{1})-(3+2s_{1})\right)\,.

(58)

The maximum value of
s_{*}
for which all pairs of shift scales
s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq s_{*}
satisfy
Eq.

58
is given by
s_{*}=1/4
, which implies that the variance corresponding to
\mu=\delta_{(s,s_{*})}
should be a nondecreasing function of the shift strength
s_{*}
, so long as
s_{*}\leq 1/4
. This behavior can be seen in
Fig.

9
, which illustrates how the variance may generally be nonmonotonic as a function of shift strength, but so long as
s_{*}\leq 1/4
(to the left of the dashed green lines), the variance is nondecreasing.

Finally, by combining the above bounds for the bias and the variance, we see that the total error corresponding to
\mu=\delta_{(s,s_{*})}
should be a nondecreasing function of the shift strength
s_{*}
, so long as
s\leq s_{*}\leq 1/4
. If
s>1/4
, these conditions cannot be satisfied, which is the case in
Fig.

9
(a), which shows that the regimes for which the bias and the variance are nondecreasing do not overlap. Indeed, when
1/4<s_{*}<s=1
, the total error is seen to decrease as a function of
s_{*}
. Note that it is not strictly necessary for the bias and the variance to both be nondecreasing for the total error itself to be nondecreasing, since one term can dominate the other, and indeed
Fig.

9
shows this to be the case for large (and small)
s_{*}
.
Fig.

9
(b) illustrates the case
s=1/250
, which allows for a finite range of
s_{*}
(shaded in blue) satisfying
s\leq s_{*}\leq 1/4
. In this regime, both the bias and the variance are nondecreasing, and so the error must be as well, which is indeed the case in the figure.

7.4
Nonlinear trends with nonzero ridge constant or underparameterization

Figure 10
:
Predictions for shifted versus unshifted error for models with varying values of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
, obtained via
Theorem

5.3
and
Corollary

5.4
for the
(3,-1/2)
-diatomic LJSD with
\phi=0.5
,
\sigma=\text{ReLU}
, and
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=0.01
. (
a
) For small but finite regularization,
{\gamma=0.005}
, the relationship is close to linear in the overparameterized regime, but it is markedly nonlinear in the underparameterized regime, highlighting the importance of the condition
\phi>\psi
in
Proposition

5.13
. (
b
) The nonlinear relationship in the underparameterized persists in the ridgeless (
\gamma=0
) setting. (
c
) For nonzero ridge constant, the relationship is nonlinear in the overparameterized regime, but because the deviations from linearity tend to be small, they are not easily visible in (a), so here we plot the deviations
(aE_{\mu_{\emptyset}}+b)-E_{\mu}
directly for several values of
\gamma
. The constants
a
and
b
are chosen to fit a line through the two points corresponding to
\phi/\psi=1+2^{-16}
and
\phi/\psi=1+2^{16}
, which correspond to the points where the various curves cross the
x
-axis. For
\gamma=0
, there is no deviation from linearity, as predicted by
Proposition

5.13
, but small deviations are evident for nonzero
\gamma
.

The exact linear relationship between in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization characterized by
Proposition

5.13
in
Eq.

32
relies crucially on the overparameterization condition,
\psi<\phi
, as evidenced in
Fig.

10
(a,b), which show marked nonlinearity in the underparameterized regime. This observation is perhaps unsurprising, as severely underparameterized models tend towards chance predictions, which produce comparable errors on shifted and unshifted data. Indeed, similar nonlinear behavior is seen in the low-accuracy regime for realistic models
(
Recht et al. 2019
, Figure 17)
.
Fig.

10
(c) illustrates that the ridgeless condition in
Proposition

5.13
is necessary in order to achieve an exactly linear relationship. In this case, the deviations from linearity are quite small, so we plot the deviations
(aE_{\mu_{\emptyset}}+b)-E_{\mu}
, where the constants
a
and
b
are chosen to fit a line through the two points corresponding to
\phi/\psi=1+2^{-16}
and
\phi/\psi=1+2^{16}
. If the function were perfectly linear, there would be no deviations from this fit line; instead, we observe small deviations for
\gamma>0
. Interestingly, these deviations are quite small, suggesting an approximately linear relationship might hold more broadly than the narrow confines of
Proposition

5.13
.

8
Conclusion

Random feature models are a powerful and practically useful class of machine learning models that are analytically tractable in the high-dimensional limit. Because these models can exhibit rich phenomenology, they have emerged as fruitful null models to separate phenomena that are merely consequences of high-dimensional statistics from those that are unique to deep learning. In this work, we extended this line of research significantly by presenting exact results for the test error, bias, and variance for random feature regression in the high-dimensional limit under covariate shift. Our results enabled the exploration of many interesting phenomena that have been observed empirically for distribution shift.

We defined a partial order over covariate shifts (motivated by the setting of linear regression) to categorize the relative hardness of shifts. We found that when the shift does not change the covariate scale, the partial order provides a model-independent condition that determines when a shift will help or hurt performance. In contrast, when the shift does adjust the scale, its impact on performance is coupled to the model through a set of constants obtained from Gaussian integrals of the activation function. We saw that when there is a discrepancy between the scales of the training and test distributions, counterintuitive behavior can occur. For example, the bias can be highly nonmonotonic as a function of the number of random features or the amount of ridge regularization. Guided by our mathematical analysis, we developed a statistical explanation for many of these unexpected behaviors, including how they may be remedied or whether they remain when the regularization is tuned optimally.

In the simple context of random feature regression, our results capture many empirical phenomena that have been widely observed for realistic models and deep neural networks, suggesting that their occurrence is likely unrelated to deep learning per se, but rather a consequence of more prosaic factors such as high-dimensionality. One such important observation is that the conventional benefits of overparameterization, which exist in the absence of covariate shift, extend to settings with covariate shift in random feature regression. Additionally, our results predict a linear relationship (and the magnitude of its slope) between the generalization error on shifted and unshifted data in the ridgeless, overparameterized random feature model, matching the empirical phenomenology observed in broad generality in
Recht et al. 2018
;
Recht et al. 2019
;
Miller et al. 2020
;
Miller et al. 2021
.

There exist several opportunities for future work. Exploring covariate shift in models which incorporate more richly structured kernels such as the neural tangent kernel (see
Jacot et al. 2018
and
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
) and accommodating models with feature learning are interesting directions. Investigating the impact of covariate shift (and the relevance of our definition of shift strength) for other generative models or loss functions—such as logistic regression—is important as well. While we focused in this work on the generalization performance of random feature models on a
single fixed
test distribution, understanding the simultaneous generalization performance of a fixed predictor across several distinct groups of test data (i.e. the worst-case loss over several test distributions) is also of interest in practice
(
Sagawa et al. 2020
)
.

Appendix A
Useful inequalities

Here we include the statements and proofs of several auxiliary inequalities that we use throughout the Appendix.

A.1
Basic properties of the self-consistent equation for
x

We begin by establishing several basic inequalities. The definitions of the following quantities can be found in
Theorem

5.3
.

Lemma A.0
.

We have the following bounds:
\omega,\tau,\bar{\tau},x,\mathcal{I}_{a,b},\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}\geq 0
and
\tfrac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\leq 0
.

Proof.

As shown in
Pennington and Worah 2018
for the unit-variance case, a simple Hermite expansion argument establishes the relation
\eta\geq\zeta
, which implies
\omega=s(\eta/\zeta-1)\geq 0
. From
Eqs.

S418
and
S419
,
\tau
and
\bar{\tau}
are traces of positive semi-definite matrices and are therefore nonnegative. From the same equations, it follows that
x=\gamma\rho\tau\bar{\tau}\geq 0
. Nonnegativity of
x
implies
\mathcal{I}_{a,b}\geq 0
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}\geq 0
from their definitions in
Eq.

19
. Finally, using the nonnegativity of
\omega
,
\tau
,
\bar{\tau}
,
x
, and
\mathcal{I}_{a,b}
, the expression for
\tfrac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}
in
Theorem

5.3
immediately gives,

\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}=-\frac{x}{\gamma+\rho\gamma(\tfrac{\psi}{\phi}\tau+\bar{\tau})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})}\leq 0.

(S1)

∎

Next we show that the self-consistent equation
x=\tfrac{1-\gamma\tau}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}
appearing in
Theorem

5.3
and defined in
Eq.

S420
admits a unique positive real solution for
x
.

Lemma A.0
.

There is a unique real
x\geq 0
satisfying
x=\tfrac{1-\gamma\tau}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}
.

Proof.

Let
t=1/x\geq 0
and define

h(t)=t\left({\frac{\rho(\psi-\phi)+\sqrt{\rho^{2}(\psi-\phi)^{2}+4\gamma\rho\phi\psi/t}}{2\rho\psi}-1}\right)+\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}(1/t)\,,

(S2)

which is a rewriting of
Eq.

S420
. It suffices to show that
h
admits a unique real positive root. To that end, first observe that
\lim_{t\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{1,1}(1/t)=0
and
\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{1,1}(1/t)=s
, so that

\displaystyle h(0)=\omega>0\,\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{t\to\infty}h(t)/t=-\min\{1,\phi/\psi\}<0\,,

(S3)

which together imply that
h
has an odd number of positive real roots. Next, we show that
h
is concave for
t\geq 0
:

h^{\prime\prime}(t)=-\frac{2\phi}{t^{3}}\left({\frac{\gamma^{2}\rho\phi\psi}{(\rho^{2}(\psi-\phi)^{2}+4\gamma\rho\phi\psi/t)^{3/2}}+\mathcal{I}_{2,3}(1/t)}\right)\leq 0\,,

(S4)

which implies that
h
has at most two positive real roots. Therefore, we conclude that
h
has exactly one positive real root.
∎

A.2
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
inequalities

We now establish some useful properties of the
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}
functionals defined in
Eq.

19
. To begin, we note that simple algebraic manipulations establish the following raising and lowering identities:

\mathcal{I}_{a-1,b-1}=\phi\mathcal{I}_{a-1,b}+x\mathcal{I}_{a,b}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a-1,b-1}=\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a-1,b}+x\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}\,.

(S5)

Next, we consider how the partial order of LJSDs given in
Definition

4.1
leads to inequalities on the
\mathcal{I}^{*}
functionals. Letting
(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b})_{1}
and
(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b})_{2}
to denote the corresponding functionals with the LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
respectively, we can establish the following useful lemma.

Lemma A.0
.

Let
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
, so
\mu_{2}
is harder then
\mu_{1}
. Suppose the functions
f,g,h:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are such that
f(\lambda)=g(\lambda)h(\lambda)
,
g(\lambda)
is nonnegative, and
h(\lambda)
is nonincreasing for all
\lambda>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rf(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rf(\lambda)]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rg(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}.

(S6)

If instead
h(\lambda)
is nondecreasing for all
\lambda>0
, then

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rf(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rf(\lambda)]}\geq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rg(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}.

(S7)

Proof.

By the law of iterated expectation, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rf(\lambda)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]}h(\lambda)}\right].

(S8)

Note that the expectation
\mathbb{E}_{\lambda}
in
Eq.

S8
over
\lambda
is the same under
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
by assumption. Moreover, the function
h(\lambda)
is nonincreasing in
\lambda>0
by assumption. Finally, observe that the factor
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)|\lambda]}/{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}
defines a change in distribution for the random variable
\lambda
, since taking its expectation over
\lambda
yields 1. Denote a new random variable with this distribution by
\tilde{\lambda}
. Then, we may apply the Harris inequality
8
8

8

See
Grimmett 1999
for example.
to
Eq.

S8
to see

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rf(\lambda)]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\lambda}}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\tilde{\lambda}]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\tilde{\lambda}]}h(\tilde{\lambda})}\right]

(S9)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\lambda}}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\tilde{\lambda}]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\tilde{\lambda}]}}\right]\mathbb{E}_{\tilde{\lambda}}\left[{h(\tilde{\lambda})}\right]

(S10)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]\mathbb{E}_{{\lambda}}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|{\lambda}]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|{\lambda}]}}\right]\mathbb{E}_{{\lambda}}\left[{\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}h({\lambda})}\right]

(S11)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[rg(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[rg(\lambda)]}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}\left[{rf(\lambda)}\right]\,.

(S12)

∎

The following corollary is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

A.3
.

Corollary A.0
.

Let
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Then, for
a\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,a})_{2}}{s_{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,a})_{1}}{s_{1}}\geq 0

(S13)

Proof.

This result follows from
Lemma

A.3
by choosing
g:\lambda\mapsto 1
and
h:\lambda\mapsto\phi(\phi+x\lambda)^{-a}
and recalling by definitions
s_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]
and
s_{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]
.
∎

The argument from
Lemma

A.3
can be extended to cases where the shift is incomparable, i.e. when neither
\mu\leq\mu_{\emptyset}
not
\mu\geq\mu_{\emptyset}
, by introducing some monotonic functions that in some sense bound the shift-strength.

Lemma A.0
.

Let
\mu
be a LJSD and define the functions

L(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf_{0\leq\lambda\leq x}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}\quad\text{and}\quad U(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq x}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}.

(S14)

Then if
f
is a nonincreasing function, we have

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda L(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[rf(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda f(\lambda)]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[rf(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\emptyset}}[rf(\lambda)]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda U(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}.

(S15)

Proof.

Recall that the marginal of
\lambda
under both
\mu
and
\mu_{\emptyset}
is
\mu_{\text{train}}
, and that
(r|\lambda)=\lambda
almost surely under
\mu_{\emptyset}
, explaining the equality in
Eq.

S15
. Clearly,
L(\lambda)\leq{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}/{\lambda}\leq U(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\geq 0
, and
L(\lambda)
and
U(\lambda)
are nonincreasing and nondecreasing respectively. Next, we see

\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[rf(\lambda)]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\frac{\lambda}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}f(\lambda)}\right],

(S16)

so

\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\frac{\lambda}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}L(\lambda)f(\lambda)}\right]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[rf(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}\leq\left[{\frac{\lambda}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}U(\lambda)f(\lambda)}\right].

(S17)

Now we can apply the Harris inequality, as in the proof of
Lemma

A.3
, to see

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda L(\lambda)}\right]}{\left({\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}\right)^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{{\lambda}f(\lambda)}\right]\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[rf(\lambda)]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda U(\lambda)}\right]}{\left({\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}[\lambda]}\right)^{2}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{{\lambda}f(\lambda)}\right],

(S18)

which can be rearranged to give the result.
∎

Appendix B
Hardness is a partial order

We restate
Definition

4.1
for clarity.

Definition B.0

(Restatement of
Definition

4.1
)
.

Let
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
be LJSDs with the same marginal distribution of
\lambda
. If the asymptotic overlap coefficients are such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]
is nondecreasing as a function of
\lambda
and
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]}
, we say
\mu_{1}
is
easier
than
\mu_{2}
(or
\mu_{2}
is
harder
than
\mu_{1}
), and write
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Comparing against the case of no shift
\mu_{\emptyset}
, we say
\mu_{1}
is
easy
when
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{\emptyset}
and
hard
when
\mu_{1}\geq\mu_{\emptyset}
.

Proposition B.0
.

Definition

4.1
(and
Definition

B.1
) form a partial order over covariate shifts
\mu
.

Proof.

Reflexivity is clearly satisfied as
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]=1
is nondecreasing for all
\mu
.

For antisymmetry, we see
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{2}\leq\mu_{1}
imply
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]
and that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]
is constant in
\lambda
as it is nonincreasing and nondecreasing. However, setting
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]=c\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]
and taking expectation over
\lambda
and rearranging yields
1=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]=c
, so in fact
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]}
. Assuming that
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are absolutely continuous (the case where they are a sum of point masses is similar), we can write their densities as
p_{i}(\lambda,r)=p_{i}(\lambda)p_{i}(r|\lambda)
. By assumption
p_{1}(\lambda)=p_{2}(\lambda)
, so it suffices to show
p_{1}(r|\lambda)=p_{2}(r|\lambda)
almost everywhere. Next note

0=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{+}}r\left({p_{1}(r|\lambda)-p_{2}(r|\lambda)}\right)\text{d}r,

(S19)

but since
r>0
over the domain of the integral, we have
p_{1}(r|\lambda)-p_{2}(r|\lambda)=0
almost everywhere.

Finally, for transitivity assume
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{2}\leq\mu_{3}
, then clearly
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{3}}[r]
. Next note

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{3}}[r|\lambda]}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]}\cdot\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{3}}[r|\lambda]},

(S20)

so
{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]}/{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{3}}[r|\lambda]}
is the product of two nondecreasing, positive functions and is thus also nondecreasing.
∎

Appendix C
Repeated eigenvalues of
\Sigma

When
\Sigma
has repeated eigenvalues (denote one such by
\lambda
), its eigendecomposition is not unique, since the eigenvectors associated to
\lambda
need only span the eigenspace
\{\mathbf{v}:\Sigma\mathbf{v}=\lambda\mathbf{v}\}
. Specifically, if the eigenspace of
\lambda
has dimension
n_{\lambda}
then the eigenvectors
\mathbf{v}^{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{\lambda}_{n_{\lambda}}
are orthonormal but not unique. However, for any other choice of orthonormal vectors
\mathbf{w}^{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}^{\lambda}_{n_{\lambda}}
that span the eigenspace of
\lambda
, there exists some orthogonal matrix
O
such that
W=VO
, where
V
and
W
contain the two bases as their columns.

Let
\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{n_{0}}
and
\lambda_{1}^{*},\ldots,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{*}
denote a choice for the eigenvectors and eigenvalues of
\Sigma^{*}
. The nonuniqueness of the eigenvectors implies that the corresponding overlaps to
\Sigma^{*}
, defined as

r^{\lambda}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}(\mathbf{v}^{*}_{j}\cdot{\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}}^{\lambda})^{2}\lambda^{*}_{j}=(\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\lambda})^{\top}\Sigma^{*}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\lambda}

(S21)

are also not unique (but do not depend on the choice of eigendecomposition for
\Sigma^{*}
). However, we note that the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]
for the EJSD is invariant to the choice of eigendecomposition for
\Sigma
. Indeed, for
V
, we have

\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]=\frac{1}{n_{\lambda}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\lambda}}r^{\lambda}_{i}=\frac{1}{n_{\lambda}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\lambda}}(\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\lambda})^{\top}\Sigma^{*}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{\lambda}=\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(V^{\top}\Sigma^{*}V),

(S22)

but this is the same as
\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(W^{\top}\Sigma W)
using
W=VO
and the cyclic property of the trace.

Since
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]
under the EJSD is invariant to the choice of eigendecomposition for
\Sigma
, this will also be true of the LJSD. Said differently, while the choice of eigendecomposition affects both the EJSD and its corresponding LJSD, all possible choices of eigendecomposition lead to JSD in the same equivalence class, where
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are equivalent when
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r|\lambda]
.

Finally, since all potential EJSDs associated to
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{*}
are in the same equivalence class, the particular choice has no affect on their downstream use. Specifically,
Definition

4.1
is not changed as it depends only on
\mathbb{E}[r|\lambda]
, and none of the functionals of
\mu
(e.g.
\mathcal{I}_{a,b}
and
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}
) are changed due to the law of iterated expectation.

Appendix D
Test error for linear regression

D.1
Asymptotic and nonasymptotic results for
m>n_{0}+1

We present a short proof of the nonasymptotic test error for linear regression.

Recall data is generated via the model for
y_{i}=\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon_{i}
, which is fit with the ridgeless linear regression estimator
\hat{\beta}=(XX^{\top})^{-1}XY
. Although in the main text we have assumed the same isotropic prior on
\beta
that we use for the random feature model, no generative assumption is needed on
\beta
for this result.

Proof of
Proposition

3.1
.

Under the condition
m>n_{0}+1
, the sample covariance is almost surely invertible so the test error can be written as

\displaystyle E_{\Sigma^{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}}-\hat{\beta}^{\top}\mathbf{x}}\right)^{2}}\right]

(S23)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\Sigma^{*}\mathbb{E}[(XX^{\top})^{-1}]\right)

(S24)

\displaystyle=\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\frac{n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}\Sigma^{-1}),

(S25)

where we used the cyclicity and linearity of the trace, as well as a formula for the expectation of the inverse sample covariance of a Gaussian matrix (which applies when
m>n_{0}+1
) from
Rosen 1997
. The asymptotic form of the result follows from the limit in the proportional asymptotics:

E_{\mu}=\lim_{n_{0},m\to\infty}E_{\Sigma^{*}}=\frac{\sigma_{\epsilon}^{2}\phi}{1-\phi}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r/\lambda]\,.

(S26)

∎

Next we prove
Proposition

3.3
using the Harris inequality before proving a slightly more general result that properly handles the case where
\Sigma
may have repeated eigenvalues.

Proof of
Proposition

3.3
.

Using the Harris inequality,

E_{\Sigma^{*}_{1}}^{\text{LR}}=\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\frac{r_{i,2}}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{r_{i,1}}{r_{i,2}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}}\leq\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}n_{0}}{m-n_{0}-1}\frac{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\frac{r_{i,2}}{\lambda_{i}}\leq E_{\Sigma^{*}_{2}}^{\text{LR}},

(S27)

since
1/\lambda_{i}
and
{r_{i,1}}/{r_{i,2}}
are nonincreasing and nondecreasing in
i
respectively.
∎

Proposition

3.3
can be strengthened, and doing so motivates the occurrence of the conditional expectation in
Definition

4.1
. Note that we assume that the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
are in nondecreasing order, and each eigenvalue has associated to it two overlap coefficients,
r_{i,1}
and
r_{i,2}
.
Proposition

3.3
assumes that
r_{i,1}/r_{i,2}
form a nondecreasing sequence. However, what happens when
\Sigma
has repeated eigenvalues? In this case, the ordering of the
\lambda_{i}
can be changed, which in turn changes the associated
r_{i,1}
and
r_{i,2}
. Therefore, the assumption on
r_{i,1}/r_{i,2}
is too strong—reordering the repeated eigenvalues might be sufficient to satisfy the condition even if it is violated for the original ordering. Instead, we can introduce the conditional expectation to handle this more gracefully.

In the following, we use
\tilde{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{\lambda}_{k}
to denote the
k
non-repeated eigenvalues of the training covariance
\Sigma
and the sets
S_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
to denote the indices of eigenvalues in
\{1,\ldots,n_{0}\}
associated to the
j
th repeated eigenvalue of
\Sigma
. Analogously, we define the corresponding non-repeated overlap coefficients as,

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{j}=\frac{1}{\lvert S_{j}\rvert}\sum_{i\in S_{j}}r_{i},\quad j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}

(S28)

This is equivalent to the conditional expectation discussed in
Eq.

S22
. In this case, the measure-theoretic definition of
hardness
in
Definition

4.1
becomes equivalent to stating that the sequence
\tfrac{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}
is nondecreasing as
j
ranges from
\{1,\ldots,k\}
when
\Sigma^{*}_{2}
is
harder
then
\Sigma^{*}_{1}
(here
\tilde{r}_{j,1}
and
\tilde{r}_{j,2}
denote the non-repeated overlap coefficients of
\Sigma^{*}_{1}
and
\Sigma^{*}_{2}
respectively).

Proposition D.0
.

Let
\tilde{r}_{j,1}
and
\tilde{r}_{j,2}
denote the non-repeated overlap coefficients
9
9

9

Recall the definition in
Eq.

S28
.
of
\Sigma^{*}_{1}
and
\Sigma^{*}_{2}
relative to
\Sigma
. If
\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})\geq\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})
and the ratios
{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}/{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}
form a nondecreasing sequence, then
E_{\Sigma^{*}_{2}}^{\text{LR}}\geq E_{\Sigma^{*}_{1}}^{\text{LR}}

Proof.

From
Proposition

3.1
it is enough to show
\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2}\Sigma^{-1})\geq\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1}\Sigma^{-1})
. First, note that
\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}r_{i,1}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert S_{j}\rvert\tilde{r}_{j,1}=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}r_{i,2}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert S_{j}\rvert\tilde{r}_{j,2}=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1}\Sigma^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\frac{r_{i,1}}{\lambda_{i}}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert S_{j}\rvert\frac{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}=\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert S_{j}\rvert\frac{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}\frac{1}{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}

(S29)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert S_{j}\rvert\frac{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lvert S_{j}\rvert\frac{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\frac{1}{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}\right)

(S30)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{1})}{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2})}\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2}\Sigma^{-1})

(S31)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*}_{2}\Sigma^{-1}),

(S32)

where the inequality is a consequence of the Harris inequality
(
Grimmett 1999
, Section 2.2)
: since
\lvert S_{j}\rvert{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}/{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}
is a normalized measured with respect to
j
, the sequence
{1}/{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}}
is nonincreasing in
j
, while the sequence
{\tilde{r}_{j,1}}/{\tilde{r}_{j,2}}
is nondecreasing in
j
.
∎

D.2
Linear regression limit of random feature regression

In this section, we show that taking appropriate limits of
Corollary

5.4
and
Corollary

5.5
recovers existing results for linear regression in high-dimensions. We first examine the ridgeless limit, for which the results fall into two cases:
\phi<1
, which we discuss in
Section

D.2.1
and connect to
Proposition

3.1
from the main text; and
\phi>1
, which we discuss in
Section

D.2.2
and connect to
Hastie et al. 2019
. We then consider non-zero regularization, and in
Section

D.2.3
we connect
Corollary

5.5
to
Wu and Xu 2020
.

To recover these results, we let
\sigma
approach the identity activation function and
\psi\to 0
10
10

10

The order of these limits does not change the result, but for concreteness we take take the limit as
\psi\to 0
first.
, since as more random features are added the kernel concentrate around its limit. Moreover, since we are taking the limit
\psi\to 0
and we assume
\phi>0
, we may assume
\phi>\psi
in all calculations for simplicity. We also note that as
\sigma
approaches the identity, the constants associated to
\sigma
approach the following limits:

\eta,\zeta,\eta_{*},\zeta_{*},\xi\to 1\quad\text{and}\quad\omega,\omega_{*}\to 0.

(S33)

D.2.1
Recovering asymptotic form of
Proposition

3.1
for
\phi<1

From
Corollary

5.4
, the expression for the total error is

\displaystyle E_{\mu}

\displaystyle=(1-\xi)^{2}s_{*}+2(1-\xi)\xi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+\xi^{2}\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}+\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{\phi-\psi}x(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})

(S34)

\displaystyle\qquad+\xi^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2},

(S35)

where
x=1/(\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})
since we are assuming
\psi<\phi
. Taking the limit
\psi\to 0
, the total error
E_{\mu}
converges to

(1-\xi)^{2}s_{*}+2(1-\xi)\xi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+\xi^{2}\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}+\xi^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}.

(S36)

Recall that
Proposition

3.1
assumes that
\phi<1
. We begin by analyzing the solution to the self-consistent equation for
x
in the ridgeless limit when the activation function
\sigma
becomes linear.

Lemma D.0
.

Suppose
0<\phi<1
and
\psi<\phi
. In the ridgeless limit, the solution
x
to the self-consistent equation in
Corollary

5.4
,

x=\frac{1}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}\,,

(S37)

satisfies
\lim_{\omega\to 0}x=\infty
.

Proof.

From the definition of
\mathcal{I}_{1,1}
, we see

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}=\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\frac{\lambda}{\phi+\lambda x}\right]=\frac{\phi}{x}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[\frac{\lambda}{\phi/x+\lambda}\right]\leq\frac{\phi}{x}.

(S38)

Using
Eq.

S37
, we find
x\geq({1-\phi})/{\omega}
. Taking
\omega\to 0
completes the proof.
∎

From
Lemma

D.2
, the solution to the self-consistent equation for
x
diverges, i.e.
x\to\infty
as
\sigma
becomes linear. In this case, by the dominated convergence theorem, we have that

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\to 0}x\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{\frac{xr}{\phi+x\lambda}}\right]=\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{r/\lambda}\right],

(S39)

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\to 0}x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{\frac{x^{2}\lambda^{2}}{(\phi+x\lambda)^{2}}}\right]=\phi,

(S40)

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\to 0}x^{2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{\frac{x^{2}r\lambda}{(\phi+x\lambda)^{2}}}\right]=\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{r/\lambda}\right],

(S41)

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\to 0}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{\frac{r}{(\phi+x\lambda)^{2}}}\right]=0,

(S42)

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\lim_{\omega\to 0}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to\infty}\phi\mathbb{E}_{\mu}\left[{\frac{r}{(\phi+x\lambda)^{2}}}\right]=0\,.

(S43)

As such, the total error in
Eq.

S36
converges to

\displaystyle\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{\phi}{1-\phi}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r/\lambda]

(S44)

as
\omega\to 0
and
\psi\to 0
, as desired.

D.2.2
Recovering results from
Hastie et al. 2019
when
\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
and
\phi>1

The minimum-norm solution for under-determined linear regression is the same as the limiting ridge-regularized solution as the ridge constant converges to 0. As such, studying the ridgeless limit as in
Corollary

5.4
allows for a comparison to prior results on minimum-norm interpolants
(
Hastie et al. 2019
)
. To do so, we again take the limit as
\sigma
becomes linear; however, in contrast to the previous section, we now assume
\phi>1
in order to compare with
Hastie et al. 2019
.

As
Hastie et al. 2019
examines the setting in which the training and test covariate distributions are equal,
\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
, we have that
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}=\mathcal{I}_{a,b}
. Using this relation, the total error
Eq.

S36
becomes

E_{\mu}=(1-\xi)^{2}s+2(1-\xi)\xi\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\xi^{2}\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}+\xi^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)\mathcal{I}_{2,2}.

(S45)

Next, letting
\sigma
become linear as in
Eq.

S33
, we obtain
x={1}/{\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}
and

\displaystyle E_{\mu}\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\omega\to 0}}{{\longrightarrow}}\,\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}},

(S46)

where we used the identity
Eq.

S5
.

We now simplify and relate the result for the limiting error from
Hastie et al. 2019
in the case of isotropic
\beta
satisfying
\lVert\beta\rVert_{2}=1
, where the total error is written as
11
11

11

Hastie et al. 2019
provides expressions for a bias and variance term separately, but the decomposition is defined slightly differently than the one we use, so we compare directly to the total error. Note that
Eq.

S47
corrects a typo in
Hastie et al. 2019
.

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi^{2}c_{0}(H,\phi)}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}c_{0}\phi\frac{\int{\frac{s^{2}}{(1+c_{0}\phi s)^{2}dH(s)}}}{\int{\frac{s}{(1+c_{0}\phi s)^{2}dH(s)}}}\,.

(S47)

The measure
H
is the limiting empirical spectral density of the covariance, which is equivalent to the marginal distribution of
\lambda
, and
c_{0}
satisfies the equation

1-\frac{1}{\phi}=\int{\frac{1}{1+c_{0}\phi s}dH(s)}.

(S48)

Substituting
c_{0}=x/\phi^{2}
and rearranging terms, we see

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\phi}-\int{\frac{1}{1+c_{0}\phi s}dH(s)}

(S49)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\phi}+1-\int{\frac{1}{1+(x/\phi)s}dH(s)}

(S50)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\phi}+x\int{\frac{s}{\phi+xs}dH(s)}

(S51)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{\phi}\big(\frac{1}{x}-\mathcal{I}_{1,1}\big),

(S52)

which is satisfied by
x=1/\mathcal{I}_{1,1}
, implying the two self-consistent equations are equivalent and validating the identification
c_{0}=x/\phi^{2}
. Using the same substitution
c_{0}=x/\phi^{2}
in
Eq.

S47
gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{1}{x\phi}\frac{x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}{\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}=\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\frac{x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}},

(S53)

which matches our expression in
Eq.

S46
.

D.2.3
Recovering results from
Wu and Xu 2020
when
\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
and
\gamma>0

Viewing the expressions in
Corollary

5.5
as functions of
\gamma
and
\omega
, the total error satisfies the self-consistency relation
E_{\mu}(\gamma,\omega)=E_{\mu}(\gamma_{\text{eff}},0)
, i.e. the error with regularizer
\gamma
and nonlinearity coefficient
\omega
(from
Eq.

17
) is identical to the error with regularizer
\gamma_{\text{eff}}
and nonlinearity constant
\omega=0
(achieved by taking a linear activation function). Given this relationship, we seek to connect
Corollary

5.5
with the results of
Wu and Xu 2020
for linear ridge regression, in the case where
\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
and their underlying
\beta
has an isotropic prior and their ridge-weighting matrix is the identity. This connection establishes that the total test error in
Corollary

5.5
with regularizer
\gamma
and nonlinearity constant
\omega
matches the total test error of linear ridge regression with regularizer
\gamma_{\text{eff}}
.

To begin, recall in the case that
\Sigma=\Sigma^{*}
,
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}=\mathcal{I}_{a,b}
and
\xi=1
. In this case, our total test error can be written as:

\displaystyle E_{\mu}=\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}\cdot x\mathcal{I}_{2,2}.

(S54)

with
x=1/(\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})
.

Simplified to the aforementioned setting, the results of
Wu and Xu 2020
can be written as
12
12

12

Note this expression subtracts the contribution of the irreducible label noise on the test point to match our definition of the test error. Additionally, the generative model in
Wu and Xu 2020
takes the form
y(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}/\sqrt{m}+\varepsilon_{i}
instead of the convention
y(\mathbf{x}_{i})=\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}_{i}/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon_{i}
we take. Hence the term
\int{\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda m(-\tilde{\gamma})+1)^{2}}dH(\lambda)}
is rescaled by a relative factor of
1/\phi
relative to the comparable term in
Wu and Xu 2020
.
,

\displaystyle E^{\text{LR}}=\frac{m^{\prime}(-\tilde{\gamma})}{m(-\tilde{\gamma})^{2}}\cdot\left(\int{\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda m(-\tilde{\gamma})+1)^{2}}dH(\lambda)}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}

(S55)

where measure
H
is the limiting empirical spectral density of the covariance, which is equivalent to the marginal distribution of
\lambda
. Additionally,
m(z)
(the Stieltjes transform of the limiting spectral density corresponding to the Gram matrix
X^{\top}X/m
) and its derivative are given by
Wu and Xu 2020
as,

\displaystyle\tilde{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m(-\tilde{\gamma})}-\phi\int{\frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda m(-\tilde{\gamma})}dH(\lambda)}

(S56)

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{m^{2}(-\tilde{\gamma})}-\phi\int{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\lambda\cdot m(-\tilde{\gamma})+1)^{2}}dH(\lambda)}\right)m^{\prime}(-\tilde{\gamma})\,,

(S57)

which can be rearranged to read,

\displaystyle\phi m(-\tilde{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\tilde{\gamma}/\phi+\phi\int{\frac{\lambda}{\phi+\lambda\cdot\phi m(-\tilde{\gamma})}dH(\lambda)}}

(S58)

\displaystyle\phi^{2}m^{\prime}(-\tilde{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\phi^{2}m^{2}(-\tilde{\gamma})}-\phi\int{\frac{\lambda^{2}}{(\lambda\cdot\phi m(-\tilde{\gamma})+\phi)^{2}}dH(\lambda)}}\,.

(S59)

Whereas we define the Gram matrix as
X^{\top}X/n_{0}
, it is defined as
X^{\top}X/m
in
Wu and Xu 2020
, leading to some normalization differences. To resolve these discrepancies, it is necessary to identify
m(-\tilde{\gamma})=x/\phi
and
\tilde{\gamma}=\phi\gamma_{\text{eff}}
in order for the regression vector
\hat{\beta}
to be consistent between the two conventions. Under these identifications,
Eq.

S58
can be written as,

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}\,,

(S60)

which agrees with the expression in
Corollary

5.5
, and
Eq.

S59
can be written as,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma_{\text{eff}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1/x^{2}-\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}

(S61)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{1/x-\mathcal{I}_{1,1}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}

(S62)

\displaystyle=-\frac{x}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}\,,

(S63)

which, using
\tfrac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma_{\text{eff}}}=\rho\tfrac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}
agrees with the expression in
Eq.

S823
. Next, we use these identifications to simplify the expression for the test error in
Eq.

S57
,

\displaystyle E^{\text{LR}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\phi^{2}m^{\prime}(-\tilde{\gamma})}{(\phi m(-\tilde{\gamma}))^{2}}\cdot\left(\phi^{2}\int{\frac{\lambda}{(\lambda\cdot\phi m(-\tilde{\gamma})+\phi)^{2}}dH(\lambda)}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}

(S64)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\cdot\left(\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\,.

(S65)

Invoking the identity
\frac{1}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}=\frac{1/x}{1/x-x\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}=\frac{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}
, which follows from
Eq.

S5
and
Eq.

S60
, we can rewrite the error as,

\displaystyle E^{\text{LR}}=\frac{\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}\cdot(\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\cdot(\mathcal{I}_{1,1}-\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}\,.

(S66)

Finally, we invoke
Eq.

S5
and
Eq.

S60
once more to obtain,

\displaystyle E^{\text{LR}}=\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}\cdot\frac{x\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}+\frac{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}x\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}\,,

(S67)

which matches the expression in
Eq.

S54
, confirming the equivalence of the two expressions.

Appendix E
Harder shifts increase the bias and the total error

To begin, we state
Proposition

5.7
in the general setting where the scales in
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
need not match. In this setting, the marginals of the two test distribution may have two scales
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
distinct from the scale of the training covariance
s
, and the parameters in
Theorem

5.3
are subscripted by a
1
or
2
to indicate it whether they are induced by
\mu_{1}
or
\mu_{2}
. As discussed in
Section

4
and
Section

7
, additional constraints on these parameters are necessary to guarantee an ordering of the bias and error.

Proposition E.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

5.7
)
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
. Then, in the setting of
Theorem

5.3
,

\displaystyle B_{\mu_{1}}\leq B_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1

(S68)

\displaystyle E_{\mu_{1}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega\,.

(S69)

Proof of
Proposition

5.7
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
, and assume
\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1
. Then,

\displaystyle B_{\mu_{2}}-B_{\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=(1-\xi_{2})^{2}s_{2}+2(1-\xi_{2})\xi_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\phi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}

(S70)

\displaystyle\qquad-(1-\xi_{1})^{2}s_{1}-2(1-\xi_{1})\xi_{1}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}-\xi_{1}^{2}\phi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}

(S71)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\xi_{2})^{2}s_{2}+2(1-\xi_{2})\xi_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}\phi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}

(S72)

\displaystyle\qquad-(1-\xi_{1})^{2}s_{2}-2(1-\xi_{1})\xi_{1}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}-\xi_{1}^{2}\phi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}

(S73)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}\left[r\bigg((1-\xi_{2})+\xi_{2}\frac{\phi}{\phi+x\lambda}\bigg)^{2}\right]-\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}\left[r\bigg((1-\xi_{1})+\xi_{1}\frac{\phi}{\phi+x\lambda}\bigg)^{2}\right]

(S74)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}\left[r\left(\bigg((1-\xi_{2})+\xi_{2}\frac{\phi}{\phi+x\lambda}\bigg)^{2}-\bigg((1-\xi_{1})+\xi_{1}\frac{\phi}{\phi+x\lambda}\bigg)^{2}\right)\right]

(S75)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}\left[r\left((\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\Big(2-(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})\frac{x\lambda}{\phi+x\lambda}\Big)\frac{x\lambda}{\phi+x\lambda}\right)\right]

(S76)

\displaystyle\geq 0\,,

(S77)

where we have again used
Lemma

A.1
and the inequalities in
Eq.

S107
which follow from
Corollary

A.4
.

Now, additionally assume that
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
and
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
. Note that the latter condition implies
s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\geq s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}
and
{\omega}_{2}/s_{2}\geq{\omega}_{1}/s_{1}
. Reorganizing the terms specifying the limiting variance, we can rewrite the total error as,

\displaystyle E_{\mu}

\displaystyle=B_{\mu}+V_{\mu}

(S78)

\displaystyle=(1-\xi)^{2}s_{*}+2(1-\xi)\xi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\xi^{2}\phi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})

\displaystyle\quad-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\bigg((\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}(\omega_{*}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\frac{\phi}{\psi x}\gamma\bar{\tau}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}-\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})\bigg)

(S79)

\displaystyle\equiv(1-\xi)^{2}s_{*}+2(1-\xi)\xi(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\xi^{2}\big(C_{1}\omega_{*}+C_{2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+C_{3}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}\big)\,,

(S80)

where the
C_{i}\geq 0
and depend on
\mu
only through the marginal
\lambda
(i.e. they only depend on the training distribution):

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=-\rho\frac{\psi}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\big((\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})+\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}\big)\geq 0

(S81)

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\left(\frac{\psi}{\phi}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})+\frac{\gamma\bar{\tau}}{x}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})\right)\geq 0

(S82)

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=-\rho\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\left(\psi\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}-\frac{\phi\gamma\bar{\tau}}{x}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})-\frac{\phi}{\rho\tfrac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}}\right)

(S83)

\displaystyle=-\rho\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\left(\psi\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}-\frac{\phi\gamma\bar{\tau}}{x}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})+\frac{\phi}{\rho x}(\gamma+\rho\gamma(\tau\psi/\phi+\bar{\tau})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}))\right)

(S84)

\displaystyle=-\rho\gamma\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\left(\psi\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}+\frac{\phi\bar{\tau}}{x}(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})+\frac{\phi}{\rho x}\big(1+\rho\tau\frac{\psi}{\phi}(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})\big)\right)

(S85)

\displaystyle\geq 0\,.

(S86)

where the inequalities follow from
Lemma

A.1
and from the assumption
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
. It is now straightforward to see that,

\displaystyle E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=(1-\xi_{2})^{2}s_{2}+2(1-\xi_{2})\xi_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}-(1-\xi_{1})^{2}s_{1}-2(1-\xi_{1})\xi_{1}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}

(S87)

\displaystyle\quad+\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\omega_{2}+C_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}+C_{3}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}\big)-\xi_{1}^{2}\big(C_{1}\omega_{1}+C_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}+C_{3}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}\big)

\displaystyle\geq(1-\xi_{2})^{2}s_{2}+2(1-\xi_{2})\xi_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}-(1-\xi_{1})^{2}s_{2}-2(1-\xi_{1})\xi_{1}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}

(S88)

\displaystyle\quad+\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\omega_{2}+C_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}+C_{3}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}\big)-\xi_{1}^{2}\big(C_{1}\omega_{1}+C_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}+C_{3}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}\big)

\displaystyle=(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\big((2-\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})s_{2}-2(1-\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}\big)

(S89)

\displaystyle\quad+s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}\big)-s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}\big(C_{1}\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}+C_{3}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})\big((2-\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}-2(1-\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}\big)

(S90)

\displaystyle\quad+s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}\big)-s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}+C_{3}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq(\xi_{1}-\xi_{2})(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2})(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}

(S91)

\displaystyle\quad+s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}\big)-s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\big(C_{1}\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq 0\,,

(S92)

where we have used the inequalities

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}\geq 0\,,\quad\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}\geq 0\,,\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}\leq s_{2}\,.

(S93)

The former two inequalities again follow from
Corollary

A.4
.
∎

E.1
Pure-scale shifts

Proposition E.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

5.9
)
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]
, i.e. a
pure-scale shift
. Then, in the setting of
Theorem

5.3
,

\displaystyle B_{\mu_{1}}\leq B_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1

(S94)

\displaystyle V_{\mu_{1}}\leq V_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}

(S95)

\displaystyle E_{\mu_{1}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{if}\quad\xi_{2}\leq\xi_{1}\leq 1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega\,.

(S96)

The result for the bias and total error follows immediately from
Proposition

5.7
. For the variance, we can reorganize the terms specifying the limiting variance as,

Proof.

\displaystyle V_{\mu}

\displaystyle=-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\bigg((\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+\frac{\phi}{\psi}\gamma\bar{\tau}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}(\omega_{*}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})\bigg)

(S97)

\displaystyle\equiv C_{1}(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})+C_{2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}+C_{3}(\omega_{*}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})\,,

(S98)

where the
C_{i}\geq 0
and depend on
\mu
only through the marginal
\lambda
(i.e. they only depend on the training distribution):

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})\geq 0

(S99)

\displaystyle C_{2}

\displaystyle=-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\gamma\bar{\tau}(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})\geq 0

(S100)

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=-\rho\xi^{2}\frac{\psi}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\gamma\tau\mathcal{I}_{2,2}\geq 0\,,

(S101)

where the inequalities follow directly from
Lemma

A.1
. From this expression, it is now straightforward to see that,

\displaystyle V_{\mu_{2}}-V_{\mu_{1}}

\displaystyle=\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2})+C_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}+C_{2}(\omega_{2}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-\xi_{1}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1})+C_{2}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{1}+C_{3}(\omega_{1}+\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}\right)

(S102)

\displaystyle=s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}})+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\phi\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}})+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}+C_{3}(\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+\phi\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}}{s_{1}})\right)

(S103)

\displaystyle\geq s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}})+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\phi\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}})+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}+C_{3}(\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+\phi\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}}{s_{1}})\right)

(S104)

\displaystyle\geq s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}})+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\phi\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad-s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{1}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}})+C_{2}\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}+C_{3}(\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\phi\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}})\right)

(S105)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(S106)

where we have used the inequalities

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}\geq 0\,,\qquad\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}\geq 0\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{2}}{s_{2}}-\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2})_{1}}{s_{1}}\geq 0\,,

(S107)

which are immediate consequences of
Corollary

A.4
and the assumption
\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r|\lambda]=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{1}}[r]/\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{2}}[r]-s_{1}/s_{2}
.
∎

E.2
Incomparable shifts of equal scale

In this section, we state a bound on the bias and total error of shifts that are incomparable to the null shift
\mu_{\emptyset}
in terms of the in-distribution bias and error.

Proposition E.0
.

Let
\mu
be a LJSD such that
\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[\lambda]=s=s_{*}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r]
, and define the functions

L(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\inf_{0\leq\lambda\leq x}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}\quad\text{and}\quad U(x)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sup_{0\leq\lambda\leq x}\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[r|\lambda]}{\lambda}.

(S108)

Denote the bias and total error of
\mu
by
B_{\mu}
and
E_{\mu}
respectively, then

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda L(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}B_{\mu_{\emptyset}}\leq B_{\mu}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda U(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}B_{\mu_{\emptyset}},

(S109)

and further if
\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq\omega
, then

\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda L(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}\leq E_{\mu}\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda U(\lambda)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\text{train}}}\left[{\lambda}\right]}E_{\mu_{\emptyset}}.

(S110)

Proof.

Since
s=s_{*}
,
\xi=1
. Thus, recall
B_{\mu}=\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}
. Note that the function
\lambda\mapsto(\phi+x\lambda)^{-2}
is nonincreasing, thus
Lemma

A.5
implies (
S109
), since
B_{\mu_{\emptyset}}=\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}
.

For the total error, recall from Eqs. (
S81
)-(
S86
) that
E_{\mu}=C_{1}\omega+C_{2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+C_{3}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}
, where the coefficients
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
are nonnegative. Moreover, these coefficients are the same for
\mu_{\emptyset}
as
s=s_{*}
. Finally, we can again apply
Lemma

A.5
as the functions
\lambda\mapsto(\phi+x\lambda)^{-1}
and
\lambda\mapsto(\phi+x\lambda)^{-2}
are nonincreasing, to find
Eq.

S110
.
∎

Appendix F
The benefit of overparameterization

F.1
The bias is nonincreasing

We begin by examining the behavior of the bias as a function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
in a more general setting than discussed in the main text. In particular, we relax
4
and allow for unequal scales,
s\neq s_{*}
. As discussed in
Section

7
, monotonicity of the bias is not guaranteed if
\xi>1
. As such, we adopt the additional condition
\xi\leq 1
.

Proposition F.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

5.10
)
.

In the setting of
Theorem

5.3
, if
\xi\leq 1
, the bias
