B_{\mu}
is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
.

Proof of
Proposition

F.1
.

Recall from
Theorem

5.3
that the bias is given by

B_{\mu}=(1-\xi)^{2}s_{*}+2(1-\xi)\xi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+\xi^{2}\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,2}\,,

(S111)

where
x
is the unique positive real root of the self-consistent equation,

x=\frac{1-\gamma\tau}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}\,.

(S112)

Differentiating
Eq.

S111
with respect to
\phi/\psi
gives,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial B_{\mu}}{\partial{(\phi/\psi)}}=-\frac{\psi^{2}}{\phi}\frac{\partial B_{\mu}}{\partial{\psi}}=2\frac{\psi^{2}}{\phi}\frac{\partial x}{\partial\psi}\cdot\left((1-\xi)\xi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}+\xi^{2}\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,3}\right)\,.

(S113)

Since by assumption
\xi\leq 1
, and
Lemma

A.1
gives
\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b}\geq 0
, it is sufficient to show
\frac{\partial x}{\partial{\psi}}\leq 0
, which immediately follows by implicitly differentiating
Eq.

S112
and simplifying the expression:

\frac{\partial x}{\partial\psi}=-\frac{\rho x\tau(\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})}{\phi\big(1+\rho(\bar{\tau}+\frac{\psi}{\phi}\tau)(\omega+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2})\big)}\leq 0\,.

(S114)

Therefore we conclude that
\frac{\partial B_{\mu}}{\partial{(\phi/\psi)}}\leq 0
.
∎

F.2
The variance is nonincreasing

Next, we turn our attention to the variance. We note that
Proposition

5.11
does not rely on
4
: the variance is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
whenever
\psi<\phi
, regardless of the magnitude of the overall covariance scales
s
and
s_{*}
. However, the proposition only focuses on the ridgeless limit, whereas

Fig.

1
(d) and
Fig.

8
(a) show that result may in fact hold for nonzero ridge constant. As the proof is considerably simpler in the ridgeless limit, we defer the analysis of the nonzero ridge setting to future work.

Proposition F.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

5.11
)
.

In the setting of
Corollary

5.4
and in the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\psi<\phi
), the variance
V_{\mu}
is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.11
.

Using the chain rule we have that,
\frac{\partial V_{\mu}}{\partial{(\phi/\psi)}}=\frac{\partial V_{\mu}}{\partial{\psi}}\left[\frac{\partial(\phi/\psi)}{\partial\psi}\right]^{-1}=-\frac{\partial V_{\mu}}{\partial{\psi}}\frac{\psi^{2}}{\phi}
, so it is sufficient to show that
\frac{\partial V_{\mu}}{\partial{\psi}}\geq 0
.

From
Corollary

5.4
in the overparameterized regime, the self-consistent equation for
x
reads
x=\tfrac{1}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}
and is independent of
\psi
. Therefore,
\partial x/\partial\psi=0
and the expression for
\partial V_{\mu}/\partial\psi
follows directly from
Eq.

25
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial V_{\mu}}{\partial\psi}=\xi^{2}\frac{\phi}{(\phi-\psi)^{2}}x(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega_{*}+\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})\geq 0\,,

(S115)

where the inequality follows from
Lemma

A.1
.
∎

F.3
The generalization gap is nonincreasing

Finally, we prove that overparameterization also confers enhanced robustness, specifically that the generalization gap between shifted and unshifted test error is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio in the overparameterized regime.

Proposition F.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

5.12
)
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
such that
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
. Then, in the setting of
Corollary

5.4
and in the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\psi<\phi
), the generalization gap
E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}}
is a nonincreasing function of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.12
.

The chain rule gives
\frac{\partial(E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}})}{\partial(\phi/\psi)}=-\frac{\psi^{2}}{\phi}\frac{\partial(E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}})}{\partial\psi}
so it is sufficient to show
{\frac{\partial(E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}})}{\partial\psi}\geq 0}
. To that end, recall from
Corollary

5.4
in the overparameterized regime that the self-consistent equation for
x
reads
x=\tfrac{1}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}
and is independent of
\psi
. Therefore,
\partial x/\partial\psi=0
, which implies
\partial B_{\mu_{1}}/\partial\psi=0
and
\partial B_{\mu_{2}}/\partial\psi=0
, so that
\frac{\partial(E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}})}{\partial\psi}=\frac{\partial(V_{\mu_{2}}-V_{\mu_{1}})}{\partial\psi}
, the expression for which follows directly from
Eq.

25
. Finally, note that, as in
Appendix

E
, the conditions
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
imply
s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\geq s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}
and
{\omega}_{2}/s_{2}\geq{\omega}_{1}/s_{1}
, so we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial(E_{\mu_{2}}-E_{\mu_{1}})}{\partial\psi}

\displaystyle=\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{E}(\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2})\right)-\xi_{1}^{2}\left(C_{E}(\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}\right)

(S116)

\displaystyle=s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{E}\left({\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}}\right)\right)-s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}\left(C_{E}\left({\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}}\right)\right)

(S117)

\displaystyle\geq s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{E}\left({\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}}\right)\right)-s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{E}\left({\frac{\omega_{1}}{s_{1}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}}\right)\right)

(S118)

\displaystyle\geq s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{E}\left({\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}}\right)\right)-s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\left(C_{E}\left({\frac{\omega_{2}}{s_{2}}+\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}}\right)\right)

(S119)

\displaystyle=0\,,

(S120)

where we have introduced the shorthand
C_{E}=\frac{\phi}{(\phi-\psi)^{2}}x(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})\geq 0
and in the last inequality we have used
Corollary

A.4
to argue
\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{s_{2}}\geq\frac{(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}{s_{1}}
.
∎

Appendix G
Linear trends between in-distribution and out-of-distribution generalization

In
Section

5.4
, we investigated the linear relationship between the shifted and unshifted test error in the ridgeless, overparameterized regime, arriving at
Proposition

5.13
. Here we provide a short proof of that proposition, which we restate for clarity.

Proposition G.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

5.13
)
.

Consider two LJSDs
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
. In the setting of
Corollary

5.4
and in the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\psi<\phi
),
E_{\mu_{2}}
is linear in
E_{\mu_{1}}
, parametrically in
\phi/\psi
:

E_{\mu_{2}}=E_{0}+\underbrace{\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}}\left({\frac{\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}}\right)}_{\textsc{slope}}E_{\mu_{1}}\,,

(S121)

where
E_{0}
and
\textsc{slope}\geq 0
are constants independent of the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
. Moreover, if
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}
, then
\textsc{slope}\geq 1
.

Proof of
Proposition

5.13
.

In the overparameterized regime (i.e.
\phi>\psi
) and in the ridgeless limit, the limiting test error for LJSD
\mu_{1}
is given by
Corollary

5.4
as

E_{\mu_{1}}=B_{\mu_{1}}+\xi_{1}^{2}\frac{1}{\phi/\psi-1}x(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1})+\xi_{1}^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{1}\,.

(S122)

Similarly, the limiting test error for LJSD
\mu_{2}
is given by
Corollary

5.4
as

E_{\mu_{2}}=B_{\mu_{2}}+\xi_{2}^{2}\frac{1}{\phi/\psi-1}x(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1})(\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2})+\xi_{2}^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}\,.

(S123)

Note that in both expressions, the self-consistent equation for
x
reads
x=\frac{1}{\omega+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}
, which has no dependence on
\psi
, and, as such,
x
,
\mathcal{I}_{a,b}
,
(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b})_{1}
,
(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{a,b})_{2}
,
B_{\mu_{1}}
, and
B_{\mu_{2}}
do not depend on the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
. Hence we can simply eliminate the quantity
\tfrac{1}{\phi/\psi-1}
from
Eqs.

S122
and
S123
to obtain

E_{\mu_{2}}=E_{0}+\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}}\Big(\frac{\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}\Big)E_{\mu_{1}}\,,

(S124)

where
E_{0}
does not depend on the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
and is given by

\begin{split}E_{0}&\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=B_{\mu_{2}}-\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}}\frac{\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}B_{\mu_{1}}+\xi_{2}^{2}x\Big(1-\frac{x(\omega-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})}{1-x^{2}\mathcal{I}_{2,2}}\Big)\Big((\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{2}-\frac{\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2})_{1}\Big)\,.\end{split}

(S125)

The fact that
\textsc{slope}\geq 0
follows immediately from the expression and the inequalities in
Lemma

A.1
. To establish the final conclusion, recall from
Appendix

E
that the conditions
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
and
{1\leq\zeta_{2}/\zeta_{1}\leq\eta_{2}/\eta_{1}}
imply
s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\geq s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}
and
{\omega}_{2}/s_{2}\geq{\omega}_{1}/s_{1}
, so we have

slope

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi_{2}^{2}}{\xi_{1}^{2}}\frac{\omega_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}}{\omega_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}}

(S126)

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}}{s_{1}\xi_{1}^{2}}\frac{\omega_{2}/s_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}/s_{2}}{\omega_{1}/s_{1}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}/s_{1}}

(S127)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}}{s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}}\frac{\omega_{2}/s_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}/s_{2}}{\omega_{2}/s_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{1}/s_{1}}

(S128)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}}{s_{2}\xi_{2}^{2}}\frac{\omega_{2}/s_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}/s_{2}}{\omega_{2}/s_{2}+(\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1})_{2}/s_{2}}

(S129)

\displaystyle=1\,,

(S130)

where in the last inequality we have used
Eq.

S107
.
∎

Appendix H
Optimal regularization

As the overparameterization ratio
\phi/\psi
goes to infinity, the expression for the test error simplifies and is given by the expressions in
Corollary

5.5
. In this case, it is straightforward to derive the value of the regularization constant that minimizes the test error.

Proposition H.0

(Restatement of
Proposition

6.1
)
.

In the setting of
Corollary

5.5
and under
4
, the optimal regularization
\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}
is independent of
\mu
and satisfies,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\argmin_{\gamma\geq-\rho\omega}E_{\mu}(\gamma)=\rho(\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}-\omega)\qquad\text{and}\qquad E_{\mu}^{\text{opt}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=E_{\mu}(\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu})=\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}\,.

(S131)

Moreover, the optimal error is ordered with respect to shift strength, i.e. for two LJSDs
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
,
E_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{opt}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{opt}}
.

Proof of
Proposition

6.1
.

From the expressions in
Section

I.7
when
\psi/\phi=0
, we have for
\gamma_{\text{eff}}=\gamma/\rho+\omega
,

\displaystyle x=\frac{1}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\mathcal{I}_{1,1}}>0\,,\qquad\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}=-\frac{\tau}{\gamma_{\text{eff}}+\phi\mathcal{I}_{1,2}}<0\,,\qquad\tau=\frac{x}{\rho}\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bar{\tau}=\frac{1}{\gamma}\,,

(S132)

where the inequalities follow from the constraint
\gamma\geq-\rho\omega
, which implies that
\gamma_{\text{eff}}\geq 0
.

Differentiation shows that,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}x}{\partial\gamma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{x}\left(\left(\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\right)^{2}-\rho\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\mathcal{I}_{2,3}\right)>0\,.

(S133)

Under
4
, these expressions and those in
Corollary

5.5
and
Eq.

S5
give,

\displaystyle E_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}+\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\rho(\gamma_{\text{eff}}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}\,,

(S134)

so that,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial E_{\mu}}{\partial\gamma}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}+\frac{\partial^{2}x}{\partial\gamma^{2}}\rho(\gamma_{\text{eff}}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}+\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}-2\left(\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\right)^{2}\rho(\gamma_{\text{eff}}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\mathcal{I}^{*}_{3,3}

(S135)

\displaystyle=\rho(\gamma_{\text{eff}}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\left(\frac{\partial^{2}x}{\partial\gamma^{2}}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,2}-2\left(\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\right)^{2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{3,3}\right)

(S136)

\displaystyle=\rho(\gamma_{\text{eff}}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\left(\frac{\partial^{2}x}{\partial\gamma^{2}}\left(\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,3}+x\mathcal{I}^{*}_{3,3}\right)-2\left(\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\right)^{2}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{3,3}\right)

(S137)

\displaystyle=\rho(\gamma_{\text{eff}}-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2})\left(\frac{\partial^{2}x}{\partial\gamma^{2}}\phi\mathcal{I}^{*}_{2,3}-2\rho\frac{\partial x}{\partial\gamma}\mathcal{I}_{2,3}\mathcal{I}^{*}_{3,3}\right)\,.

(S138)

Noting that the term in parentheses on the last line is positive, we find that the unique minimizer
13
13

13

Note that
\partial E_{\mu}/\partial\gamma<0
for
\gamma_{\text{eff}}<\sigma^{2}
and
\partial E_{\mu}/\partial\gamma>0
for
\gamma_{\text{eff}}>\sigma^{2}
.
of the error is given by
\gamma_{\text{eff}}=\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}
.
Eq.

S131
then follows immediately from the definition of
\gamma_{\text{eff}}=\gamma/\rho+\omega
and from
Eq.

S134
.

Finally, to establish the last statement, namely that
E_{\mu_{1}}^{\text{opt}}\leq E_{\mu_{2}}^{\text{opt}}
for
\mu_{1}\leq\mu_{2}
, simply note that
E_{\mu}(\gamma^{\text{opt}}_{\mu})=\mathcal{I}^{*}_{1,1}
and apply
Corollary

A.4
.
∎

Appendix I
Proof of
Theorem

5.3

As discussed in
Section

2
, we consider predictive functions
\hat{y}
defined by random feature kernel ridge regression,

\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=YK^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}}\,

(S139)

for
K\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=K(X,X)+\gamma I_{m}
,
K_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=K(X,\mathbf{x})
, with
\gamma
is a ridge regularization constant. Here

\displaystyle K

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}}F^{\top}F+\gamma I_{m}\,,

(S140)

and, as in the main text, we have introduced the abbreviations
F\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sigma(WX/\sqrt{n_{0}})
. The labels are generated by a linear function parameterized by
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}}
, whose entries are drawn independently from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, i.e.
Y=\beta^{\top}X/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon
. In this section, we develop the techniques and detailed calculations needed to determine the high-dimensional limit of the test error,

E_{\Sigma^{*}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}\mathbb{E}[(y(\mathbf{x})-\hat{y}(\mathbf{x}))^{2}]-\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}\mathbb{E}[(\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}}-YK^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}})^{2}]\,,

(S141)

as well as its decomposition into bias and variance terms,

E_{\Sigma^{*}}=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}\mathbb{E}[(\mathbb{E}[\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})]-y(\mathbf{x}))^{2}]}_{B_{\Sigma^{*}}}+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x},\beta}[\mathbb{V}[\hat{y}(\mathbf{x})]]}_{V_{\Sigma^{*}}}\,.

(S142)

We may also write the test loss as,

E_{\Sigma^{*}}=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},y)}(y-\hat{y}(\mathbf{x}))^{2}=E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}

(S143)

with

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}y(\mathbf{x})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}\mathrm{tr}(y(\mathbf{x})y(\mathbf{x})^{\top})

(S144)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y)y(\mathbf{x})=-2\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\varepsilon)}\mathrm{tr}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}y(\mathbf{x}))

(S145)

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\varepsilon)}\mathrm{tr}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}YK^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}})\,.

(S146)

I.1
Reducing to the mean-zero case

In this section, we aim to show that we may assume without loss of generality that the activation function is centered. While many of the ideas in the proof are similar to those of
Mei and Montanari 2021
, at the technical level many distinctions arise owing to our differing assumptions on the data matrix
X
and weight matrix
W
. In particular, their approach utilizes a decomposition into Gegenbauer polynomials and exact orthogonality among these orthogonal basis functions. Since this approach is not feasible in our setting of iid Gaussian matrices, we develop an alternative proof that we present below.

To proceed, we define centered versions of the the random feature matrices
F
and
f
as

\bar{F}_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=F_{ij}-\mathbb{E}_{Z}\sigma\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)}Z}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{f}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=f_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{Z}\sigma\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z}\right)

(S147)

for all
i
and
j
and
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Note that this centering operation is
n_{0}
-dependent, but in the limit
\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma),\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})\to s,s_{*}
, which appear in the limiting self-consistent equation. To show replacing
F
and
f
by
\bar{F}
and
\bar{f}
does not alter the test error in the limit, we have to show that the
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
terms of Equations (
S144
)-(
S146
) are not changed in the limit. Clearly this is true for
E_{1}
as it contains neither
F
nor
f
. For
E_{2}
we must show

\mathbb{E}\left[{(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}-\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\to 0

(S148)

and for
E_{3}
we must show

\mathbb{E}\left[{(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}-(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}}\right]\to 0,

(S149)

where

\bar{K}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\bar{F}^{\top}\bar{F}+\gamma I\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\bar{F}^{\top}\bar{f}.

(S150)

Define the random variable

\Delta\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}-\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1})Y^{\top}.

(S151)

To control the typical behavior of
\Delta
, we will define an event
\mathcal{E}
(see
Definition

I.8
), and let
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}
be an indicator for
\mathcal{E}
. In
Lemma

I.9
we show
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{c}}]\to 0
.

We can argue

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{(1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}})\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert,

(S152)

for Equation (
S148
), and

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\Delta(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}+\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})}\right]\right\rvert

(S153)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{(1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}})\left({(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}-(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}}\right)}\right]\right\rvert,

(S154)

for Equation (
S149
). We demonstrate Equations (
S152
) and (
S153
) are
o(1)
in two steps. The first terms on the right-hand sides of Equations (
S152
) and (
S153
) represent the typical behavior of the random variables. To bound them, we show that, given
\mathcal{E}
,
\Delta\to 0
in
Lemma

I.10
. The second terms on the right-hand sides of Equations (
S152
) and (
S153
) represent the atypical behavior of the random variables. To bound them, we use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and the fact that
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{c}}]\to 0
in
Lemma

I.7
.

To briefly outline the structure of this section:
Section

I.1.1
proves concentration of some quadratic forms of the underlying random matrices and bounds their operator norms with high probability;
Section

I.1.2
controls the atypical behavior mentioned above;
Section

I.1.3
applies the Schur complement formula to derive an expression for
\Delta
that we can bound;
Section

I.1.4
defines an event where the expression for
\Delta
from the Schur complement formula can be easily bounded; and
Section

I.1.5
completes the argument by bounding
\Delta
on this typical event.

I.1.1
Prerequisites for concentration and linearization

Just as in
Adlam et al. 2019
, the concentration of the linear data kernel about its expectation is a key ingredient to the analysis. Define the random variables

\Upsilon\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\frac{1}{n_{0}}X^{\top}X\quad\text{and}\quad\upsilon_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\lVert\mathbf{x}\rVert_{2}^{2}/n_{0}.

(S155)

Remark I.0
.

Throughout this section and the next we will use
C
to denote an arbitrarily large,
n_{0}
-independent, positive constant, which can increase from line to line. For example, if
X\leq C
and
Y\leq C
, we will simply write
XY\leq C
, since
C^{2}
is still some
n_{0}
-independent constant. This approach is valid as such replacements only occur a finite number of times. Similarly,
c
denotes some arbitrarily small,
n_{0}
-independent, positive constant that can decrease from line to line.

Then we note

\mathbb{E}\Upsilon=\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)I_{n_{0}}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{E}\upsilon_{*}=\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}).

(S156)

For the variance, we see by
1
that

\mathbb{V}\Upsilon_{ij}=\frac{1+\mathbb{I}(i=j)}{n_{0}}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)\leq C/n_{0}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{V}\upsilon_{*}=\frac{2}{n_{0}}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}((\Sigma^{*})^{2})\leq C/n_{0}.

(S157)

This motivates the following lemma.

Lemma I.0
.

The event

\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\leq C}\right\}\cap\left\{{\left\lvert\upsilon_{*}\!-\!\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{i,j}\left\{{\left\lvert\Upsilon_{ij}\!-\!\delta_{ij}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}\right\}

(S158)

occurs with high-probability, that is, for some positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
C

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S159)

for any positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
c
.

Proof.

Tighter concentration than is obtained directly from the variance can be shown by observing that
\Upsilon
and
\upsilon_{*}
are equal in distribution to

\frac{1}{n_{0}}Z^{T}\Sigma Z\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{n_{0}}z^{\top}\Sigma^{*}z

(S160)

for
Z
an
(n_{0},m)
-dimensional matrix and
z
an
n_{0}
-dimensional vector both containing i.i.d. standard Gaussian random variables. Then using the assumption
\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty},\lVert\Sigma^{*}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C
and applying the results of
Erdős et al. 2012
(or similar concentration results), we find that for some positive constant
C
that

\mathbb{P}[\left\lvert\Upsilon_{ij}-\delta_{ij}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)\right\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}]\leq C\exp({-n_{0}^{c}}),

(S161)

where
c
is any positive constant. An identical concentration result holds for
\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})
. Then, by the union bound, we see

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[{\left\{{\left\lvert\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})\right\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{-c}}\right\}\cup\bigcup_{i,j}\left\{{\left\lvert\Upsilon_{ij}-\delta_{ij}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)\right\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}\right\}}\right]

(S162)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq Cn_{0}^{2}C\exp\left({-n_{0}^{c}}\right)

(S163)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\exp(-n_{0}^{c}).

(S164)

The operator norm of
\Upsilon
can also be bounded probabilistically. For some
n_{0}
-independent constant
C
,

\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}\lVert Z/\sqrt{n_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leq C\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}\left({1+\sqrt{\frac{m}{n_{0}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(2/\delta)}{n_{0}}}}\right)\leq C

(S165)

with probability at least
1-\delta
, where
Z
is i.i.d. Gaussian
(see for example
Vershynin 2018
, Theorem 4.4.5)
. Thus, setting
\delta=n_{0}^{-c}
, we see
\mathbb{P}[\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\geq C]\leq n_{0}^{-c}
. Applying the union bound again completes the proof.
∎

Lemma I.0
.

The (squared) expected operator norm of
\Upsilon
is also bounded:

\mathbb{E}\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leq C

(S166)

for some positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
C
.

Proof.

This result follows from a tail integration argument. That is using
Vershynin 2018
) we have that

\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}\lVert Z/\sqrt{n_{0}}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\leq C\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}\left({1+\sqrt{\frac{m}{n_{0}}}+u}\right)

(S167)

with probability at least
1-2\exp(-n_{0}u^{2})
, where
Z
is i.i.d. Gaussian. Hence we have that
\mathbb{P}[\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\geq C\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}\left({1+\sqrt{\frac{m}{n_{0}}}+u}\right)]\leq 2\exp(-n_{0}u^{2}).

Now by the tail integration identity,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}^{2}]

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}u\mathbb{P}[\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\geq u]du

(S168)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\!\left({1\!+\!\sqrt{\frac{m}{n_{0}}}}\right)^{2}\!+\!C^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!u\mathbb{P}\left[{\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}\!\geq\!C\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}\!\left({1\!+\!\sqrt{\frac{m}{n_{0}}}\!+\!u}\right)}\right]du

(S169)

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left({1+\sqrt{\frac{m}{n_{0}}}}\right)^{2}+C^{2}\lVert\Sigma\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\cdot\frac{1}{n_{0}}

(S170)

\displaystyle\leq C.

(S171)

by integrating the Gaussian tail.
∎

Next, we state similar results for the matrix
\tilde{\Upsilon}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=W\Sigma^{*}W^{\top}/n_{0}
as were obtained for
\Upsilon
above.

Lemma I.0
.

The event

\mathcal{E}_{W}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{\left\lVert\tilde{\Upsilon}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{i,j}\left\{{\left\lvert\tilde{\Upsilon}_{ij}\!-\!\delta_{ij}\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}\right\}

(S172)

occurs with high-probability, that is, for some positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
C

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{W}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S173)

for any positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
c
.

Proof.

The claims follow identically to
Lemma

I.2
.
∎

Lemma I.0
.

Moreover on the event
\mathcal{E}_{W}
, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})_{i}\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}

(S174)

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})^{\top}=\zeta_{*}\tilde{\Upsilon}+(\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*})I_{m}+\Delta,

(S175)

where
\lVert\Delta\rVert_{\infty}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}
.

Proof.

Note that conditional on
W
,
W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tilde{\Upsilon})
. Using a Taylor expansion in
{\epsilon\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tilde{\Upsilon}_{ii}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma*)}
below with
3
shows that elementwise

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})_{i}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{Z}\bar{\sigma}\left({\sqrt{\tilde{\Upsilon}_{ii}}Z}\right)\right\rvert

(S176)

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{Z}{\sigma}\left({\sqrt{\epsilon+\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z}\right)-\mathbb{E}_{Z}{\sigma}\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z}\right)\right\rvert

(S177)

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon

(S178)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2},

(S179)

where
Z
is a standard Gaussian. See
Adlam et al. 2019
for additional details. This allows us to assume that
\sigma(W\mathbf{x})
is centered as a conditional expectation over
\mathbf{x}
at the expense of an elementwise
Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}
error. Denoting
\bar{f}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})
, we note this can be extended to the matrix
\mathbb{E}\bar{f}\bar{f}^{\top}
. We see

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f}\bar{f}^{\top}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[(\bar{f}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})(\bar{f}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})^{\top}]+(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})^{\top}.

(S180)

However, the second term on the right-hand side of Equation (
S180
) is small is operator norm, since it is rank-1 and

\left\lVert(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})^{\top}\right\rVert_{\infty}=\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}

(S181)

by Equation (
S179
). Next, we see for
i\neq j
that

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f}_{i}\bar{f}_{j}=\mathbb{E}_{Z_{1},Z_{2}}\bar{\sigma}(c_{1}Z_{1})\bar{\sigma}(c_{12}Z_{1}+c_{2}Z_{2})

(S182)

for constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, and
c_{12}
depending on
W
and
\Sigma^{*}
, where
Z_{1}
and
Z_{2}
are independent, standard Gaussians. Again, see
Adlam et al. 2019
for details. The
i=j
terms can be handled similarly. Moreover,
{c_{12}=\tilde{\Upsilon}_{ij}/\sqrt{\tilde{\Upsilon}_{ii}}\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}
, so Taylor expanding in
c_{12}Z_{1}
, we find

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f}\bar{f}^{\top}=\zeta_{*}\Upsilon+(\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*})I+\tilde{\Delta}+(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})^{\top}.

(S183)

As a consequence of the Taylor expansion and
3
,
\tilde{\Delta}
has entries that are bounded by
Cn_{0}^{c-1}
in absolute value
(
Adlam et al. 2019
)
. The final conclusion for
{\Delta=\tilde{\Delta}+(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{f})^{\top}}
follows by upper bounding
\lVert\tilde{\Delta}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\lVert\tilde{\Delta}\rVert_{F}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}
and using the previous bound in Equation (
S181
).
∎

Finally we include a bound on the operator norm of the random feature matrix. The argument follows
Mei and Montanari 2021
closely.

Lemma I.0
.

Under
3
, the event

\mathcal{E}_{F}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left\{{\left\lVert\bar{F}/\sqrt{n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}\right\}

(S184)

occurs with high-probability, that is, for some positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
C

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{F}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S185)

for any positive,
n_{0}
-independent constant
c<10
.

Proof.

Consider the matrix

\displaystyle\bar{R}_{ij}=1_{i\neq j}\bar{\sigma}(z_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}z_{j}/\sqrt{n_{1}})/\sqrt{n_{1}}

(S186)

for
z_{i}=\Sigma^{-1/2}X_{i}
(i.e. columns of
\Sigma^{-1/2}X
) for
1\leq i\leq m
and
z_{m+i}=W_{i}
(i.e. rows of
W
) for
1\leq i\leq n_{0}
. By construction, we note that
\bar{F}/\sqrt{n_{1}}
is a minor of
\bar{R}
. Thus, bounding the operator norm of
\bar{R}
suffices to bound the operator norm of
\bar{F}
. Moreover,
z_{i}
are independent Gaussians distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n_{0}})
.

Next we show that the entries to the activation function cannot be too large with high probability. For convenience throughout we define
M=m+n_{0}
and let
\Omega
be a symmetric matrix with
\lVert\Omega\rVert_{\infty}\leq C
. Note that, for
i\neq j
, the random variable
z_{i}^{\top}\Omega z_{j}/\sqrt{n_{0}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{0}}\lambda_{k}(\Omega)a_{k}b_{k}/\sqrt{n_{0}}
where
a_{k}
and
b_{k}
are i.i.d. from
\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. So the moment generating function can be bounded as,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\exp\left({\sum_{i=1}^{n_{0}}t\lambda_{i}(\Omega)a_{i}b_{i}/\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\mathbb{E}[\exp(a_{i}\cdot t\lambda_{i}(\Omega)b_{i}/\sqrt{n_{0}})]

(S187)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\mathbb{E}[\exp(t^{2}b_{i}^{2}\lambda_{i}(\Omega)^{2}/(2n_{0})]

(S188)

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=1}^{n_{0}}\mathbb{E}[\exp(t^{2}b_{i}^{2}C^{2}/(2n_{0})]

(S189)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}[\exp(t^{2}b_{i}^{2}C^{2}/2)]

(S190)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-C^{2}t^{2}}}

(S191)

for
\lvert t\rvert\leq\frac{1}{C}
. Further
\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-C^{2}t^{2}}}\leq\exp(C^{2}t^{2})
for
\lvert t\rvert\leq\frac{1}{2C}
. This establishes the original random variable is subexponential. Thus this random variables satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert z_{i}^{\top}\Omega z_{j}/\sqrt{n_{0}}\rvert\geq u]\leq 2\max\{\exp(-u^{2}/2C^{2}),\exp(-u/2C)\}

(S192)

(see for example
Wainwright 2019
, Proposition 2.9)
. Define the event

\mathcal{G}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bigcap_{1\leq i<j\leq M}\left\{{\left\lvert\frac{z_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}z_{j}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right\rvert\geq C\sqrt{\log M}}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{1\leq i<j\leq M}\left\{{\left\lvert\frac{z_{i}^{\top}\Sigma z_{j}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right\rvert\geq C^{2}\sqrt{\log M}}\right\}.

(S193)

Then by a union bound and Markov’s inequality, we have that

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq{4}/{M^{20}}

(S194)

for large enough
C
.

We now define a modified version of
\bar{\sigma}
, that is the same up to a constant factor on
\mathcal{G}
but is truncated outside of
\mathcal{G}
. Let
\bar{u}=C\sqrt{\log M}
and taking the constants from
3
, we see

\tilde{\sigma}(u)\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\begin{cases}\bar{\sigma}(u)\exp(-c_{1}\lvert\bar{u}\rvert)/c_{0}&\text{for }\lvert u\rvert\leq\bar{u}\\
\bar{\sigma}(\bar{u})\exp(-c_{1}\lvert\bar{u}\rvert)/c_{0}&\text{for }u>\bar{u}\\
\bar{\sigma}(-\bar{u})\exp(-c_{1}\lvert\bar{u}\rvert)/c_{0}&\text{for }u<-\bar{u}\end{cases}.

(S195)

Just as we did with
\sigma
in Equation (
S147
), we center
\tilde{\sigma}
with its mean
\tilde{a}
. Note
\tilde{\sigma}
is a 1-bounded and 1-Lipschitz
function so
\lvert\tilde{a}\rvert\leq 1
.

Now consider the matrix

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1_{i\neq j}(\tilde{\sigma}(z_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}z_{j}/\sqrt{n_{0}})-\tilde{a})/\sqrt{n_{1}}.

(S196)

By controlling the operator norm of
\tilde{R}
, we can control the operator norm of
\bar{R}
at the end of the proof. By a covering argument it suffices to control

\displaystyle\lVert\tilde{R}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\max_{v\in S}10\underbrace{\lvert v^{\top}\tilde{R}v\rvert}_{F_{v}(Z)},

(S197)

where
S
is a
1/4
-covering of the
M
-dimensional sphere with cardinality
\exp(cM)
and the matrix
Z
is of all the variables
z_{i}
.

We now seek to apply
Deshpande and Montanari 2016
. To this end
we wish to show that
F_{v}(Z)
is Lipschitz in
Z
, that is, if we define
\mathcal{Z}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{t}Z+\sqrt{1-t}Z^{\prime}
for (
Z,Z^{\prime})\in\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}
, we need to show that

\displaystyle\max_{v\in S}\max_{t\in[0,1]}\left\lVert\nabla F_{v}(\sqrt{t}Z+\sqrt{1-t}Z^{\prime})\right\rVert_{F}\leq L

(S198)

for suitable
L
. Consider the gradient with respect to a column of
\mathcal{Z}
(denoted by
\zeta_{l}
):

\displaystyle\nabla_{\zeta_{l}}F_{v}(\mathcal{Z})

\displaystyle=2\frac{v_{l}}{\sqrt{n_{0}n_{1}}}\sum_{i\neq l}\Sigma^{1/2}\zeta_{i}\underbrace{v_{i}\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}(\zeta_{i}^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}\zeta_{l}^{\top}/\sqrt{n_{0}})}_{\xi_{i}}

(S199)

\displaystyle=2\frac{v_{l}}{\sqrt{n_{0}n_{1}}}\Sigma^{1/2}\mathcal{Z}\mathbf{\xi},

(S200)

where
\mathbf{\xi}
is the vector with coordinates
\xi_{i}
except at
l
where it is zero. Continuing, on the set
\mathcal{G}
, we find

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla_{w_{l}}F_{v}(W)\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{2}v_{l}^{2}/n_{0}^{2}\sum_{i\neq l,j\neq l}\left\lvert\xi_{i}\xi_{j}w_{i}^{\top}\Sigma w_{j}\right\rvert

(S201)

\displaystyle\leq Cv_{l}^{2}\sqrt{\log M}/n_{0}^{2}\sum_{i}\sum_{j\neq i}\lvert\xi_{i}\xi_{j}\rvert

(S202)

\displaystyle\leq Cv_{l}^{2}\sqrt{\log M}/n_{0}^{2}\sum_{i}\sum_{j\neq i}(\xi_{i}^{2}+\xi_{j}^{2})

(S203)

\displaystyle\leq Cv_{l}^{2}\sqrt{\log M}/n_{0}

(S204)

where the last line follows since
\lvert\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}(\cdot)\rvert_{2}\leq 1
implies that
\lVert\xi\rVert\leq 1
. So finally, we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert\nabla_{W}F_{v}(W)\rVert_{F}^{2}\leq C\sqrt{\log M}/n_{0}=:L^{2}.

(S205)

Now
Deshpande and Montanari 2016
shows for a constant
D
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[{\lvert v^{\top}\tilde{R}v\rvert}>D]\leq C\exp\left(Cn_{0}-\frac{D^{2}}{L^{2}}\right)+\frac{C}{D^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\max_{v\in S}(F_{v}(Z)-F_{v}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{c}}}].

(S206)

We use a crude bound on the complement. First note that

\max_{v\in S}(F_{v}(Z)-F_{v}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}\leq C\lVert\tilde{\sigma}(Z^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}Z/\sqrt{n_{0}})\rVert_{F}^{2}\leq C\lVert Z^{\top}Z/\sqrt{n_{0}}\rVert_{F}^{2}+Cn_{0}^{2},

(S207)

since removing the 1-bounded-Lipschitz
\tilde{\sigma}(\cdot)
can be done by centering each activation with
\tilde{\sigma}(0)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\max_{v\in S}(F_{v}(Z)-F_{v}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{c}}}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\frac{C}{n_{1}}(\mathbb{E}[\lVert Z^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}Z/\sqrt{n_{0}}\rVert_{F}^{4}]+n_{0}^{4})\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{c}}]}.

(S208)

A short computation shows that
\mathbb{E}[\lVert Z^{\top}\Sigma^{1/2}Z/\sqrt{n_{0}}\rVert_{F}^{4}]\leq Cn_{0}^{8}
. Combining all terms then shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert v^{\top}\tilde{R}v\rvert>D]\leq C\exp(Cn_{0})\cdot\exp\left({-\frac{n_{0}D^{2}}{\log M}}\right)+\frac{C}{D^{2}}\cdot\frac{C}{M^{5}}.

(S209)

Choosing
D=c_{3}\sqrt{\log M}
for sufficiently large
c_{3}
shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert v^{\top}\tilde{R}v\rvert>D]\leq C\exp(-cn_{0})+\frac{C}{n_{0}^{10}}\leq\frac{C}{n_{0}^{10}},

(S210)

where
c>0
.

Recalling the original
\epsilon
-net covering, this implies that

\displaystyle\lVert\tilde{R}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C\sqrt{\log n_{0}}

(S211)

with probability at least
C/n^{10}
. We can now finish the argument by relating the operator norm of
\bar{R}
and
\tilde{R}
. Recalling the rescaling between the modified and unmodified activations, we see

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lVert\bar{R}\rVert_{\infty}\geq C\sqrt{\log n_{0}}\cdot c_{0}\exp(c_{1}\lvert\bar{u}\rvert)]\leq\mathbb{P}[\lVert\tilde{R}\rVert_{\infty}\geq C\sqrt{\log(n_{0})},\mathcal{G}]+\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{G}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq C/n_{0}^{10}.

(S212)

∎

I.1.2
Controlling the atypical behavior

Since the argument for the atypical event is the most straightforward, we provide that first.

Lemma I.0
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{c}}]=Cn_{0}^{-c}
for some
n_{0}
-independent constants
C>0
and
c>0
. Then,

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{(1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}})\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert\to 0

(S213)

and

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{(1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}})\left({(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}-(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}}\right)}\right]\right\rvert\to 0

(S214)

as
n_{0}\to\infty
.

Proof.

Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to the left-hand side of Equation (
S213
), we may bound it by

\mathbb{E}\left\lvert\left[{(1-\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}})\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert\leq\left({\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{c}}}}\right]}\right)^{1/2}\left({\mathbb{E}\left\lvert\Delta y\right\rvert^{2}}\right)^{1/2}.

(S215)

Since the first term
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{c}]\to 0
it also follows that
(\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{c}])^{1/2}\to 0
. So it suffices to show
{\mathbb{E}[\lvert\Delta y\rvert^{2}]=O(1)}
.

We now apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality again to bound the second term by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lvert(\Delta y(\mathbf{x}))^{2}\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{4}]\mathbb{E}[y(\mathbf{x})^{4}]}.

(S216)

A simple argument shows that
\mathbb{E}[y(\mathbf{x})^{4}]\leq C(\mathbb{E}[(\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x})^{4}/n_{0}^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{4}])\leq C\mathbb{E}[\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbf{z}\rVert^{4}/n_{0}^{2}]+C\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{4}\leq C
for
\mathbf{z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
since
\mathbb{E}[\lVert\mathbf{z}\rVert_{2}^{4}]/n_{0}^{2}]\leq C
. Hence it suffices to show
\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{4}]\leq C
. To this end note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{4}]\leq C(\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]+\mathbb{E}[(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}])

(S217)

An identical argument can be applied to bound both terms. We outline the argument for the first term.
We see

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]\rvert\leq\mathbb{E}\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\beta,\varepsilon}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}|W,X,x]\rvert.

(S218)

Now note that by the definition of
Y=\beta^{\top}X/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon
we have that,

\displaystyle(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}\leq C\left((K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}\frac{X}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\beta)^{4}+(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}\varepsilon)^{4}\right)

(S219)

Recalling that
\beta\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n_{0}})
and
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{m})
, we can compute the Gaussian moments (marginally in
\beta,\varepsilon
) as,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\beta,\varepsilon}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}\frac{X}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\beta)^{4}+(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}\varepsilon)^{4}|W,X,x]

(S220)

\displaystyle\qquad\leq C\left((K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}\left(\frac{X^{\top}X}{n_{0}}\right)K^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top})^{2}+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-2}K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top})^{2}\right)

(S221)

Continuing, using the definition of
K_{\mathbf{x}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}\left(\frac{X^{\top}X}{n_{0}}\right)K^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top})^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\frac{f^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\frac{FK^{-1}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}(\frac{X^{\top}X}{n_{0}})\frac{K^{-1}F^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\frac{f}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right\|_{\infty}^{2}\right]

(S222)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[{\left\lVert\frac{f}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{4}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{FK^{-1}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{4}\cdot\left\lVert\frac{X^{\top}X}{n_{0}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\right]

(S223)

Noting that
K=FF^{\top}/n_{0}+\gamma I_{m}
an application of the SVD to
\frac{F}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}
(denoting the corresponding singular values as
s_{i}
) shows that
\lVert\frac{FK^{-1}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\rVert_{\infty}=\max_{i}\frac{s_{i}}{s_{i}^{2}+\gamma}\leq\max_{s\geq 0}\frac{s}{s^{2}+\gamma}=1/({2\sqrt{\gamma}})
. Hence, we can continue bounding Equation (
S223
) by

\displaystyle C\mathbb{E}[\left\lVert{f}/{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{4}]\cdot\mathbb{E}[\|X^{\top}X/n_{0}\|_{\infty}^{2}],

(S224)

where we exploited the independence of
f
and
X
.
Lemma

I.3
ensures that
\mathbb{E}[\lVert X^{\top}X/n_{0}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\lVert\Upsilon\rVert_{\infty}^{2}]\leq C
. The former term becomes
\mathbb{E}[\|{f}/{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\|_{2}^{4}]=\mathbb{E}[\sigma(\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}({\Sigma^{*}})}z)^{4}]\leq C
for
z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
by
3
. Thus, the previous displays are bounded by some constant
C
. An entirely analogous argument shows that
\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-2}K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top})^{2}]\leq C
. Together these two results along with the previous computations establish that,

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]\rvert\leq C

(S225)

Note that our argument did not exploit any explicit properties of the centered vs uncentered activation function
\sigma
vs.
\bar{\sigma}
. Hence an identical argument shows that,
\lvert\mathbb{E}[(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]\rvert\leq C
.
These two results imply
{\mathbb{E}[\Delta^{4}]}\leq C
as desired.

Now we turn to the second term. Using an identical application of the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality as used for the first term, we can bound Equation (
S214
) by

\left({\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{c}}}}\right]}\right)^{1/2}\left({\mathbb{E}\left\lvert(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}-(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}\right\rvert^{2}}\right)^{1/2}.

(S226)

Hence we can show Equation (
S214
) is
o(1)
if

\mathbb{E}\left\lvert(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}-(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}\right\rvert^{2}=O(1)
. This result follows immediately from the computations shown for the previous term since we have already established that
\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]\leq C
and
\mathbb{E}[(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]\leq C
. These two previously shown results imply

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lvert(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}-(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}\right\rvert^{2}\leq C\left(\mathbb{E}[(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]+\mathbb{E}[(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})^{4}]\right)\leq C.

(S227)

∎

I.1.3
Schur complement formula for bounding
\Delta

The centering procedure is a rank-1 change to
F
and
f
, so applying the Schur complement formula to understand its effect is natural. Write
{a\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}_{Z}\sigma\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)}Z}\right)}
,
{a_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}_{Z}\sigma\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z}\right)}
,
{\mathbf{v}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1/n_{1}\bar{F}^{\top}1_{n_{1}}}
,
{\mathbf{u}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=a1_{m}}
,
{U\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=[\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}]^{\top}}
, and
{C\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
. Then,

\displaystyle K^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\bar{K}+\mathbf{u}\mathbf{v}^{\top}+\mathbf{v}\mathbf{u}^{\top}+\mathbf{u}\mathbf{u}^{\top})^{-1}

(S228)

\displaystyle=(\bar{K}+U^{\top}CU)^{-1}

(S229)

\displaystyle=\bar{K}^{-1}-\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}(C^{-1}+U\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top})^{-1}U\bar{K}^{-1}\,,

(S230)

and, for
\delta\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1/n_{1}\bar{f}^{\top}1_{n_{1}}
and
P\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\delta+a_{*},a_{*})^{\top}
,

\displaystyle K_{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+\frac{1}{n_{1}}(F-\bar{F})^{\top}\bar{f}+\frac{1}{n_{1}}\bar{F}^{\top}(f-\bar{f})+\frac{1}{n_{1}}(F-\bar{F})^{\top}(f-\bar{f})

(S231)

\displaystyle=\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+(\delta+a_{*})\mathbf{u}+a_{*}\mathbf{v}

(S232)

\displaystyle=\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+U^{\top}P\,.

(S233)

Combining these expressions,

\displaystyle K^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=K^{-1}(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+U^{\top}P)

(S234)

\displaystyle=\bar{K}^{-1}(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+U^{\top}P)-\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}(C^{-1}+U\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top})^{-1}U\bar{K}^{-1}(\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+U^{\top}P)

(S235)

\displaystyle=\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+T_{1}+T_{2}\,,

(S236)

with

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\left(I_{2}-(C^{-1}+U\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top})^{-1}U\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)P

(S237)

\displaystyle=\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}P

(S238)

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=-\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}(C^{-1}+U\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top})^{-1}U\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}

(S239)

\displaystyle=-\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}CU\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\,.

(S240)

Furthermore, writing
{c_{0}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\tfrac{1}{m}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}}
,
{c_{1}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}}
,

{c_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1-\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}}
,

\displaystyle\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}&c_{2}-c_{1}\\
-mc_{0}&mc_{0}+c_{1}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(S241)

so that,

\displaystyle\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}P

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}\delta+c_{2}a_{*}\\
-mc_{0}\delta+c_{1}a_{*}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(S242)

and,

\displaystyle\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}C

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}c_{2}&c_{1}\\
c_{1}&-mc_{0}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(S243)

I.1.4
Concentration with high-probability

Definition I.0
.

Recall the definitions from
Section

I.1.3
. To characterize the typical behavior of the random variables, we define the event

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}\cap\mathcal{E}_{W}\cap\mathcal{E}_{F}\cap\left\{{|\delta|\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}\right\}\cap\left\{{cn_{0}^{-c}\leq c_{0}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}\right\}\cap\left\{{c_{1}\leq Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}}\right\}

(S244)

\displaystyle\quad\cap\left\{{cn_{0}^{-c}\leq c_{2}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}\right\}\cap\left\{{Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}\right\}\cap\left\{{Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\leq Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}}\right\}.

(S245)

\displaystyle\cap\left\{{\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}\right\}\cap\left\{{\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}}\right\}\cap\left\{{y(\mathbf{x})\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}\right\}

(S246)

Lemma I.0
.

The event
\mathcal{E}
is high-probability, that is,
\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}
for some constant
C>0
and any constant
0<c<10
.

Proof.

First, we already know
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
,
\mathcal{E}_{W}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{F}
all occur with high-probability (see
Lemmas

I.2
,
I.4
and
I.6
). When bounding the other events in Equations (
S244
)-(
S246
), it will be useful to introduce conditioning on one of
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
,
\mathcal{E}_{W}
, or
\mathcal{E}_{F}
. This will suffice since for any event
\mathcal{A}
, we have

\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{c}}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]+\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}^{\mathsf{c}}]\leq\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{A}^{\mathsf{c}}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]+Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S247)

for example. We will explicitly denote this conditioning, but when taking expectation over some random variables (e.g.
x
or
W
) we will not explicitly denote that we are conditioning on the remaining independent random variables (e.g.
X
). Once we have bounded the probability of the complement of each event in Equations (
S244
)-(
S246
), we can apply the union bound to complete the proof.

For future reference recall
{\mathbf{v}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1/n_{1}\bar{F}^{\top}1_{n_{1}}}
,
{\mathbf{u}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=a1_{m}}
. We deal with each event in Equations (
S244
)-(
S246
) in turn.

Controlling
\delta
:

Here, we introduce conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
. To control
\delta
, we want to calculate its mean and variance to apply Chebyshev’s inequality. Note that
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
is independent of
W
, so the expectations over
W
below are unchanged by this conditioning. Recall

\delta=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}{\sigma}\left({\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}W_{kj}x_{j}/\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right)-a_{*}

(S248)

and note that conditional on
x
the sum
\sum_{j}W_{kj}x_{j}/\sqrt{n_{0}}
is distributed as
\mathcal{N}(0,\upsilon_{*})
for all
k
.

Expanding in
\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})
, we see

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{W}[\delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}[{\sigma}(\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})+(\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}))}Z)]-a_{*}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]

(S249)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}_{Z}[{\sigma}(\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z)]-a_{*}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]

(S250)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}))}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z{\sigma}(\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z)]+\Delta\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}}\right]

(S251)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*}))}{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}\mathbb{E}_{Z}[\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z{\sigma}(\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z)]+\Delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}}\right],

(S252)

where
Z
is a standard Gaussian. The first term of Equation (
S252
) is less than
Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}
and the remainder term
\Delta
is less than
Cn_{0}^{c-1}
on
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
. More detail on this argument can be found in
Adlam et al. 2019
. Taking expectation over the remaining randomness, we see
\mathbb{E}[\delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}
.

Similarly,

\mathbb{V}_{W}[\delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1}}\mathbb{V}_{W}\left[{{\sigma}\left({\sum_{j=1}^{n_{0}}W_{kj}x_{j}/\sqrt{n_{0}}}\right)\Big|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}}\right]=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\mathbb{V}_{Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\upsilon_{*})}{\sigma}(Z),

(S253)

where we can again Taylor expand in
\upsilon_{*}-\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})
to get the bound
\mathbb{V}_{W}[\delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq C/n_{0}
on
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
. Using the law of total variance, we can then bound
\mathbb{V}[\delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq C/n_{0}
.

Finally applying Chebyshev’s inequality implies for all
c>0
that

\mathbb{P}[\lvert\delta-\mathbb{E}[\delta|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\rvert>Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c},

(S254)

and so

\mathbb{P}[\lvert\delta\rvert>Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}.

(S255)

Controlling
c_{0}
,
c_{1}
, and
c_{2}
:

Here, we introduce conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{F}
. The results for
c_{0}
,
c_{1}
, and
c_{2}
can be found using a similar argument to terms in
Mei and Montanari 2021
. The identifications
c_{0}\leftrightarrow K_{11}
,
c_{1}\leftrightarrow K_{12}
,
c_{2}\leftrightarrow 1-K_{22}
hold.

For
c_{0}=\frac{1}{m}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}
we have that

c_{0}\leq\frac{a^{2}\lVert 1_{m}\rVert^{2}}{m}\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\rVert_{\infty}\leq\frac{C}{\gamma}

(S256)

and

c_{0}\geq\frac{a^{2}\lVert 1_{m}\rVert^{2}}{m}\lambda_{\min}(\bar{K}^{-1})\geq\frac{C}{(\gamma+\lVert\bar{F}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}/n_{0})}\geq cn_{0}^{-c}

(S257)

using the operator norm bound on the event
\mathcal{E}_{F}
.

For
c_{1}=1+\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}
we have

\lvert c_{1}\rvert=\left\lvert 1+a1_{m}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\frac{1_{n_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rvert\leq 1+a\lVert 1_{m}\rVert_{2}\frac{\lVert 1_{n_{1}}\rVert_{2}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\rVert\leq Cn_{0}^{1/2},

(S258)

where the bound
\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\rVert\leq C
follows by considering the SVD of
\bar{F}
.

For
c_{2}=1-\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}
we obviously have that
c_{2}\leq 1
since
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\geq 0
. Additionally, we have that

\displaystyle c_{2}

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{n_{1}}1_{n_{1}}^{\top}\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}1_{n_{1}}

(S259)

\displaystyle=\frac{1_{n_{1}}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\left({I_{n_{1}}-\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}}\right)\frac{1_{n_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}

(S260)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\rVert_{\infty}

(S261)

\displaystyle=1-\frac{\lVert\bar{F}/\sqrt{n_{1}}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}{\gamma+\lVert\bar{F}/\sqrt{n_{1}}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}}\geq 1-\frac{cn_{0}^{c}}{\gamma+cn_{0}^{c}}

(S262)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C\gamma}{cn_{0}^{c}}

(S263)

\displaystyle\geq cn_{0}^{-c}

(S264)

as desired once again using the conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{F}
.

Finally,

\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}1_{n_{1}}^{\top}\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}1_{n_{1}}\leq\frac{\lVert 1_{n_{1}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{n_{1}}\left\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq C

(S265)

since
\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\leq C
follows once again by considering the SVD of
\bar{F}
.

Controlling
Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}
:

Here, we introduce conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
and note its independence from
\beta
and
\varepsilon
. Recalling that
Y=\beta^{\top}X/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon
, we note that
Y
has expectation zero since
\mathbb{E}_{\beta,\varepsilon}[Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]=0
. Similarly the conditional variance can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\beta,\varepsilon}[Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}(\Upsilon+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{m})\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}

(S266)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Upsilon+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{m}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\left\lVert\frac{1_{n_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

(S267)

\displaystyle\leq C.

(S268)

The bound
\|\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\|_{\infty}\leq C
follows by considering the SVD of
F
as in the proof of
Lemma

I.7
, while the bound
\|\Upsilon\|\leq C
holds on
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
. The law of total variance then shows
{\mathbb{V}[Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq C}
also. Hence an application of Chebyshev’s inequality shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S269)

for some
C>0
and any
c>0
.

Controlling
Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}
:

Again, we introduce conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}
and note its independence from
\beta
and
\varepsilon
. A nearly identical argument to the previous one suffices, so we only outline the argument. Again
\mathbb{E}[Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]=0
and the conditional variance can be bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\beta,\varepsilon}[Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{\text{data}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}(\Upsilon+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{m})\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}

(S270)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\rVert_{\infty}^{2}\|\Upsilon+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{m}\|_{\infty}\|\mathbf{u}\|_{2}^{2}

(S271)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\cdot a^{2}m\cdot\|\Upsilon+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{m}\|_{\infty}

(S272)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}.

(S273)

Finally, Chebyshev’s inequality suffices to show

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}|\mathcal{E}_{data}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S274)

for some
C>0
and any
c>0
.

Controlling
\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}
:

Here, we introduce conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{W}
and note its independence from
\mathbf{x}
. The overall argument then uses Chebyshev’s inequality exploiting the randomness in
\mathbf{x}
. First,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{f}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}/\sqrt{n_{1}}\right\rvert

(S275)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{f}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\mathbf{u}/\sqrt{n_{1}}\right\rVert_{2}

(S276)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{c}.

(S277)

The bound
\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C
follows by an SVD as before, while the first bound
{\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{f}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\rVert_{2}\leq Cn_{0}^{c}}
follows from the fact
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x})_{i}\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}
on
\mathcal{E}_{W}
(see
Lemma

I.5
). Next we turn to the conditional variance. We see

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}

(S278)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{F}^{\top}\big(\frac{\rho_{*}}{n_{0}}W\Sigma^{*}W^{\top}+(\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*})I_{n_{1}}+\Delta\big)\bar{F}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}

(S279)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\frac{\mathbf{u}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\left\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\cdot\left({\left\lVert\rho_{*}\tilde{\Upsilon}\right\rVert_{\infty}\!+\left\lVert(\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*})I_{n_{1}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\!+\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}}\right)

(S280)

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot(C+C+n_{0}^{c})

(S281)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{c}.

(S282)

Once again the bound
\lVert\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\rVert_{2}^{2}
follows by considering the SVD, while the nontrivial operator norm bounds follow from
Lemma

I.5
. Using the law of total variance, we see

\mathbb{V}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]|\mathcal{E}_{W}]+\mathbb{V}[\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\leq Cn_{0}^{c}

(S283)

by Equations (
S277
) and (
S282
). Applying Chebyshev’s inequality yields

\mathbb{P}[\lvert\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}]\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c},

(S284)

and so

\mathbb{P}[\lvert\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S285)

by the triangle inequality since
\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}]\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c}
.

Controlling
\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}
:

Again, we introduce conditioning on the event
\mathcal{E}_{W}
and note its independence from
\mathbf{x}
. A nearly identical argument to the previous one shows the result for this term. Hence we only outline the argument. First, we see

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{f}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\frac{1_{n_{1}}}{n_{1}}\right\rvert

(S286)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{f}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\right\rVert_{2}\left\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\left\lVert\frac{1_{n_{1}}}{n_{1}}\right\rVert_{2}

(S287)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}.

(S288)

The bound
\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1
follows by an SVD as before, while the first bound again follows from
Lemma

I.5
. Next we compute the conditional variance and bound as in Equation (
S282
) to find

\displaystyle\mathbb{V}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}[\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}

(S289)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{4}}1_{n_{1}}^{\top}\bar{F}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{F}^{\top}\left({\frac{\rho_{*}}{n_{0}}W\Sigma^{*}W^{\top}+(\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*})I_{n_{1}}+\Delta}\right)\bar{F}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{F}^{\top}1_{n_{1}}

(S290)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{1}}\left\lVert\frac{1_{n_{1}}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\!\cdot\left\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\!\cdot\left({\left\lVert{\rho_{*}}\tilde{\Upsilon}\right\rVert_{\infty}\!+\!\left\lVert(\eta_{*}\!-\!\zeta_{*})I_{n_{1}}\right\rVert_{\infty}\!+\!\left\lVert\Delta\right\rVert_{\infty}}\right)

(S291)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\cdot(C+C+n_{0}^{c})}{n_{1}}

(S292)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1}.

(S293)

Once again the bound
\lVert\frac{\bar{F}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\bar{K}^{-1}\frac{\bar{F}^{\top}}{\sqrt{n_{1}}}\rVert_{2}\leq 1
follows by considering the SVD while the nontrivial operator norm bounds follow on
\mathcal{E}_{W}
. Now applying Chebyshev’s inequality as before shows

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}|\mathcal{E}_{W}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}.

(S294)

Controlling
y(\mathbf{x})
:

Finally we show
y(\mathbf{x})\leq Cn_{0}^{c}
with probability at least
Cn_{0}^{-c}
. Note that
\mathbb{E}[y(\mathbf{x})]=0
and a short computation shows that
\mathbb{V}[y(\mathbf{x})]=\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}\leq C
. So a direct application of Chebyshev’s inequality shows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\lvert y(\mathbf{x})\rvert\geq Cn_{0}^{c}]\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}.

(S295)

∎

I.1.5
Completing the argument for the typical behavior

Lemma I.0
.

For some
n_{0}
-independent constants
c>0
and
C>0
, we have

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S296)

and

\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\Delta(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}+\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})}\right]\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{-c}

(S297)

Proof.

Obviously,
\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]\right\rvert
is bounded by
\left\lvert\mathbb{E}\left[{\Delta y(\mathbf{x})}\right]|\mathcal{E}\right\rvert
, so we can remove the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{E}}
from the expectations in Equations (
S296
) and (
S297
) by conditioning on
\mathcal{E}
.

We want to show that conditional on
\mathcal{E}
and for some
n_{0}
-independent constants
c>0
and
C>0
, the bounds
\left\lvert\Delta\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{-2c}
,
\left\lvert y(\mathbf{x})\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c}
, and
\left\lvert(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}+\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}Y^{\top})\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c}
hold.

Recall,
{a\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}_{Z}\sigma\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma)}Z}\right)}
,
{a_{*}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}_{Z}\sigma\left({\sqrt{\bar{\mathrm{tr}}(\Sigma^{*})}Z}\right)}
,
{\mathbf{v}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=1/n_{1}\bar{F}^{\top}1_{n_{1}}}
,
{\mathbf{u}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=a1_{m}}
,
{U\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=[\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}]^{\top}}
, and
{C\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
.

First, consider the term
\Delta=YT_{1}+YT_{2}
.
To show that
\left\lvert\Delta\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{-2c}
on
\mathcal{E}
, we write out
YT_{1}
and
YT_{2}
more explicitly:

\displaystyle YT_{1}

\displaystyle=Y\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}P

(S298)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\\
Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}\delta+c_{2}a_{*}\\
-mc_{0}\delta+c_{1}a_{*}\end{pmatrix}

(S299)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\left(Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\cdot(c_{1}\delta+c_{2}a_{*})+Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\cdot(-mc_{0}\delta+c_{1}a_{*})\right)

(S300)

and

\displaystyle YT_{2}

\displaystyle=-Y\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\left(I_{2}+CU\bar{K}^{-1}U^{\top}\right)^{-1}CU\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}

(S302)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\begin{pmatrix}Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\\
Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\begin{pmatrix}c_{2}&c_{1}\\
c_{1}&-mc_{0}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\\
\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\end{pmatrix}

(S303)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\Big(Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\cdot(c_{2}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}+c_{1}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}})

(S304)

\displaystyle\qquad+Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\cdot(c_{1}\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}-mc_{0}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}})\Big).

(S305)

Now using Equation (
S298
) and the definition of
\mathcal{E}
, we see that on
\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle\left\lvert YT_{1}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\right\rvert\left({\left\lvert Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\right\rvert(\left\lvert c_{1}\right\rvert\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert+\left\lvert c_{2}\right\rvert\left\lvert a_{*}\right\rvert)+\left\lvert Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\right\rvert(m\left\lvert c_{0}\right\rvert\left\lvert\delta\right\rvert+\left\lvert c_{1}\right\rvert\left\lvert a_{*}\right\rvert)}\right)

(S306)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{2c-1}\left({Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}(Cn_{0}^{c}Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}+Cn_{0}^{c})+Cn_{0}^{c}(Cn_{0}Cn_{0}^{c}Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}+Cn_{0}^{c})}\right)

(S307)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{5c-1/2}.

(S308)

Since the
c
s used in the bounds above can be arbitrarily small, we may replace write
\left\lvert YT_{1}\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}
for arbitrarily small
c>0
. Finally, a similar argument for Equation (
S302
) yields the bound

\displaystyle\left\lvert YT_{2}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\frac{1}{c_{1}^{2}+mc_{0}c_{2}}\right\rvert\Big(\lvert Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{u}\rvert\cdot(\lvert c_{2}\rvert\lvert\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\rvert+\lvert c_{1}\rvert\lvert\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\rvert)

(S309)

\displaystyle\qquad+\lvert Y\bar{K}^{-1}\mathbf{v}\rvert\cdot(\lvert c_{1}\rvert\lvert\mathbf{u}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\rvert-m\lvert c_{0}\rvert\lvert\mathbf{v}^{\top}\bar{K}^{-1}\bar{K}_{\mathbf{x}}\rvert)\Big)

(S310)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{2c-1}\cdot\Big(Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}\cdot(Cn_{0}^{c}Cn_{0}^{c}+Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}Cn_{0}^{c-1/2})

(S311)

\displaystyle\qquad+Cn_{0}^{c}\cdot(Cn_{0}^{c+1/2}Cn_{0}^{c}+Cn_{0}Cn_{0}^{c}Cn_{0}^{c-1/2})\Big)

(S312)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{2c-1}\cdot(Cn_{0}^{3c+1/2}+Cn_{0}^{3c+1/2})

(S313)

\displaystyle\leq Cn_{0}^{5c-1/2}.

(S314)

and as before since
c
can be chosen arbitrarily small we can write
\left\lvert YT_{2}\right\rvert\leq Cn_{0}^{c-1/2}
.
∎

I.2
Gaussian equivalents

Before describing how (in the high-dimensional limit) Gaussian equivalents can be used to compute the test error we make the following observation: the limiting test error is invariant to the centering of the activation function across the training and test distributions. That is, by
Section

I.1
the limiting test error is unchanged by replacing,

\bar{F}_{ij}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=F_{ij}-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}\sigma\left({z}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\bar{f}_{i}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=f_{i}-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}\sigma\left({z}\right)

(S315)

for all
i
and
j
.

With this simplification in mind, the proof of
Theorem

5.3
relies on the concept of Gaussian equivalents and the linearization analysis developed in
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
;
Adlam and Pennington 2020b
;
Adlam et al. 2019
, which we briefly review here, though we refer the reader to these works for a more detailed description. To proceed, we first define the moment-matched Gaussian linearizations,

\displaystyle\bar{F}\to

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\rho}}{n_{0}}}WX+\sqrt{\bar{\eta}-\bar{\zeta}}\Theta

(S316)

\displaystyle\bar{f}\to

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\bar{\rho}_{*}}{n_{0}}}W\mathbf{x}+\sqrt{\bar{\eta}_{*}-\bar{\zeta}_{*}}\theta

(S317)

where
\bar{f}\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\sigma}(W\mathbf{x}/\sqrt{n_{0}})
is the random feature representation of the test point
\mathbf{x}
and
y\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{0}}}\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}
is its corresponding label, which we assume has no additive noise (including noise on the test labels merely shifts by the total error by an irreducible additive constant). The centered parameters
14
14

14

With some abuse of notation, we use
\bar{\sigma}(\cdot)
to denote the centering with respect to the training scale
s
and
s_{*}
for unasterisked and asterisked quantities respectively.
are defined as,
\bar{\zeta}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{*}
as
\bar{\zeta}=s\bar{\rho}
and
\bar{\zeta}_{*}=s_{*}\bar{\rho}_{*}
with,

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[\bar{\sigma}(z)^{2}]\,,\;\;

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[z\bar{\sigma}(z)])^{2}\,,

(S318)

\displaystyle\bar{\eta}_{*}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[\bar{\sigma}(z)^{2}]\,,\;\;

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{*}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=(\frac{1}{s_{*}}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[z\bar{\sigma}(z)])^{2}\,.

(S319)

The new objects
\Theta
,
\theta
are matrices of the appropriate shapes with i.i.d. standard Gaussian entries independent of the other random variables under consideration. The constants
\bar{\eta}
,
\bar{\zeta}
,
\bar{\eta}_{*}
,
\bar{\zeta}_{*}
are chosen so that the mixed moments up to second order are the same for the original and linearized matrices. The Gaussian equivalents defined are essentially constructed via a Taylor expansion of the nonlinearity
\bar{\sigma}(\cdot)
. The explicit calculations defining these expressions can be found via the Gaussian moment matching technique in
Adlam et al. 2019
.

Having appropriately linearized the random feature matrices, we can map back to the definition of the original activation functions
by recalling that the variables
\zeta
and
\zeta_{*}
are defined in
Section

5.1
as
\zeta=s\rho
and
\zeta_{*}=s_{*}\rho_{*}
, and

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\eta}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[(\sigma(z)-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[\sigma(z)])^{2}]=\mathbb{V}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[\sigma(z)]

(S320)

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\rho}=(\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[z(\sigma(z)-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[\sigma(z)])])^{2}=(\frac{1}{s}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[z(\sigma(z)])^{2}

(S321)

\displaystyle\eta_{*}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\eta_{*}}=\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[(\sigma(z)-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[\sigma(z)])^{2}]=\mathbb{V}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[\sigma(z)]

(S322)

\displaystyle\rho_{*}

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{:}}=\bar{\rho_{*}}=(\frac{1}{s_{*}}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[z(\sigma(z)-\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s)}[\sigma(z)])])^{2}=(\frac{1}{s_{*}}\mathbb{E}_{z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,s_{*})}[z(\sigma(z)])^{2}\,.

(S323)

by using the definition of the centering. Effectively, this is equivalent to using the linearizations,

\displaystyle F\to\;\bar{F}

\displaystyle\to\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{n_{0}}}WX+\sqrt{\eta-\zeta}\Theta

(S324)

\displaystyle f\to\;\bar{f}

\displaystyle\to\sqrt{\frac{\rho_{*}}{n_{0}}}W\mathbf{x}+\sqrt{\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*}}\theta

(S325)

directly to compute the test error.

Simply by definition, the teacher function is exactly linear,

\displaystyle Y=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{0}}}\beta^{\top}X+\varepsilon

(S326)

\displaystyle y=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{1}{n_{0}}}\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}\,.

(S327)

We emphasize that the restriction to linear teacher functions is simply a matter of convenience and simplicity—nonlinear teacher neural networks can be studied by means of an analogous linearization process, as discussed in
Adlam and Pennington 2020a
, which merely requires introducing additional moment-matching constants and additional i.i.d. standard Gaussian terms
\theta
, and
\theta_{y}
.

In the high-dimensional limit the bulk statistics we compute defining the error, bias, and variance are invariant to the above replacements by linearized Gaussian equivalents. We remark that further intuition for these linearized information-plus-noise replacements can be gathered from the universality results of
Banna et al. 2015
;
Banna et al. 2020
. To summarize briefly, the final expressions we compute are tracial (nonlinear) functions of several random matrices. A large body of universality results stemming from
Banna et al. 2020
;
Erdos 2019
show that in the limit it is sufficient to match them to an equivalent tracial functional of a (linearized) rational function of random matrices, whose moments match their nonlinear counterparts.

More specifically, two basic trace objects arise in the calculations, which take the form

\mathrm{tr}(AB)\,,\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{tr}\left({A\frac{1}{B-zI}}\right)\,.

(S328)

For the first case,
\mathrm{tr}(AB)
, if the random matrices
A
and
B
are independent, its asymptotics can be understood via concentration of measure arguments. Conditionally on
A
, it is sufficient to replace
B
with an equivalent matrix designed to match low-order moments, at the expense of an error that vanishes asymptotically. The second case,
\mathrm{tr}\left({A\frac{1}{B-zI}}\right)
, is a bit more involved. Our arguments proceed by 1) using the linearized matrices in
Eqs.

S324
and
S325
to express the trace object as a rational function of i.i.d. Gaussian matrices, and then 2) using the linear pencil method
15
15

15

This is essentially an iterative application of the Schur complement formula.
to express it as the trace of a large (inverted) block matrix
(
Helton et al. 2018
;
Mingo and Speicher 2017
)
. The reason the linearization over
B
preserves the asymptotic statistics even for general
A
with correlated entries stems from the matrix Dyson equation
(
Erdos 2019
)
.

I.3
Decomposition of the test loss

Recall the expression for the test loss in
Eq.

S143
,

E_{\Sigma^{*}}=E_{1}+E_{2}+E_{3}

(S329)

with

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}y(\mathbf{x})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\varepsilon)}\mathrm{tr}(y(\mathbf{x})y(\mathbf{x})^{\top})

(S330)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=-2\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y)y(\mathbf{x})=-2\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\varepsilon)}\mathrm{tr}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}y(\mathbf{x}))

(S331)

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\beta,\varepsilon)}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top})^{2}=\mathbb{E}_{(\mathbf{x},\varepsilon)}\mathrm{tr}(K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}K^{-1}Y^{\top}YK^{-1}K_{\mathbf{x}})\,,

(S332)

where the kernels
K=K(X,X)
and
K_{\mathbf{x}}=K(X,\mathbf{x})
are given by,

K=\frac{F^{\top}\!F}{n_{1}}+\gamma I_{m}\qquad\text{and}\qquad K_{\mathbf{x}}=\frac{1}{n_{1}}F^{\top}f\,.

(S333)

Using the cyclicity and linearity of the trace, the expectation over
\mathbf{x}
requires the computation of

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}K_{\mathbf{x}}K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\,,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}y(\mathbf{x})K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}\,,\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}y(\mathbf{x})y(\mathbf{x})^{\top}\,.

(S334)

As described in
Section

I.2
, without loss of generality we consider the case of a linear teacher, and
Eqs.

S327
and
S325
read

y=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}\beta^{\top}\mathbf{x}\,\qquad\text{and}\qquad f\to f^{\text{lin}}=\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{*}}}{\sqrt{n_{0}}}W\mathbf{x}+\sqrt{\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*}}\theta\,.

(S335)

The expectations over
\mathbf{x}
are now trivial and we readily find,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}K_{\mathbf{x}}K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}F^{\top}\left({\frac{\rho_{*}}{n_{0}}W\Sigma^{*}W^{\top}+(\eta_{*}-\zeta_{*})I_{n_{1}}}\right)F

(S336)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}y(\mathbf{x})K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{*}}}{n_{0}n_{1}}\beta^{\top}\Sigma^{*}W^{\top}F

(S337)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}y(\mathbf{x})y(\mathbf{x})^{\top}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{0}}\beta\Sigma^{*}\beta^{\top}

(S338)

One may interpret the substitution in
Eq.

S335
as a tool to calculate the expectations above to leading order, which generates terms like
Eq.

S328
. Next, we recall the definition,
Y=\beta^{\top}X/\sqrt{n_{0}}+\varepsilon
, and, as above, we consider the leading order behavior with respect to the random variables
\beta
to find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\beta,\varepsilon}\left[{Y^{\top}Y}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n_{0}}X^{\top}X+\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{2}I_{m}

(S339)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\beta,\varepsilon}\left[{Y^{\top}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}}y(\mathbf{x})K_{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\rho_{*}}}{n_{0}^{3/2}n_{1}}X^{\top}\Sigma^{*}W^{\top}F\,.

(S340)

Putting these pieces together, we have

\displaystyle E_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma^{*})}{n_{0}}

(S341)

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=E_{21}

(S342)

\displaystyle E_{3}

\displaystyle=E_{31}+E_{32}\,,

(S343)

where,

\displaystyle E_{21}
